Mean curvature flow with generic initial data II
Otis Chodosh, Kyeongsu Choi, Felix Schulze

Abstract
. We show that the mean curvature flow of a generic closed surface in ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} avoids multiplicity one tangent flows that are not round spheres/cylinders. In particular, we show that any non-cylindrical self-shrinker with a cylindrical end cannot arise generically.



[title=Index of Notation]

1.
Introduction

Mean curvature flow is the natural heat equation for submanifolds. A family of surfaces
M(t)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
flows by mean curvature flow if

(1.1)

\left(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathbf{x}\right)^{\perp}=\mathbf{H}_{M(t)}(\mathbf{x}),

where
\mathbf{H}_{M(t)}(\mathbf{x})
denotes the mean curvature vector of
M(t)
at
\mathbf{x}
. When the initial surface
M(0)
is compact, mean curvature flow is guaranteed to become singular in finite time. The simplest way to analyze such a singularity is to parabolically dilate around a singular point in space-time. Huisken’s monotonicity formula
[
Hui90
]
(cf.
[
Ilm95
]
) guarantees that a subsequential limit of such dilations will weakly limit to a
tangent flow
which will be a weak solution to (
1.1
), evolving only by homothety. Ilmanen has showed that such a tangent flow will be supported on a smooth surface
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
so that
(-\infty,0)\mapsto\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
is a solution to (
1.1
). Such a surface is called a
self-shrinker
(see Section
3.1
).

A deeper understanding of such tangent flows is necessary in order to continue the flow past the onset of singularities (either by constructing a flow with surgery, or by showing that weak solutions to the flow have good partial regularity and well-posedness properties). The two main obstructions to doing so are the potential presence of
multiplicity
and of tangent flows supported on self-shrinkers whose scalar mean curvature
changes sign
. By a result of Huisken
[
Hui90
]
(cf.
[
CM12a
]
) a shrinker whose mean curvature does not change sign is either a plane or a round sphere or cylinder. Such singularities (when they occur with multiplicity one) are known to be well-behaved, thanks to regularity results of Colding–Minicozzi
[
CM16
]
(cf.
[
Whi97
,
CIM15
,
CM15
]
) and the resolution of the mean convex neighborhood conjecture by Choi–Haslhofer–Hershkovits
[
CHH22
]
.

In this paper, we show that the second obstruction does not occur for
generic
initial data
M(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Our main result is roughly as follows (see Section
1.5
for a more detailed overview and Theorem
9.2
for the precise statement):

The mean curvature flow of a generic initial surface
M(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
encounters only spherical and cylindrical singularities at least until multiplicity occurs for some tangent flow. In particular, up to the first time multiplicity occurs, the flow is well-posed and is completely smooth for almost every time.

This confirms—up to the potential occurrence of multiplicity—a long-standing conjecture of Huisken
[
Ilm03
, # 8]
.

The well-known
multiplicity-one conjecture
posits that multiplicity never occurs, but this is widely open even for generic initial data (we note that considerable progress has been made under additional geometric assumptions on
M(0)
or the flow; see
[
Whi00
,
SW09
,
And12
,
Lin20
,
Bre15
,
LW19
,
LW22
,
BN21
]
). We review previous work related to generic mean curvature flow in Section
1.3
below.

There is strong evidence that the
generic
hypothesis in the above result is necessary. Indeed, Ilmanen–White have indicated
[
Whi02
]
a construction of a closed surface
M(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
whose mean curvature flow is not well-posed after the onset of singularities. (On the other hand, if
M(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
has genus zero, an important work of Brendle
[
Bre16
]
shows that the statement above holds without “generic.”)

Remark
.

By combining our main result with a surgery construction by Daniels-Holgate
[
DH22
]
, one can construct a mean curvature flow with surgery for a generic initial
M(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
until the first time multiplicity occurs for the weak flow above.

1.1.
The no-cylinder conjecture

At the first time singularities appear (or more generally, the first time that some singularity other than multiplicity-one spherical/cylindrical singularities occur) we have seen that any tangent flow is supported on a smooth self-shrinker
\Sigma
(possibly with multiplicity). A fundamental result of Wang
[
Wan16a
]
shows that if
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a non-compact self-shrinker then any end of
\Sigma
is smoothly asymptotic—with multiplicity one—to a smooth cone or a cylinder.

Ilmanen has asked if it is possible that a non-cylindrical shrinker has a cylindrical end (the
no-cylinder conjecture

[
Ilm03
, # 12]
). See
[
Wan16b
]
for some partial progress towards non-existence. Moreover, we note that are by now many constructions of self-shrinkers (both numerical and rigorous)
[
Ang92
,
Cho94
,
KKM18
,
Ngu14
,
Ket16
,
SWZ20
,
BNS21
]
but no example with a cylindrical end has been found.

The main new ingredient this paper can be stated as follows:

Even if a non-cylindrical shrinker with a cylindrical end exists, it does not arise generically (with multiplicity one at the first singular time).

This builds on our previous work with Mantoulidis
[
CCMS20
]
where we proved that asymptotically conical and compact shrinkers do not arise generically, but left open the possibility of a shrinker with a cylindrical end. As we discuss below, the analysis in the cylindrical end case is significantly complicated by the
a priori
lack of graphicality of the corresponding ancient flow along the cylindrical end.

1.2.
Generic initial data and one-sided ancient flows

In our previous paper with Mantoulidis
[
CCMS20
]
, we developed a new approach to the study of the mean curvature flow of generic initial data by relating this to the classification of certain ancient solutions. (One may view this as the
parabolic
analogue of the Hardt–Simon foliation and corresponding generic regularity results for area minimizing hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{8}

[
HS85
,
Sma93
,
Wan22
]
, cf.
[
CMS23
]
.)

1.2.1.
One-sided flows as local models for generic mean curvature flow

The basic strategy is as follows (see also
[
CCMS20
, §1.5]
for a more detailed exposition of the method). Embed the initial surface
M_{0}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
in a local foliation
M_{s}(0)
and flow all of the surface simultaneously by mean curvature flow
M_{s}(t)
. Suppose that
M_{0}(t)
encounters a singularity at time
T
at point
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. (Let us assume that this is the first singular time, but in reality we can work with the first time with a singularity other a multiplicity one sphere/cylinder.)

As explained above, the parabolically dilated flows

\lambda(M_{0}(T+\lambda^{-2}t)-\mathbf{x}):=M_{0}^{\lambda}(t)

weakly limit subsequentially (as
\lambda\to\infty
) to the shrinking flow
t\mapsto\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
,
t<0
, associated to a smooth self-shrinker
\Sigma
. (Of course, this limit could also occur with some integer multiplicity
k\geq 2
; we will assume that
k=1
below.)

The key observation in
[
CCMS20
]
is that if we
simultaneously
dilate the entire foliation

\lambda(M_{s}(T+\lambda^{-2}t)-\mathbf{x}):=M_{s}^{\lambda}(t)

then by choosing a subsequence of
s\to 0
appropriately with
\lambda\to\infty
we can pass
M_{s}^{\lambda}(t)
to the limit to find
\check{M}(t)
a (nontrivial) ancient mean curvature flow that’s disjoint from
t\mapsto\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
(we call such a flow
one-sided
). The main strategy is then to show that
\check{M}(t)
has
improved
behavior as compared to
M_{0}(t)
and
t\mapsto\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
. This is achieved by showing (see Section
1.4
for a more detailed discussion) that up to a parabolic dilation, there exists a
unique
ancient one-sided flow
\overline{M}(t)
so that
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\overline{M}(t)
is close (in a
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}
sense, with multiplicity one) to
\Sigma
as
t\to-\infty
(existence is proven in Proposition
5.6
and uniqueness is proven in Theorem
7.22
) and moreover
\overline{M}(t)
has the following improved properties (see Propositions
5.8
and
8.5
)

(1)

\overline{M}(t)
is a shrinker mean-convex flow; geometrically this means that the space-time track is parabolically star-shaped
1
1

1

Compare with the “elliptic” star-shapedness of the Hardt–Simon foliation.
around
(\mathbf{0},0)
,

(2)

as a consequence of the shrinker mean-convexity, all singularities of
\overline{M}(t)
are spherical/cylindrical with multiplicity-one,

(3)

for
t\ll 0
,
\frac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\overline{M}(t)
is a small
C^{\infty}
graph over
\Sigma
, and

(4)

for
t<0
sufficiently close to
0
,
\overline{M}(t)
has genus zero.

Properties (1) and (2) can be used to show that for
s\sim 0
appropriately chosen, the flow
M_{s}(t)
will only have spherical/cylindrical singularities near
(\mathbf{x},T)
. Thus, if we assumed that the tangent flow to
M_{0}(t)
at
(\mathbf{x},T)
was supported on a more complicated shrinker, the flow
M_{s}(t)
has improved
locally
. Of course, this does not rule out the possibility of
M_{s}(t)
having other such singularities elsewhere.

To ensure that this improvement is actually reflected
globally
it is crucial that we only have to iterate this perturbation process finitely many times (if we perturb infinitely many times, smooth regions that we already controlled might become singular in the limit). This is guaranteed by combining (3) and (4) with the classification of genus zero self-shrinkers due to Brendle
[
Bre16
]
. Indeed, at any non-spherical/cylindrical singularity,
[
Bre16
]
and (3) implies that
\overline{M}(t)
has non-trivial genus for
t\ll 0
. In particular, some handle of
M_{s}(t)
“concentrates” near
(\mathbf{x},T)
. However, handle pinches off before time
T
, since by (4) it does so for the local model
\overline{M}(t)
. Along with a result of White
[
Whi95
]
stating that genus is monotone non-increasing along mean curvature flow, this implies that we need only perturb finitely many times.

1.2.2.
Existence/uniqueness of ancient flows

Fix a self-shrinker
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In the above sketch, we crucially used the existence of a one-sided ancient flow with properties (1)-(4) as well as the uniqueness of this flow. The basic mechanism involves the spectral properties of the
L
-operator

(1.2)

Lu=\Delta_{\Sigma}u-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\nabla_{\Sigma}u+|A_{\Sigma}|^{2}u+\frac{1}{2}u

obtained by linearizing
\mathbf{H}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{\perp}
at
\Sigma
(with respect to normal variations). In their foundational work
[
CM12a
]
, Colding–Minicozzi introduced the
L
-operator and proved (among many other things) that the first eigenvalue
\mu
of
L
(with respect to the Gaussian weighted space) is negative.

We can thus give an indication at the linear level why the existence and uniqueness result should hold. After an appropriate rescaling, an ancient solution to (rescaled
2
2

2

Given
N(t)
a solution to mean curvature flow, the rescaled solution around
(\mathbf{x},T)
is the one-parameter family of surfaces
\tau\mapsto e^{\tau/2}(N(T-e^{-\tau})-\mathbf{x})
. It is easily checked that this procedure (around
(\mathbf{0},0)
) converts the shrinking self-shrinker
t\mapsto\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
into a static
\tau\mapsto\Sigma
. In general, the rescaled family will solve
\left(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathbf{x}\right)^{\perp}=\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{x})+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{\perp}
(compare with (
1.1
)).
) mean curvature flow that’s close to
\Sigma
can be modeled on an ancient solution to the parabolic equation

(1.3)

\partial_{\tau}u=Lu

with
u\to 0
as
\tau\to-\infty
. One-sidedness is reflected at the linear level by
u>0
and shrinker mean convexity is reflected at the linear level by
\partial_{\tau}u\geq 0
(shrinker mean convexity is equivalent to the rescaled mean curvature flow moving in one direction).

For “existence,” we have the solution
e^{-\mu\tau}\varphi
, for
\varphi
the first eigenfunction. Since the first eigenfunction does not change sign, we see that this is a “one-sided” solution and since
\mu<0
we see that this is a “shrinker mean convex” solution. Conversely, for “uniqueness” we note that by a straightforward linear analysis, any ancient solution to (
1.3
) will be of the form

(1.4)

u(\mathbf{x},t)=ce^{-\mu\tau}\varphi+\sum_{\mu<\lambda<0}e^{-\lambda\tau}\varphi_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

where the sum is taken over eigenvalues of
L
and
\varphi_{\lambda}
is a some corresponding eigenfunction (each of which must change sign, by the usual characterization of the first eigenfunction). Since
e^{-\lambda\tau}\gg e^{-\mu\tau}
for
\tau\to-\infty
, we see that if
u
is “one-sided” (i.e.,
u>0
) then it holds that
u(\mathbf{x},t)=ce^{-\mu\tau}\varphi
, giving uniqueness in the linear setting.

Of course, in the actual problem one must account for the nonlinearity of the equation. To prove existence we rely on a new upper/lower barrier construction for the ancient flow (cf. Lemma
5.1
). (This allows for a somewhat more direct proof that the one-sided flow is shrinker mean convex, cf.
[
CCMS20
, §7]
.)

For uniqueness, if we were to assume
a priori
that the unknown one-sided rescaled flow is an
entire
graph (with good decay properties) over
\Sigma
for very negative times, then a Merle–Zaag type analysis readily yields uniqueness, cf. Proposition
5.10
. However, establishing this graphicality represents a major issue in this work. Indeed, in contrast to the conical ends (where graphicality follows automatically from pseudolocality, see
[
CCMS20
, Lemma 7.18]
), an ancient one-sided rescaled flow is not
a priori
an entire graph over the cylindrical ends of
\Sigma
for very negative times. Establishing this graphicality is the main technical ingredient needed to carry out the above linear analysis. We prove graphicality and the corresponding uniqueness statement in Section
7
, see Section
1.4
below for an outline of the proof.

1.3.
Related work

The first major advance towards a theory of generic mean curvature flow was taken by Colding–Minicozzi who introduced the notion of entropy (cf. Section
2.3
) and classified the sphere/cylinder as the unique
linearly stable
self-shrinkers in a precise sense
[
CM12a
]
. In particular, their work shows that if
M(0)=\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a non-spherical/cylindrical closed self-shrinker, then there is
M^{\prime}(0)
a small
C^{\infty}
-graph over
M(0)
so that the mean curvature flow
M^{\prime}(t)
does not have the shrinking flow associated to
\Sigma
at any tangent flow.

Remark
.

It follows from Colding–Minicozzi’s linear analysis that any shrinker becomes shrinker mean-convex when perturbed to one side. This crucial observation was first used by Colding–Ilmanen–Minicozzi–White
[
CIMW13
]
to classify the sphere as the closed self-shrinker of minimal entropy (see Section
2.3
). This was later generalized in various ways by Bernstein–Wang
[
BW16
,
BW17
,
BW18b
]
(cf.
[
Zhu16
,
HW19
]
). Of particular relevance to this article, we note that Bernstein–Wang’s proof
[
BW17
]
that the cylinder has the second-least entropy among non-flat shrinkers in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
proceeds by constructing a “pre-ancient” one-sided mean curvature flow (on one side of a hypothetical lower entropy asymptotically conical shrinker) and observing that it must lose genus at (non-rescaled) time
t=0
.

More recently, in a joint work with Mantoulidis
[
CCMS20
]
we introduced a new approach to the study of mean curvature flow with generic initial data. In particular, as explained above, we showed how the problem was related to the existence/uniqueness of ancient one-sided flows for a given shrinker
\Sigma
and studied the case where
\Sigma
is smoothly asymptotic to a cone. In this case, using pseudolocality (cf.
[
CS21
]
), such a flow is automatically an entire graph over
\Sigma
in the ancient past and the techniques from work of the second-named author and Mantoulidis
[
CM22
]
could be used to prove existence/uniqueness. In particular,
[
CCMS20
]
proved that asymptotically conical self-shrinkers do not arise as multiplicity one tangent flows at the first (non-generic) singular time for a generic initial surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. (The current paper generalizes the techniques from
[
CCMS20
]
to account for cylindrical ends.)

We have also developed a related, but different, strategy to study generic mean curvature flows in a separate joint work with Mantoulidis
[
CCMS21
]
. This approach avoids the need for a classification of ancient flows and instead relies on a softer
density drop
argument. This argument is most effective in a
low entropy
setting and in particular proved that in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
a surface
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
with entropy
\lambda(M)\leq 2
can be perturbed so as to only encounter multiplicity one spherical/cylindrical singularities. However, the argument does not seem sensitive to the
genus drop
proven in this paper (and in
[
CCMS20
]
) which limits its applicability—at present—to the low entropy case. Similarly, the work
[
CCMS21
]
applied in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
to yield a similar result for hypersurfaces
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
with entropy
\lambda(M)\leq\lambda(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
; combined with the surgery result of Daniels–Holgate
[
DH22
]
this proves that the Schoenflies conjecture holds under such a hypothesis (this improves on an influential work of Bernstein–Wang who established the Schoenflies conjecture under the assumption
\lambda(M)\leq\lambda(\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{R})
, cf.
[
BW21b
,
BW21a
,
BW18a
,
BW22c
,
BW22d
,
BW22b
,
BW22a
]
).

Remark
.

The existence and uniqueness result for ancient flows proven here in fact holds for self-shrinking hypersurfaces
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
(
n+1=3,\dots,7
) with smooth conical and cylindrical ends. In fact, we can even allow for
\Sigma
to have ends modeled on cylinders of the form
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
, for
\hat{\Sigma}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
an arbitrary closed shrinker. However, in the case of ends modeled on
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
, we have to impose an extra condition on
\Sigma
, namely we require that the first eigenfunction of the
L
operator is
o(1)
at spatial infinity (we call this
\Sigma
having
strictly stable ends
). (This holds automatically for
\hat{\Sigma}
a round sphere, by work of Colding–Minicozzi
[
CM12a
]
; cf. Proposition
3.5
and Lemma
3.6
). This generalization has some consequences for perturbing away such singularities in higher dimensions (in particular, yielding precise local information in contrast with the local perturbation result described in
[
CCMS21
, Appendix C]
).

Remark
.

Some time after our work with Mantoulidis
[
CCMS20
,
CCMS21
]
appeared, a more analytic approach to the mean curvature flow of generic initial data has been studied by Sun–Xue
[
SX21b
,
SX21a
,
SX22
]
(modeled on an earlier program introduced by Colding–Minicozzi
[
CM19
]
). They have proven that tangent flows modeled on (multiplicity one) compact or asymptotically conical tangent flows (other than spheres/cylinders) at the first singular time can be
locally
perturbed away by adjusting the initial data. It is yet unclear if their methods can be applied to shrinkers with cylindrical ends (as we do in this paper) or if their methods can recover the crucial
genus drop
property used here to prove
global
results.

1.4.
Outline of existence/uniqueness for the one-sided ancient flow

In this section we give a more detailed description of the existence and uniqueness of the one-sided ancient flow. Consider
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
(for
3
3

3

This is a technical assumption related to certain difficulties in White’s theory of mean convex flows in dimensions admitting non-flat stable minimal cones; we expect that it could be removed, but have not pursued this further. Of course, this restriction does not prevent applications in the most relevant dimensions
n=2,3
.

n\leq 6
) a smoothly embedded self-shrinker. We assume that any end of
\Sigma
is either smoothly asymptotic to a smooth cone (with multiplicity one) or smoothly asymptotic to a cylinder
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
for
\hat{\Sigma}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
a closed smooth self-shrinker. We call this the
nice ends
hypothesis (cf. Definition
3.1
).

It follows from standard linear theory for the Gaussian weighted
L^{2}
-space that the
L
operator (
1.2
) admits a positive first eigenfunction
\varphi
. We will assume that
\Sigma
has
strictly stable ends
(cf. Definition
3.4
) meaning that
\varphi(\mathbf{x})=o(1)
as
\mathbf{x}\to\infty
. By work of Colding–Minicozzi and the barrier argument given in Lemma
3.6
this automatically holds if all cylindrical ends of
\Sigma
are asymptotic to
round cylinders
but we have been unable to determine if it holds in general. (Note that for
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, work of Wang and Colding–Minicozzi guarantee that
\Sigma
has nice, stable ends, see Proposition
3.5
.)

Remark
.

The stable ends hypothesis does not seem to have been considered elsewhere. If there exists
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with nice but unstable ends, we conjecture that there exists an ancient one-sided flow coming out of
\Sigma
, but the flow is not graphical in the ancient past. Indeed, one may expect that the ancient flow along a cylindrical end
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
is modeled on the elliptic regularization translator solution that limits to the one-sided flow coming out of
\hat{\Sigma}
(in one dimension lower).
To prove existence and uniqueness in this setting one would need to analyze the “tips” of the cylindrical ends by generalizing the arguments from
[
ADS20
]
to apply in this setting. Since this is not needed for the main result in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we leave such investigations for future work.

We now state (somewhat less informally) our existence and uniqueness results for the ancient one-sided flow.

Theorem

(Existence of ancient one-sided flow, informal)
.

Consider
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
n\leq 6
, a self-shrinker with nice, strictly stable ends. Fix one of the two open sets
\Omega
with
\Sigma=\partial\Omega
and choose the inwards pointing unit normal for
\Sigma
. There exists a weak
4
4

4

precisely,
\bar{M}(t)
is an integral cyclic unit regular Brakke flow with corresponding weak set flow
\mathcal{K}
so that
\bar{M}(t)=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
rescaled mean curvature flow
(\bar{M}(\tau))_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}}
, with the following properties:

(1)

the flow is one-sided:
\bar{M}(\tau)\subset\Omega

(2)

the flow is shrinker mean convex: the speed of
\tau\mapsto\bar{M}(\tau)
is everywhere positive

(3)

the flow has good regularity properties:
\bar{M}(\tau)
has only spherical/cylindrical multiplicity one singularities

(4)

the flow is graphical in the ancient past: for
\tau\ll 0
, there is
v(\cdot,\tau)>0\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
so that
\bar{M}(\tau)
is the normal graph of
v(\cdot,\tau)
,

(5)

convergence in the ancient past: the function
v(\cdot,\tau)\to 0
in
C^{k}(\Sigma)
as
\tau\to-\infty
, and

(6)

the graph is first eigenfunction dominated: the function
v(\cdot,\tau)
satisfies
\tfrac{1}{2}e^{-\mu\tau}\varphi(\cdot)\leq v(\cdot,\tau)\leq 2e^{-\mu\tau}\varphi(\cdot)
, for
\mu
and
\varphi
the first eigenvalue and eigenfunction of the
L
-operator.

Precise references are as follows: for (1)-(3) see Propositions
5.7
and
5.8
, for (4)-(6) see Proposition
5.6
.

Theorem

(Uniqueness of ancient one-sided flow, informal)
.

For
\Sigma,\Omega,\bar{M}(\tau)
as in the existence result, consider
\check{M}(\tau)
an ancient weak
5
5

5

integral unit regular Brakke flow
rescaled mean curvature flow with the following properties:

(1)

one-sided:
\check{M}(\tau)\subset\Omega

(2)

smoothly limiting in the ancient past:
\check{M}(\tau)
limits to
\Sigma
in
C^{\infty}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Sigma)
with multiplicity one as
\tau\to-\infty
.

Then there is
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
so that
\check{M}(\tau)=\bar{M}(\tau+\tau_{0})
.

The precise reference is Theorem
7.22
.

1.4.1.
Outline of the proof of existence

For
\mu,\varphi
the first eigenvalue/eigenfunction for the
L
-operator, we prove in Lemma
5.1
that the graphs of

v_{\pm}(\mathbf{x},\tau):=(e^{-\mu\tau}\pm Me^{-2\mu\tau})\varphi(\mathbf{x}),

define a supersolution (for
v_{+}
) and subsolution (for
v_{-}
) for rescaled mean curvature flow, for
\tau\ll 0
and
M\gg 0
(see Lemma
5.1
).
6
6

6

This is a key innovation in the existence part of the present paper, allowing us to simplify several arguments from
[
CCMS20
]
even though the cylindrical ends seem like they should complicate certain steps.
The key feature is that to highest order
v_{\pm}\sim e^{-\mu\tau}\varphi
decays at exactly the expected rate. Using these solutions as barriers, we see that the rescaled mean curvature flow with initial condition the graph of
\varepsilon\varphi
starting at time
\tau=|\mu|^{-1}\log\varepsilon
—where
\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small—exists until some fixed time
\tau_{0}
independent of
\varepsilon>0
(this follows from the bootstrap arguments in Section
4
, see Proposition
5.2
).

Because the flows exist for a uniform amount of time as
\varepsilon\to 0
, we can pass to the
\varepsilon\to 0
limit to obtain a graphical ancient one-sided rescaled mean curvature flow
\bar{M}(\tau)
defined for
\tau\in(-\infty,\tau_{0}]
. In fact, using standard elliptic regularization methods we can continue
\bar{M}(\tau)
to all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
as a weak flow. This yields (1). Properties (4)-(6) follow from the barriers.

Shrinker mean convexity for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
follows from shrinker mean convexity in the ancient past and spatial infinity. This proves (2) and allows us (as in
[
CCMS20
]
) to apply White’s regularity theory to deduce (3).

1.4.2.
Outline of the proof of uniqueness

As mentioned above, establishing uniqueness of the ancient one-sided flow represents the central difficulty in this paper.

For a shrinker
\Sigma=\partial\Omega
with nice, stable ends consider an ancient weak rescaled mean curvature flow
\check{M}(\tau)
that converges to
\Sigma
in
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}
with multiplicity-one as
\tau\to-\infty
. We claim that
\check{M}(\tau)=\bar{M}(\tau+\tau_{0})
for some
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\bar{M}(\cdot)
is the ancient one-sided flow constructed above.

For simplicity, we assume in this outline that
\Sigma
has exactly one end and is asymptotically cylindrical, modeled on
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
. Multiple cylindrical ends do not interact in a significant way and conical ends can be handled by the arguments from
[
CCMS20
]
, so this is not a significant loss of generality. We will choose coordinates
({\bm{\omega}},z)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
so that the
\mathbb{R}
-factor of the asymptotic cylinder points in the
\mathbf{e}=\partial_{z}
direction.

The steps to prove uniqueness are as follows:

(1)

We prove a nearly sharp upper bound for the graphical function
\check{u}
in a fixed compact region
\Sigma_{0}\Subset\Sigma
(with smooth boundary) as
\tau\to-\infty
. Namely, for any
\mu_{0}
the first eigenvalue of
L
on
\Sigma_{0}
(with Dirichlet boundary conditions) we show (see Theorem
7.1
) that

(1.5)

\sup_{\Sigma_{0}}\check{u}\leq Ce^{-\mu_{0}\tau}

as
\tau\to-\infty
. (By taking
\Sigma_{0}
larger we can take
\mu_{0}
as close to
\mu
as we want.)

The basic idea is to consider
\varphi_{0}
the first eigenfunction for
L
on
\Sigma_{0}
with Dirichlet boundary and to form a subsolution
7
7

7

In reality, this expression fails to be a subsolution near
\partial\Sigma_{0}
. We use a different argument to prevent contact there by contrasting the Hopf boundary point lemma with a differential Harnack inequality proven for
\check{u}
.
of the form

ae^{-\mu_{0}\tau}(1-aMe^{-\mu_{0}\tau})\varphi_{0}

(this is similar to the ansatz used in the proof of existence). If the desired decay was false then by using the (parabolic) Harnack inequality we would be able to fit the subsolution below
\check{M}(\tau)
for larger and larger values of
a
(as
\tau\to-\infty
).

This part of the argument is very general and we expect it will be useful in other contexts.

(2)

Fixing
\delta(\tau):=\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{4}(\Sigma\cap B_{4R}(\mathbf{0}))}
for
R\gg 0
fixed, step (1) (along with Schauder theory) imply that
\delta(\tau)
has nearly sharp exponential decay as
\tau\to-\infty
. We combine this with a barrier argument to extend this to graphicality/decay in the
z
-direction (i.e., along the cylindrical end). In particular, we prove that there is some
\alpha\gg 0
so that
\check{M}(\tau)
is a small graph over the exponentially growing region
\Sigma\cap B_{\tfrac{1}{2}\delta(\tau)^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}}(\mathbf{0})
(see Proposition
7.10
). The barrier argument proceeds in the following manner:

(a)

Construction of the barriers: In the case that the cylindrical end was modeled on a round cylinder, we could try to use the foliation of the complement of the end of the cylinder by self-shrinkers as introduced by Angenent–Daskalopoulos–Šešum
[
ADS19
]
(cf.
[
KM14
]
).

However, since the actual end of
\Sigma
is likely to cross in and outside of the cylinder, these shrinkers will not fit perfectly inside/outside of
\Sigma
. To solve this problem, we observe that we can construct a “long” barrier by first writing the end of
\Sigma
as a graph over
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
and then perturbing this graph by
\eta z^{\alpha}\hat{\varphi}({\bm{\omega}})
for
\hat{\varphi}
the first eigenfunction of
L_{\hat{\Sigma}}
(see Proposition
6.5
). When
\eta>0
is sufficiently small, this property holds for
\alpha\leq z\leq\eta^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
. When
z=\eta^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
, the long barrier has peeled away from
\Sigma
a sufficient amount so that it can be “welded” (cf. Proposition
6.6
) to the
z\geq\eta^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
portion of a ADS shrinker that fits within the end of
\Sigma
.

(When
\hat{\Sigma}
is some arbitrary closed shrinker rather than the round sphere, we construct a replacement for the ADS shrinkers formed from the translator approximations to the rescaled (ancient) mean curvature flow coming out of
\hat{\Sigma}
; see Section
6.2
. We note also that Sun–Wang have very recently
[
SW23
]
constructed complete translators asymptotic to
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
which could alternatively be used here.)

(b)

The flow fits under the barrier with
\eta\sim\delta(\tau)
: We then need to show that the flow
\check{M}(\tau)
can be controlled along the end of
\Sigma
by using the barrier described in (a). To do so, we prove that the barriers from (a) either have bounded length or are asymptotically conical (see Lemma
6.4
) and that
\check{M}(\tau)
decays to
\Sigma
in a sublinear manner (see Proposition
7.3
). Combining these facts (and the decay from (1) above, we see that the flow
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
is pinched between
\Sigma
and the barrier.

(c)

Upgrading the barrier argument to graphicality:
A priori
it could hold that the flow bends into multiple sheets or develops a singularity in the region where it is pinched between the barrier and
\Sigma
. We know that this does not occur on fixed compact sets. Using a geometric continuity/bootstrap argument we can then extend this to the full barrier region (see Proposition
7.9
).

(3)

At this point the barrier argument from (b) has given that
\check{M}(\tau)
is graphical on a exponentially growing region (with nearly sharp pointwise estimates). We can use this, along with a Merle–Zaag type spectral dynamic analysis to prove that the graphical function
\check{u}
is equal to
\check{a}e^{-\mu\tau}\varphi+O(e^{(-\mu+\tfrac{\delta_{0}}{4})\tau})
on compact sets, i.e.,
\check{u}
behaves like one would expect based on a naive linear analysis as in (
1.4
) above (see Theorem
7.13
). Below we will assume that
\check{a}=1
(this can be arranged by a translation in time unless
\check{a}=0
; we ignore this possibility in this sketch, as it is easily handled by a modification of the
\check{a}=1
argument). We will also assume that
\bar{M}(\tau)
(the graphical flow constructed above) has the same top order spectral behavior, i.e.,
\bar{a}=1
.

(4)

We now prove that
\bar{M}(\tau)
lies (weakly)
below

\check{M}(\tau)
. (Later we will show they agree.) The main idea is for
\tau_{m}\to-\infty
appropriately chosen, we can cut off
(1-o(1))e^{-\mu\tau_{m}}\varphi(\cdot)
near radius
r_{m}\to\infty
to form an initial condition for the rescaled mean curvature flow that lies below both solutions
\check{M}(\tau_{m}),\bar{M}(\tau_{m})
. Since
\bar{M}(\tau_{m})
remains graphical (with good exponential growth estimates) until a fixed time (independent of
m
), a bootstrap argument implies that the rescaled mean curvature flow of this initial condition also exists until a fixed time (independent of
m
) and remains below both solutions
\check{M}(\tau),\bar{M}(\tau)
.

As
m\to\infty
these new solutions limit to ancient one-sided solutions that satisfy similar graphicality/decay estimates to the constructed graphical solution
\bar{M}(\tau)
(see Proposition
7.20
). Because the initial condition is formed by cutting off
(1-o(1))e^{-\mu\tau_{m}}\varphi(\cdot)
, a simple spectral dynamics and uniqueness argument implies that in fact this new solution agrees with
\bar{M}(\tau)
. Thus,
\bar{M}(\tau)
lies below
\check{M}(\tau)
(see Proposition
7.21
).

(5)

It thus remains to estimate
\check{M}(\tau)
“from above” (in reality we do not yet know that
\check{M}(\tau)
is an entire graph over
\Sigma
). As a first step, we observe that we could repeat the barrier argument used in (2) to pinch
\check{M}(\tau)
between barriers and
\bar{M}(\tau)
(instead of pinching it between barriers and
\Sigma
). This involves observing that the “long barrier” can be also considered as a graph over
\bar{M}(\tau)
instead of
\Sigma
and one can construct suitable barriers in this setting as well. This allows us to bootstrap estimates for the quantity

W(\tau)^{2}:=\sup_{s\leq\tau}\int_{\Sigma\cap B_{5R}(\mathbf{0})}(\check{u}(\cdot,s)-\bar{u}(\cdot,s))^{2}e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}.

Indeed, when
W(\tau)
is very small, we can fit very close barriers (upgrading
L^{2}
-smallness to
L^{\infty}
-smallness using Schauder estimates) to obtain graphicality on a very large scale
\sim W(\tau)^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}
. Then, this implies that the cutoff error estimates in a spectral dynamics argument for
\check{u}(\cdot,s)-\bar{u}(\cdot,s)
are very small, leading to further improved estimates for
W(\tau)
. Eventually (see Theorem
7.22
), this implies that
W(\tau)\equiv 0
, finishing the proof.

1.5.
Outline of application to mean curvature flow in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

Consider
M(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
a closed embedded surface. Assume that some weak
8
8

8

integral unit regular Brakke flow
mean curvature flow
M(t)
starting at
M(0)
has only multiplicity one spherical and cylindrical tangent flows until time
t=T
9
9

9

By the resolution of the mean convex neighborhood conjecture
[
CHH22
]
any two integral unit regular Brakke flows with the given initial condition agree at least until
t=T
.
. Assume that all singularities at time
t=T
have multiplicity one. For simplicity, we assume that there is exactly one singular point at
t=T
, at
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

We now consider a local foliation
M_{s}(0)
around
M(0)
and flow each leaf simultaneously into
M_{s}(t)
. Fixing
s_{j}\to 0
so that the level set flow of
M_{s_{j}}(0)
does not fatten, we parabolically dilate
M_{s_{j}}(t)
and
M_{0}(t)
around
(\mathbf{x},T)
by the parabolic distance
d_{j}
of the (space-time) support of the flow
t\mapsto M_{s_{j}}(t)
to
(\mathbf{x},T)
. Passing both flows to the limit, we obtain an ancient flow
\check{M}(t)
on one side of some
10
10

10

Uniqueness of tangent flows has not been proven in the case of shrinkers with cylindrical ends, cf.
[
Sch14
,
CM15
,
CS21
]
.
tangent flow to
t\mapsto M_{0}(t)
at
(\mathbf{x},T)
. By a standard argument based on Huisken’s monotonicity formula (cf. the proof of Lemma
9.8
) we have
\lambda(\check{M}(t))\leq F(\Sigma)
, so the tangent flow to
t\mapsto\check{M}(t)
is
t\mapsto\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
(i.e., the multiplicity is one). By the choice of rescaling,
\check{M}(t)
is not
t\mapsto\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
, so by the uniqueness result discussed above, it must be a parabolic dilation of the ancient one-sided flow
\bar{M}(t)
. We have that
t\mapsto\bar{M}(t)
has only multiplicity one spherical/cylindrical singularities, at least for
t<0
11
11

11

For the arguments developed here, we only need to analyze the flow for
t<0
. This is a strong contrast to
[
CCMS20
]
which considers the properties of the one-sided flow for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
(see, however
[
CCMS20
, §8.4]
).
.

In particular, returning to the original scale, we find that
t\mapsto M_{s_{j}}(t)
has only multiplicity one spherical/cylindrical singularities, at least until time
t=T-o(d_{j}^{2})
as
j\to\infty
. We claim that the genus of
M_{s_{j}}(T-o(d_{j}^{2}))
has strictly dropped in comparison to the genus of
M_{0}(t)
right before
t=T
. Recalling that the genus is non-increasing along mean curvature flow by work of White
[
Whi95
]
, we thus conclude the following result after
\leq\genus(M(0))
such perturbations:

Theorem

(Mean curvature flow of generic initial data in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, informal)
.

For
M(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
closed embedded surface, there is
M^{\prime}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
an arbitrarily small
C^{\infty}
normal graph over
M(0)
so that the mean curvature flow
t\mapsto M^{\prime}(t)
satisfies one of the following conditions:

•

all tangent flows at singular points are multiplicity one shrinking spheres or cylinders, or

•

at the first time this fails, some tangent flow has multiplicity
\geq 2
.

See Theorem
9.2
for the precise statement.

As such, the remaining ingredient we need to discuss is the genus drop. It suffices to show that the genus of the one-sided ancient rescaled flow
\tau\mapsto\bar{M}(\tau)
loses genus at some (rescaled) time
\tau<\infty
. Indeed, by an important result of Brendle
[
Bre16
]
,
\Sigma
has non-zero genus (if it is not a sphere/cylinder) and thus
\bar{M}(\tau)
does as well, for
\tau\ll 0
, since it is graphical at such times. In
[
CCMS20
]
we established the genus drop (when
\Sigma
is asymptotically conical or compact) by generalizing an observation of Bernstein–Wang
[
BW17
]
who noted that shrinker mean-convexity implies that the flow is
star-shaped
(and thus genus zero) at time
t=0
.

Here, we use a softer argument (cf. Proposition
8.5
), still using shrinker mean convexity, but only in the sense that the rescaled flow
\tau\mapsto\bar{M}(\tau)
flows in one direction. If
M(\tau)
has positive genus for
\tau\gg 0
we can find a non-trivial loop in the component of
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus M(\tau)
that
M(\tau)
is flowing
into
. (Some care must be taken to avoid a loop
\gamma
that just goes around a cylindrical end.) The avoidance principle for mean curvature flows rescales to imply that
M(\tau)
diverges as
\tau\to\infty
. Thus the loop
\gamma
can be chosen outside of any fixed compact set. In particular, with respect to
M(\tau)
,
\tau\ll 0
(so
M(\tau)
is
C^{\infty}
close to
\Sigma
), the loop
\gamma
lies near infinity and thus bounds a disk (near infinity
\Sigma
only consists of cylindrical/conical ends and we can choose
\gamma
not going around any of these ends). By work of White
[
Whi95
]
, a loop cannot bound a disk in the complement of a mean curvature flow at an earlier time but not a later time. This is a contradiction, completing the proof.

1.6.
Organization of the paper

We collect several fundamental definitions and conventions in Section
2
. In Section
3
we discuss self-shrinkers including their ends, and linear theory for the
L
operator. Section
4
contains some techniques for constructing graphical rescaled mean curvature flows over shrinkers. We prove
existence
of the ancient one-sided flow
\tau\mapsto\bar{M}(\tau)
in Section
5
. In Section
6
we construct barriers along the cylindrical end and then use these (along with other arguments) in Section to prove
uniqueness
of ancient one-sided flows in Section
7
. We prove the genus drop for ancient one-sided flows in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in Section
8
and combine everything to prove the main generic flow result in Section
9
.

There are also several appendices. Appendix
A
contains some important computations concerning the specific form of the quadratic error term in the linearization of the rescaled mean curvature flow equation over a shrinker. Appendix
B
continues this investigation with the additional assumption that the graph of a positive function, proving a Li–Yau estimate used in the proof of Theorem
7.1
. Appendix
C
recalls a definition of Brakke flow with additional forces while Appendix
D
recalls Ilmanen’s avoidance principle. Appendix
E
contains a result (possibly of independent interest) concerning the avoidance of a Brakke flow and a non-compact flow with uniformly bounded curvature. Appendix
F
contains some results about the topology of regions in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and Appendix
G
recalls some concepts from
[
CCMS20
]
concerning localized topological monotonicity for mean curvature flows. Finally, Appendix
H
contains some results about weak versions of uniqueness of tangent flows in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(also possibly of independent interest).

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we collect some preliminary definitions, conventions, and results.

2.1.
Spacetime and the level set flow

We define the
time
map
\mathfrak{t}:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
to be the projection
\mathfrak{t}(\mathbf{x},t)=t
. For
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
we will write
E(t):=E\cap\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(t)
. The knowledge of
E(t)
for all
t
is the same thing as knowing
E
, so we will often ignore the distinction.

For a compact
n
-manifold
M
(possibly with boundary), we consider
f:M\times[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
so that (i)
f
is continuous (ii)
f
is smooth on
(M\setminus\partial M)\times(a,b]
(iii)
f|_{M\times\{t\}}
is injective for each
t\in[a,b]
and (iii)
t\mapsto f(M\setminus\partial M,t)
is flowing by mean curvature flow. In this case we call

\mathcal{M}:=\cup_{t\in[a,b]}f(M,t)\times\{t\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}

a
classical mean curvature flow
and define the
heat boundary
of
\mathcal{M}
by

\partial\mathcal{M}:=f(M,a)\cup f(\partial M,[a,b]).

Classical flows that intersect must intersect in a point that belongs to at least one of their heat boundaries (cf.
[
Whi95
, Lemma 3.1]
).

For
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times[0,\infty)
,
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
is a
weak set flow
(generated by
\Gamma
) if
\mathcal{M}(0)=\Gamma(0)
and if
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a classical flow with
\partial\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
disjoint from
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
disjoint from
\Gamma
then
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is disjoint from
\mathcal{M}
. There may be more than one weak set flow generated by
\Gamma
.

The biggest such flow is called the
level set flow
, which can be constructed as follows: For
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times[0,\infty)
as above, we set

W_{0}:=\{(\mathbf{x},0):(\mathbf{x},0)\not\in\Gamma\}

and then let
W_{k+1}
denote the union of all classical flows
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
disjoint from
\Gamma
and
\partial\mathcal{M}\subset W_{k}
. The
level set flow
generated by
\Gamma
is then defined by

\mathcal{M}:=(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times[0,\infty))\setminus\cup_{k}W_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times[0,\infty).

See
[
ES91
,
Ilm94
,
Whi95
]
. If
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\{0\}
, we will write
F_{t}(\Gamma):=\mathcal{M}(t)
for the time
t
slice of the corresponding level set flow.

Fix
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
compact. We say that the level set flow of
\Gamma
is
non-fattening
if
F_{t}(\Gamma)
has no interior for each
t\geq 0
. This condition holds generically, namely if
u_{0}
is a continuous function with compact level sets
u_{0}^{-1}(s)
then the level set flow of
u_{0}^{-1}(s)
fattens for at most countably many values of
s

[
Ilm94
, §11.3-4]
.

2.2.
Integral Brakke flows

An (
n
-dimensional
12
12

12

Of course one can consider
k
-dimensional flows in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
but we will never do so in this paper, so we will often omit the “
n
-dimensionality” and implicitly assume that all Brakke flows are flows of “hypersurfaces.”
) integral Brakke flow in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is a
1
-parameter family of Radon measures
(\mu(t))_{t\in I}
so that

(1)

For almost every
t\in I
there is an integral
n
-dimensional varifold
V(t)
with
\mu(t)=\mu_{V(t)}
and so that
V(t)
has locally bounded first variation and mean curvature
\mathbf{H}
orthogonal to
\textrm{Tan}(V(t),\cdot)
almost everywhere.

(2)

For a bounded interval
[t_{1},t_{2}]\in I
and
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
compact, we have

\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{K}(1+|\mathbf{H}|^{2})d\mu(t)dt<\infty.

(3)

If
[t_{1},t_{2}]\subset I
and
f\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times[t_{1},t_{2}])
has
f\geq 0
then

\int f(\cdot,t_{2})d\mu(t_{2})-\int f(\cdot,t_{1})d\mu(t_{1})\leq\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int(-|\mathbf{H}|^{2}f+\mathbf{H}\cdot\nabla f+\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial t})d\mu(t)dt.

We will sometimes write
\mathcal{M}
to represent a Brakke flow.

We define the support of
\mathcal{M}=(\mu(t))_{t}
to be
\overline{\cup_{t}\supp\mu(t)\times\{t\}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
. It is useful to recall that the support a Brakke flow (with
t\in[0,\infty)
) is a weak set flow (generated by
\supp\mu(0)
)
[
Ilm94
, 10.5]
.

We say that a sequence of integral Brakke flows
\mathcal{M}_{i}
converges to another integral Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}
(written
\mathcal{M}_{i}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{M}
) if
\mu_{i}(t)
weakly converges to
\mu(t)
for all
t
and for almost every
t
, after passing to a further subsequence depending on
t
, the associated integral varifolds converge
V_{i}(t)\to V(t)
. (Recall that if
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is a sequence of integral Brakke flows with uniform local mass bounds then a subsequence converges to an integral Brakke flow
[
Ilm94
, §7]
.)

We denote the set of points so that an integral Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}
is locally (in a backwards-forwards parabolic neighborhood) a smooth multiplicity one flow as
\reg\mathcal{M}
and if
\mathcal{M}
is defined on
[T_{0},\infty)
, we set
\sing\mathcal{M}:=\supp\mathcal{M}\setminus(\reg\mathcal{M}\cup\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\{T_{0}\})
.

2.3.
Density, Huisken’s monotonicity, and entropy

For
X_{0}=(\mathbf{x}_{0},t_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
we consider the (
n
-dimensional) backwards heat kernel based at
X_{0}
:

(2.1)

\rho_{X_{0}}(\mathbf{x},t):=(4\pi(t_{0}-t))^{-\frac{n}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}_{0}|^{2}}{4(t_{0}-t)}\right)

for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1},t<t_{0}
. For
\mathcal{M}
a Brakke flow defined on
[T_{0},\infty)
,
t_{0}>T_{0}
and
0<r\leq\sqrt{T_{0}-t_{0}}
, we set

\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(X_{0},r):=\int\rho_{X_{0}}(\mathbf{x},t_{0}-r^{2})d\mu(t_{0}-r^{2}).

Huisken’s monotonicity formula
[
Hui90
,
Ilm95
]
implies that
r\mapsto\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(X_{0},r)
is non-decreasing (and constant only for a shrinking self-shrinker centered at
X_{0}
, cf. Section
3.1
). In particular we can define the density of
\mathcal{M}
at such
X_{0}
by

\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(X_{0}):=\lim_{r\searrow 0}\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(X_{0},r).

We call an integral Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}

unit-regular
if
\mathcal{M}
is smooth in a forwards-backwards space-time neighborhood of any space-time point
X
with
\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(X)=1
. Note that we can then write
\sing\mathcal{M}=\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}:\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(X)>1\}
. Note that by
[
SW20a
, Theorem 4.2]
the class of unit-regular integral Brakke flows is closed under the convergence of Brakke flows. Furthermore, combining
[
SW20a
, Lemma 4.1]
and
[
Whi05
]
it follows that there is
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, depending only on dimension, such that every point
X\in\sing\mathcal{M}
has
\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(X)\geq 1+\varepsilon_{0}
. Upper semi-continuity of density then implies that
\sing\mathcal{M}
is closed.

Following
[
CM12a
]
for a Radon measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
we define the entropy of
\mu
by

\lambda(\mu):=\sup_{\mathbf{x}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1},t_{0}>0}\int\rho_{(\mathbf{x}_{0},t_{0})}(\mathbf{x},0)d\mu

where
\rho
is the Gaussian as in (
2.1
). When
\mu=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor M
for
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
a properly embedded submanifold we will write
\lambda(M)
instead. We define
\lambda_{j}:=\lambda(\mathbb{S}^{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{j+1})
and note that
\lambda_{j}=\lambda(\mathbb{S}^{j}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-j})
.

2.4.
Cyclic Brakke flows

We call an integral Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}=(\mu(t))_{t\in I}

cyclic
if for a.e.
t\in I
it holds that
\mu(t)=\mu_{V(t)}
for an integral varifold
V(t)
whose uniquely associated rectifiable mod-
2
flat chain
[V(t)]
has
\partial[V(t)]=0
. Loosely speaking, the cyclic condition can be used to rule out triple junctions (and generalizations thereof). By
[
Whi09
, Theorem 4.2]
the cyclic property is preserved under weak limits of integral Brakke flows.

Part I
Existence and uniqueness of one sided ancient flows

3.
Self-shrinkers

3.1.
Self-shrinkers and curvature conventions

We recall that a
self-shrinker
is a hypersurface
\Sigma^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
satisfying

(3.1)

\mathbf{H}+\frac{\mathbf{x}^{\perp}}{2}=0\,

where
\mathbf{H}
is its
mean curvature vector
,
\mathbf{x}
the position vector and
⟂
the projection to the normal space.

Recall that (
3.1
) is equivalent to any of the following conditions:

•

t\mapsto\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
is a mean curvature flow for
t<0
,

•

\Sigma
is a minimal hypersurface with respect to
e^{-\frac{1}{2n}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}g_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}
, or

•

\Sigma
is a critical point of the
F
-functional

F(\Sigma):=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{\Sigma}e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}

among compactly supported deformations and translations/dilations.

See
[
CM12a
, Section 3]
.

We denote with
\mathcal{S}_{n}
the set of smooth properly embedded self shrinkers
\Sigma^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with finitely many ends and let
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{*}
denote the subset of non-flat ones. Moreover (cf.
[
BW18b
]
) set

\mathcal{S}_{n}(\Lambda)=\{\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}:\lambda(\Sigma)<\Lambda\}.

Furthermore, we consider tuples
(\Sigma,\Omega)
where
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
and
\Omega
is one of the two unique choices of open subsets
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
\partial\Omega=\Sigma
. By an abuse of notation we will write
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
.

We also define

\mathcal{S}^{\textrm{gen}}_{n}:=\left\{O(\mathbb{S}^{j}(\sqrt{2j})\times\mathbb{R}^{n-j})\in\mathcal{S}_{n}:j=1,\dots,k,\,O\in O(n+1)\right\}

to be the set of (round) self-shrinking spheres and cylinders in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

We denote with
\nu_{\Sigma}
the choice of unit normal vector field to
\Sigma
pointing into
\Omega
. We define its
shape operator
(or Weingarten map)

(3.2)

S_{p}:T_{p}\Sigma\to T_{p}\Sigma,\qquad\xi\mapsto-D_{\xi}\nu_{\Sigma}

and its second fundamental form

(3.3)

A:T_{p}\Sigma\times T_{p}\Sigma\to\mathbb{R},\qquad(\xi,\zeta)\mapsto A(\xi,\zeta)=S_{p}(\xi)\cdot\zeta\,.

We fix the sign of the
scalar mean curvature

H
as follows

\mathbf{H}=H\,\nu_{\Sigma}\,,

and thus
H=\tr S=\tr A
, with the principal curvatures of
\Sigma
being the eigenvalues of
S
. Observe that the shrinker mean curvature can be written as

\mathbf{H}+\frac{\mathbf{x}^{\perp}}{2}=\left(H+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma}\right)\nu_{\Sigma}

Note that with these conventions, a sphere bounding a ball has normal vector pointing to the inside, positive mean curvature and positive principal curvatures. Similarly, a sphere bounding the complement of a closed ball has normal vector pointing to the outside, negative mean curvature and negative principal curvatures.

For a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
with Levi–Civita connection
\nabla
, we define the
Riemannian curvature tensor
by

(3.4)

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}}\mathbf{Z}-\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{Z}

(3.5)

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{W})

\displaystyle=g(R(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\mathbf{W},\mathbf{Z})

With this convention, if
\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2}\in T_{p}M
are orthonormal, the sectional curvature of
\Pi=\Span\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2}\}
is given by
K(\Pi)=R(\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2},\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2})
. Furthermore, if
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is a hypersurface then the Gauss equations become

(3.6)

R(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{W})=A(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Z})A(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{W})-A(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{W})A(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}).

In particular, we have

(3.7)

\Ric(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=HA(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})-A^{2}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}),

where
A^{2}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}A(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{e}_{i})A(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{e}_{i})
for an orthonormal basis
\mathbf{e}_{i}
.

3.2.
Ends of shrinkers

For any
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
, define

\sqrt{0}\Sigma:=\lim_{t\nearrow 0}\sqrt{-t}\Sigma

where the limit is taken in the Hausdorff sense. For
\mathbf{x}\in\sqrt{0}\Sigma
, define
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x})
to be the set of tangent flows to the flow
\{\sqrt{-t}\Sigma\}_{\{t<0\}}
at
(\mathbf{x},0)
. Note that if
\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{0}
, any tangent flow in
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x})
(weakly) splits a line in the
\mathbf{x}
direction.

We define the class of shrinkers that we consider in this paper as follows.

Definition 3.1

(Shrinkers with nice ends)
.

We say that
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
has
nice ends
if for
\mathbf{x}\in\sqrt{0}\Sigma\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
, the set
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x})
has precisely one element
(-\infty,0)\ni t\mapsto\mathcal{M}(t)
and moreover, one of the following holds:

(1)

\mathcal{M}(t)
is a multiplicity one plane, or

(2)

\mathcal{M}(t)
is the the multiplicity one flow associated to the smooth flow
\sqrt{-t}\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathbf{x}}
, for
\hat{\Sigma}
a
compact
smooth shrinker in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\mathbf{x}^{\perp}}:=\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:\left\langle\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\right\rangle=0\}
.

If
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
has nice ends, we write
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{n}
. We define
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}(\Lambda)=\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{S}_{n}(\Lambda)
.

The following lemma follows immediately from the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}
combined with Brakke regularity.

Lemma 3.2

(Asymptotic decomposition of shrinkers with nice ends)
.

For
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}
, there is
R=R(\Sigma)
sufficiently large so that

\Sigma\setminus B_{R}(\mathbf{0})=\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}\cup\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}

so that
\lambda\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}
converges in
C^{\infty}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus\{0\})
with multiplicity one to
\sqrt{0}\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}
as
\lambda\to 0
, and for any connected component
\Sigma^{\prime}\subset\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}
there exists
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and a compact smooth shrinker
\hat{\Sigma}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\mathbf{x}^{\perp}}
so that
\Sigma^{\prime}-s\mathbf{x}
converges in
C^{\infty}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
with multiplicity one to
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathbf{x}}
as
s\to\infty
.

3.3.
Function spaces

For ease of reference, we collect below various norms to be used in later sections. We will use the notation
L^{2}_{W}
for the Gaussian weighted
L^{2}
-space on a shrinker
\Sigma
, i.e.,

\left\langle u,v\right\rangle_{W}=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{\Sigma}vwe^{-\tfrac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}

The following weighted Hölder spaces will be used along the conical ends.

\displaystyle\|f\|^{(d)}_{k;\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}}|\mathbf{x}|^{-d+i}|\nabla^{i}_{\Sigma}f(\mathbf{x})|

\displaystyle[f]^{(d)}_{\alpha;\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{y}\in\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}|^{d-\alpha}+|\mathbf{y}|^{d-\alpha}}\frac{|f(\mathbf{x})-f(\mathbf{y})|}{d_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\|f\|^{(d)}_{k,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}}

\displaystyle:=\|f\|^{(d)}_{k;\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}}+[\nabla^{k}_{\Sigma}f]^{(d-k)}_{\alpha;\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}}.

When working with flows that are not known to be graphical over the entire cylindrical end, we will use the following semi-global norms

\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha;\text{cyl}}(\rho)=\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha;\Sigma\cap B_{2R_{0}}(\mathbf{0})}+\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}\cap B_{\rho}(\mathbf{0})}

and

\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha}(\rho)=\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha;\text{cyl}}(\rho)+\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}\,,

where the first norm only considers the compact part along with a finite part of the cylindrical piece, while the second normal also controls the conical ends (with an appropriate weight). These norms are used in the proof of uniqueness in the ancient past in Section
7
.

3.4.
Linear theory on shrinkers with stable ends

Consider a shrinker with nice ends
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{n}
. By Lemma
3.2
the ends of
\Sigma
decompose as the disjoint union
\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}\cup\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}
of cylindrical and smoothly asymptotically conical ends. We denote the asymptotically conical ends by
\Sigma^{\prime}_{\textnormal{con},i}
for
i=1,\dots,N_{\textnormal{con}}
with asymptotic cones
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and the asymptotically cylindrical ends
\Sigma^{\prime}_{\textnormal{cyl},j}
for
j=1,\dots,N_{\textnormal{cyl}}
with asymptotic directions
\mathbf{x}_{j}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
and asymptotic compact smooth shrinkers
\hat{\Sigma}_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\perp}}
.

We denote by
\mu
the smallest eigenvalue of the following operator
13
13

13

Recall that we say that
\mu
is an eigenvalue of
L
with eigenfunction
\varphi
if
L\varphi=-\mu\varphi
. We will write
L_{\Sigma}
,
\Delta_{\Sigma}
, etc., when the dependence on
\Sigma
is not clear.
acting on functions
u\in C^{2}_{\textrm{loc}}(\Sigma)

Lu=\Delta u-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\nabla u+\tfrac{1}{2}u+|A|^{2}u

(where
A
is the second fundamental form of
\Sigma
) and will write
0<\varphi\in H^{1}_{W}(\Sigma)
for the corresponding first eigenfunction (unique up to scaling). We normalize
\|\varphi\|_{W}=1
. The existence and uniqueness of
\varphi
follows as in the proof of
[
BW17
, Proposition 4.1]
, using the results in
[
CM12a
]
. We denote the corresponding eigenvalues and first eigenfunctions of the asymptotic shrinkers
\hat{\Sigma}_{j}
by
\hat{\mu}_{j}
and
\hat{\varphi}_{j}
respectively. Recall that by Colding–Minicozzi’s classification of entropy stable shrinkers,
[
CM12a
, Theorems 0.17 and 9.36]
, all non flat shrinkers which are not a sphere or a (generalised) round cylinder satisfy
\mu<-1
.

Definition 3.3

(Shrinkers with nice stable ends)
.

We say a shrinker
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
has
stable ends
if the first eigenfunction
\varphi
satisfies
\varphi\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
.
If
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{n}
has nice stable ends, we write
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
. We define
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\Lambda)=\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime}\cap\mathcal{S}_{n}(\Lambda)
.

Definition 3.4

(Shrinkers with nice strictly stable ends)
.

We say a shrinker
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
has
strictly stable ends
if the first eigenfunction
\varphi
satisfies
\varphi(\mathbf{x})=o(1)
for
\Sigma\ni\mathbf{x}\to\infty
. If
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{n}
has nice strictly stable ends, we write
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{n}
. We define
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}(\Lambda)=\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}\cap\mathcal{S}_{n}(\Lambda)
.

Clearly if
\Sigma=\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
for
\hat{\Sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{n-1}
a smooth compact shrinker, it holds that
\Sigma
has nice stable ends
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
(but
\Sigma\not\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}
). For a non-cylindrical shrinker with nice ends, we show in part (2) of Proposition
3.6
below that if
\hat{\mu}_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,N_{\textnormal{cyl}}
are the first eigenvalues of the cylindrical ends of
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}
, then a sufficient condition for
\Sigma
to have strictly stable ends is
\mu<\hat{\mu}_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,N_{\textnormal{cyl}}
.

We note that by the result of Wang
[
Wan16a
]
, for
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
(i.e., an embedded shrinker in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
), all ends are either cylindrical, with
\hat{\Sigma}
being the shrinking circle, or asymptotically conical. Thus,
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\prime}
. Combining this with Colding–Minicozzi’s estimate
\mu<-1
and properties (1) and (2) Proposition
3.6
below we see that any non-cylindrical shrinker in
\mathcal{S}_{2}
has nice, strictly stable ends.

Proposition 3.5

(
[
Wan16a
,
CM12a
]
)
.

\mathcal{S}_{2}=\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\textnormal{gen}}=\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

It is an interesting question to what extent the inclusions
\mathcal{S}_{3}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathcal{S}_{3}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{S}_{3}
,
\mathcal{S}_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime}\subset\mathcal{S}_{3}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{3}^{\textrm{gen}}
are strict.
14
14

14

For example, if we fix a smooth closed embedded shrinker
\hat{\Sigma}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
(e.g., the round sphere, or the Angenent torus), we do not know if there exists
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{3}^{\prime}
with an end asymptotic to
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
(other than
\Sigma=\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
) and if such
\Sigma
exists, we do not know if it must have (strictly) stable ends when
\Sigma
is not a round sphere. These questions are likely to be relevant to the understanding of (generic) mean curvature flow of hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

We extend here the analysis in
[
BW17
, Proposition 4.1]
of the first eigenfunction of
L_{\Sigma}
from asymptotically conical shrinkers to shrinkers with nice ends.

Lemma 3.6

(Decay for first eigenfunction and sufficient condition for stable ends)
.

Assume
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{n}
is a smooth shrinker with nice ends. Write
\mu
for the first eigenvalue of
L_{\Sigma}
on
\Sigma
.

(1)

Fix
\beta>0
. Along
\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}
we have

\displaystyle(1+|\mathbf{x}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}+\mu-\beta}\lesssim\varphi(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+|\mathbf{x}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}+\mu+\beta},

\displaystyle|\nabla^{m}\varphi(\mathbf{x})|

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+|\mathbf{x}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}+\mu+\beta-\frac{m}{2}}.

(2)

Write
\mu_{j}
for the first eigenvalue of
L_{\hat{\Sigma}_{j}\times\mathbb{R}}
for the exact cylinder associated to each cylindrical end
\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl},j}^{\prime}
,
j=1,\dots,N_{\textnormal{cyl}}
. Assume that

(3.8)

\mu<\mu_{j}\textrm{ for }j=1,\dots,N_{\textnormal{cyl}}.

Then, along
\Sigma^{\prime}_{\textnormal{cyl},j}\subset\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}
, we have

(3.9)

\displaystyle(1+|\mathbf{x}|^{2})^{\mu-\hat{\mu}_{j}-\beta}\lesssim\varphi(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+|\mathbf{x}|^{2})^{\mu-\hat{\mu}_{j}+\beta},

(3.10)

\displaystyle|\nabla^{m}\varphi(\mathbf{x})|

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+|\mathbf{x}|^{2})^{\mu-\hat{\mu}_{j}+\beta}.

In particular, (
3.8
) implies that
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

(3)

Assume that
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
, i.e.,
\Sigma
has stable ends (but do not necessarily assume the
\mu<\mu_{j}
condition in (
3.8
)). Then,
|\nabla^{m}\varphi|\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
and
\varphi^{-1}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
.

Moreover, there is
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\Sigma)>0
with the following properties. For
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, the normal graph of
\varepsilon\varphi
is a smooth surface
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\subset\Omega
. Denote
\Omega_{\varepsilon}\subset\Omega
by the open set with
\partial\Omega_{\varepsilon}=\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
. The surface
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
is strictly shrinker mean convex to the interior of
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
in the sense that

(3.11)

2H_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}+\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}\geq\varepsilon(2|\mu|-C\varepsilon)\varphi(\mathbf{x})

for
C=C(\Sigma)
. Furthermore, the entropy of
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the bound
\lambda(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\leq F(\Sigma)+o(1)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

Recall from the proof of
[
BW17
, Proposition 4.1]
that for every
\beta>0
there exists
R=R(\Sigma,\beta)
such along each asymptotically conical end
\Sigma^{\prime}_{\textnormal{con},i}
we have barriers

(3.12)

\overline{g}(\mathbf{x})=|x|^{1+2\mu+2\beta}\text{ and }\underline{g}(\mathbf{x})=|x|^{1+2\mu-2\beta}\,,

which satisfy

(3.13)

L\overline{g}+\mu\overline{g}<0\text{ and }L\underline{g}+\mu\underline{g}>0

on
\Sigma^{\prime}_{\textnormal{con},i}\setminus\overline{B}_{R}
.

Similarly, for an asymptotically cylindrical end
\Sigma^{\prime}_{\textnormal{cyl},j}
, so that
\mu_{j}<\mu
,
let
\mathbf{e}:=\mathbf{x}_{j}
and denote the coordinate in direction
\mathbf{e}
by
z
. Let
\bar{\varphi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\mathbf{e}^{\perp}})
be an extension of the first eigenfunction of
L_{\hat{\Sigma}_{i}}
,
\hat{\varphi}_{j}
, to a neighborhood of
\hat{\Sigma}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\mathbf{e}^{\perp}}
. We make the ansatz for a barrier as the ambient function

g:=z^{-\alpha}\bar{\varphi}

for some
\alpha>0
, where we extend
\bar{\varphi}
constant in
\mathbf{e}
-direction. Note that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}g

\displaystyle=-\alpha z^{-\alpha-1}\bar{\varphi}\mathbf{e}+z^{-\alpha}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\bar{\varphi}

(3.14)

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}g

\displaystyle=\alpha(\alpha+1)z^{-\alpha-2}\bar{\varphi}\,dz\otimes dz-\alpha z^{-\alpha-1}\left(dz\otimes d\bar{\varphi}+d\bar{\varphi}\otimes dz\right)+z^{-\alpha}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\bar{\varphi}

Since
g
is an ambient function we compute (compare the proof of
[
CS21
, Lemma 3.15]
)

Lg=\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}g-\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}g(\nu,\nu)-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}g+\tfrac{1}{2}g+|A|^{2}g\,.

Note that

-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}g=\tfrac{\alpha}{2}g-\tfrac{1}{2}z^{-\alpha}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\mathbf{e}^{\perp}}}\bar{\varphi}\,.

Furthermore, since
\hat{\varphi}_{j}>0
, using (
3.14
), we can estimate as
z\to\infty
that

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}g(\nu_{\Sigma},\nu_{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=z^{-\alpha}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\mathbf{e}^{\perp}}}\bar{\varphi}(\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}_{j}},\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}_{j}})+o(1)g

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}g

\displaystyle=z^{-\alpha}\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\mathbf{e}^{\perp}}}\bar{\varphi}+o(1)g

\displaystyle|A_{\Sigma}|^{2}g

\displaystyle=z^{-\alpha}|A_{\hat{\Sigma}_{j}}|^{2}\bar{\varphi}+o(1)g\,,

where have extended
\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}_{j}}
and
|A_{\hat{\Sigma}_{j}}|^{2}
to a neighborhood of
\hat{\Sigma}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\mathbf{e}^{\perp}}
. We thus can estimate

\begin{split}Lg&=z^{-\alpha}\left(\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\mathbf{e}^{\perp}}}\bar{\varphi}-\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\mathbf{e}^{\perp}}}\bar{\varphi}(\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}_{j}},\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}_{j}})-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\mathbf{e}^{\perp}}}\bar{\varphi}+\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{\varphi}+|A_{\hat{\Sigma}_{j}}|^{2}\bar{\varphi}\right)+\tfrac{\alpha}{2}g+o(1)g\\
&=\left(\tfrac{\alpha}{2}-\hat{\mu}_{j}+o(1)\right)g\,.\end{split}

Thus for every
\beta>0
there is
R=R(\Sigma^{\prime}_{j},\beta)
such that choosing
\alpha=2(\hat{\mu}_{j}-\mu-\beta)
we have an upper barrier
\overline{g}
and choosing
\alpha=2(\hat{\mu}_{j}-\mu+\beta)
we have a lower barrier
\underline{g}
satisfying

(3.15)

L\overline{g}+\mu\overline{g}<0\text{ and }L\underline{g}+\mu\underline{g}>0

on
\Sigma^{\prime}_{j}\setminus B_{R}
.
In both the asymptotically conical and the asymptotically cylindrical case we can choose
C>1
such that

C^{-1}\underline{g}<\varphi<C\overline{g}

on
\partial(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus B_{R})
. As such,
v=\varphi-C\overline{g}
satisfies
Lv+\mu v>0
and
v<0
in a neighborhood of
\partial(\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus B_{R})
. We now argue that
v\leq 0
on
\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus B_{R}
. Since
v^{+}\in H^{1}_{W}
, we find that

\begin{split}-\mu\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{R}}(v^{+})^{2}\rho\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}&\leq\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{R}}v^{+}Lv\rho\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}\\
&=\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{R}}\left(-|\nabla v^{+}|^{2}+\left(\frac{1}{2}+|A_{\Sigma}|^{2}\right)(v^{+})^{2}\right)\rho\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}\,.\end{split}

Thus, using Ecker’s Sobolev inequality
[
Eck00
]
(cf.
[
CS21
, Proposition 3.9]
) and that
|A_{\Sigma}|
is bounded, we find that

R^{2}\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{R}}(v^{+})^{2}\rho\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}\leq C\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{R}}(v^{+})^{2}\rho\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}\,.

Thus choosing
R
sufficiently large, if necessary, we thus see that
v^{+}\equiv 0
on
\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus B_{R}(0)
.

Thus
\varphi\leq C\overline{g}
on
\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus B_{R}(0)
. The same reasoning gives
\varphi\geq C^{-1}\underline{g}
on
\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus B_{R}(0)
. This proves the
C^{0}
-estimate in properties (1) and (2). In particular, this proves that (
3.8
) implies
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

We now explain the derivative estimates in (1) and (2). In the asymptotically conical case, the decay estimates for the higher derivatives of
\varphi
follow as in the proof of
[
BW17
, Proposition 4.1]
.
For the asymptotically cylindrical ends, consider for
t<0
and
\mathbf{q}\in\sqrt{-t}\Sigma:=\Sigma_{t}
the function

\tilde{\varphi}(\mathbf{q},t)=\varphi\left(\frac{\mathbf{q}}{\sqrt{-t}}\right)\,.

Then since
L\varphi=-\mu\varphi
we have that

\left(\frac{d}{dt}-\Delta_{\Sigma_{t}}\right)\tilde{\varphi}=\left(|A_{\Sigma_{t}}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}+\mu\right)\tilde{\varphi}\,.

Note that (
3.9
) implies that for
t\in(-3,-1]
along
(\Sigma^{\prime}_{j})_{t}

\tilde{\varphi}(\mathbf{x})\leq C(1+|\mathbf{x}|^{2})^{\mu-\hat{\mu}_{j}+\beta}

which is decaying for
\beta<\hat{\mu}_{j}-\mu
and thus bounded. Standard interior estimates imply that all higher derivatives of
\tilde{\varphi}
are uniformly bounded along
(\Sigma^{\prime}_{j})_{t}
for
t\in[-2,-1]
. Then (
3.10
) follows from (
3.9
) via interpolation.

Finally, we consider (3). Assume that
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
. We first note that the bounds for higher derivatives of the first eigenfunction (with no decay) follows from interior estimates (on
t\mapsto\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
) exactly as in the above argument. We now estimate
\varphi^{-1}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}
. Using the fact that
\Sigma
has bounded geometry and the fact that
\varphi
has bounded
C^{2}
norm, there is
r>0
so that normal coordinates exist on a ball of radius
r
centered at each
\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma
and for
\mathbf{y}\in B_{r}(\mathbf{x})
we have the following Taylor expansion

0\leq\varphi(\mathbf{y})\leq\varphi(\mathbf{x})+\partial_{i}\varphi|_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y}^{i}-\mathbf{x}^{i})+C|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|^{2}.

in the normal coordinates (with constant
C
uniformly bounded for all such
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
). Since
\nabla\varphi\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, we can choose
\mathbf{y}^{i}:=-s\partial_{i}\varphi|_{\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{x}^{i}\in B_{r}(\mathbf{x})
, for
s>0
sufficiently small (independent of
\mathbf{x}
). Thus, we find

0\leq\varphi(\mathbf{x})-s|\nabla\varphi(\mathbf{x})|^{2}+Cs^{2}|\nabla\varphi(\mathbf{x})|^{2}.

Taking
s>0
even smaller, we conclude that
\varphi^{-1}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
.
15
15

15

Note that this estimate could alternatively have been proven by appropriately modifying the argument in
[
Ilm94
, Lemma 6.6]
.

Because
\Sigma
has a uniform tubular neighborhood, for
\varepsilon>0
, the normal graph of
\varepsilon\varphi
,
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
, is a smooth surface. Moreover, by Corollary
A.4
(and recalling that
v=(\nu_{\Gamma}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma})^{-1}=1+o(1)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
) we have

v(\mathbf{x})(H+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma}\cdot\nu_{\Gamma})=\varepsilon L\varphi+E=-\varepsilon\mu\varphi+E

where

E=\varepsilon\varphi E_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}E_{2}(\nabla\varphi,\nabla\varphi)

with
|E_{1}|=O(\varepsilon),|E_{2}|=O(1)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly on
\Sigma
. Using the
\varphi^{-1}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
estimate from above, we find

v(\mathbf{x})(H+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma}\cdot\nu_{\Gamma})=\varepsilon L\varphi+E=-\varepsilon\mu\varphi+O(\varepsilon^{2}\varphi)

This proves the strictly shrinker mean convex assertion.

The final statement on the entropy follows directly from the smooth convergence of
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\to\Sigma
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
∎

4.
Geometric estimates for graphical flows

This section contains some well-known considerations relating graphs over shrinkers to rescaled mean curvature flows, including a long-time existence for rescaled flows with small
L^{\infty}
-norm.

We begin by using pseudolocality and interior estimates to prove higher derivative estimates for a graphical rescaled mean curvature flow over a shrinker.

Proposition 4.1

(Bootstrap for graphical flows)
.

Fix a shrinker
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}
. There are constants
\psi_{0}=\psi_{0}(\Sigma),\psi_{1}=\psi_{1}(\Sigma)
with the following properties. For
T>1
, suppose that
v\in C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Sigma\times[0,T))
has

(4.1)

\sup_{\Sigma}|v(\cdot,\tau)|\leq\psi_{0},\qquad\sup_{\Sigma}|\nabla_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,\tau)|\leq\psi_{1}

for all
\tau\in[0,T)
and
\tau\mapsto\Gamma(\tau):=\Graph_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,\tau)
is a rescaled mean curvature flow.

Then, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds that

\sup_{\Sigma\times[1,T)}|\nabla^{m}_{\Sigma}v|\leq C=C(m,\Sigma)

Furthermore, if

\limsup_{\tau\to T}\sup_{\Sigma}|v(\cdot,T)|<\psi_{0}

then we can extend the flow to
v^{\prime}\in(\Sigma\times[0,T^{\prime}])
for some
T^{\prime}>T
with
v^{\prime}
still satisfying (
4.1
) for all
\tau\in[0,T^{\prime}]
.

Proof.

Choose
\psi_{1}=\psi_{1}(\Sigma)
sufficiently small so that if
w\in C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Sigma)
has

\sup_{\Sigma}|w(\cdot)|,\sup_{\Sigma}|\nabla_{\Sigma}w(\cdot)|\leq\psi_{1}

then
\Graph_{\Sigma}w(\cdot)
is a uniformly small Lipschitz graph in balls of uniform size (over tangent planes to
\Sigma
) and
F(\Graph_{\Sigma}w(\cdot))\leq(2-\delta)F(\Sigma)
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
fixed.

Now, consider
T>1
and
v\in C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Sigma\times[0,T))
with

(4.2)

\sup_{\Sigma}|v(\cdot,\tau)|\leq\psi_{1},\qquad\sup_{\Sigma}|\nabla_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,\tau)|\leq\psi_{1}

for all
\tau\in[0,T)
. Assume that
\tau\mapsto\Gamma(\tau)
is a rescaled mean curvature flow. Due to the choice of
\psi_{1}
above, we can locally write the flow,

[-e,-1]\ni t\mapsto\sqrt{-t}\left(\Graph_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,\bar{\tau}-\log(-t))\right)

as a uniformly bounded Lipschitz graphs over balls of uniform size. Interior estimates
[
EH91
, Corollary 3.5]
for mean curvature flow applied at
t=-1
thus imply that

\sup_{\Gamma(\bar{\tau})}|\nabla^{m}A_{\Gamma(\bar{\tau})}|\leq C^{\prime}(m,\Sigma)

This yields the asserted
C^{m}
-estimates as long as
\psi_{0}\leq\psi_{1}
.

It remains to choose
\psi_{0}=\psi_{0}(\Sigma)\leq\psi_{1}
sufficiently small so that the second property holds. Before doing so, we observe that the curvature estimates just derived show that
\Gamma(\tau)
smoothly limits to some
\Gamma(T)
and combined with
[
EH91
, Theorem 4.2]
, we find that there is
T^{\prime\prime}>T
(independent of
\psi_{0}
) so that the rescaled mean curvature flow
\tau\mapsto\Gamma(\tau)
exists for
\tau\in[0,T^{\prime\prime}]
. The same argument used above implies that

(4.3)

\sup_{\Gamma(\bar{\tau})}|\nabla^{m}A_{\Gamma(\bar{\tau})}|\leq C^{\prime}(m,\Sigma)

holds for
\tau\in[T,T^{\prime\prime}]
(after shrinking
T^{\prime\prime}
if necessary).

We now fix
\psi_{0}=\psi_{0}(\Sigma,\psi_{1})\leq\psi_{1}
so that if
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is a properly embedded hypersurface with
\Gamma\subset U_{\psi_{0}}(\Sigma)
,
F(\Gamma)\leq(2-\delta)F(\Sigma)
and
|A_{\Gamma}|\leq C^{\prime}(1,\Sigma)
(the constant in (
4.3
) with
m=1
), then

\Gamma=\Graph_{\Sigma}w,

where
w\in C^{2}_{\textrm{loc}}(\Sigma)
satisfies

\sup_{\Sigma}|w(\cdot)|\leq\psi_{0},\qquad\sup_{\Sigma}|\nabla_{\Sigma}w(\cdot)|\leq\psi_{1}.

(To see that
\psi_{0}
exists, note that as
\psi_{0}\to 0
,
\Gamma
will converge to
\Sigma
in
C^{1,\alpha}
due to the entropy and second fundamental form bounds. In this limit, we have the uniform decay
\nabla_{\Sigma}w=o(1)
, while
|w|\leq\psi_{0}
follows from
\Gamma\subset U_{\psi_{0}}(\Sigma)
.)

We now assume that

\limsup_{\tau\to T}\sup_{\Sigma}|v(\cdot,T)|<\psi_{0}

where we have chosen
\psi_{0}
above. Using small spheres as barriers we can conclude that there is
T^{\prime}\in[T,T^{\prime\prime}]
so that
\Gamma(\tau)\subset U_{\psi_{0}}(\Sigma)
for
\tau\in[T,T^{\prime}]
. Moreover,
F(\Gamma(\tau))\leq(2-\delta)F(\Sigma)
by monotonicity of
F
under the rescaled flow. Finally, (
4.3
) implies that
|A_{\Gamma(\tau)}|\leq C^{\prime}(1,\Sigma)
for
\tau\in[T,T^{\prime}]
. Thus, by the choice of
\psi_{0}
, we see that
\Gamma(\tau)=\Graph_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,\tau)
with

\sup_{\Sigma}|v(\cdot,\tau)|\leq\psi_{0},\qquad\sup_{\Sigma}|\nabla_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,\tau)|\leq\psi_{1}.

for all
\tau\in[T,T^{\prime}]
. This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 4.2

(Existence of graphical flows)
.

Fix a shrinker
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}
. There is a constant
\psi^{*}=\psi^{*}(\Sigma)
so that if
v_{0}\in C^{2}(\Sigma)
has
\|v_{0}\|_{C^{2}(\Sigma)}\leq\psi^{*}
then there is
T\in[1,\infty]
and
v\in C^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\Sigma\times[0,T])
so that:

(1)

v(\cdot,0)=v_{0}(\cdot)
,

(2)

\Gamma(\tau):=\Graph_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,\tau)
is a rescaled mean curvature flow,

(3)

we have

\sup_{\Sigma}|\nabla^{m}v(\cdot,\tau)|\leq C(m,\Sigma)

for all
\tau\in[1,T]
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and

(4)

if
T<\infty
, then it must hold that

\sup_{\Sigma}|v(\cdot,T)|=\psi_{0},

for
\psi_{0}=\psi_{0}(\Sigma)
fixed in Proposition
4.1
.

Proof.

Taking
\psi^{*}=\psi^{*}(\Sigma)
sufficiently small it is easy to see (e.g. using
[
EH91
, Theorem 4.2]
) that the graphical rescaled mean curvature flow starting at
v_{0}
exists and satisfies

(4.4)

\sup_{\Sigma}|v(\cdot,\tau)|\leq\psi_{0},\qquad\sup_{\Sigma}|\nabla_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,\tau)|\leq\psi_{1},

at least for
\tau\in[0,2]
. Choose
T\in(1,\infty]
maximal so that the flow exists and satisfies (
4.4
) for
\tau\in[0,T)
. Property (3) for
\tau<T
follows immediately from Proposition
4.1
. This verifies (1) and (2) (since if
T<\infty
then (3) guarantees the flow extends to
\tau=T
.)

In the case that
T=\infty
, condition (4) is vacuous so the proof is completed. Thus, we must verify (4) when
T<\infty
. If (4) fails, then

\sup_{\Sigma}|v(\cdot,T)|<\psi_{0}.

However, due to Proposition
4.1
, this would would contradict the maximality of
T
, so this case cannot occur. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.3

(Barrier principle for graphical flows)
.

Fix a shrinker
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}
and the corresponding constant
\psi^{*}
from Corollary
4.2
. For
v_{0}\in C^{2}(\Sigma)\setminus\{0\}
with
v_{0}\geq 0
and
\|v_{0}\|_{C^{2}(\Sigma)}\leq\psi^{*}
, let
[0,T]\ni\tau\mapsto v(\cdot,\tau)
denote the function whose graph is a rescaled mean curvature flow as in Corollary
4.2
. Then
1\leq T<\infty
.

In particular,

\sup_{\Sigma}|v(\cdot,T)|=\psi_{0}.

Moreover, if
[0,T]\ni\tau\mapsto\mathcal{M}(\tau)
is a rescaled Brakke flow with
\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)\subset\Omega
for all
\tau\in[0,T]
and

\supp\mathcal{M}(0)\subset\Omega\setminus\{\mathbf{x}+sv_{0}(\mathbf{x})\nu_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x}):\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma,s\in[0,1]\}

then

\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)\subset\Omega\setminus\{\mathbf{x}+sv(\mathbf{x},\tau)\nu_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x}):\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma,s\in[0,1)\}.

Proof.

Assume that
T=\infty
