. First note that
v(\cdot,\tau)>0
for all
\tau\in[0,\infty)
by the Ecker–Huisken maximum principle
[
EH91
, Theorem 4.3]
. Then, Ilmanen’s localized avoidance principle (Theorem
D.1
) applied to
t\mapsto\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
and
t\mapsto\Graph_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,-\log(-t))
implies that for any
R>0
, there is
t_{0}=t_{0}(R)<0
so that
\Graph_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,-\log(-t_{0}))\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0})=\emptyset
. This is a contradiction, when
R
is large. Thus
T<\infty
, so the “blowup” condition

\sup_{\Sigma}|v(\cdot,T)|=\psi_{0}.

follows from (4) in Corollary
4.2
.

The remaining statement (“Moreover…”) follows from Proposition
E.1
.
∎

5.
Existence of a one-sided ancient flow

Fix
n\leq 6
and a shrinker
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}_{n}
with nice stable ends. (Recall that this means that the ends of
\Sigma
are smoothly asymptotic to cones or cylinders and the first eigenfunction of
L
is bounded.) We construct here an ancient shrinker mean convex rescaled Brakke flow inside of
\Omega
converging to
\Sigma
(with multiplicity one) in the ancient past.

Recall that the unit normal to
\Sigma
points into
\Omega
. By Proposition
3.6
, the first eigenfunction
\varphi
(with first eigenvalue
\mu
) of
L
along
\Sigma
has the property that there is
\varepsilon_{0}>0
so that for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, the normal graph of
\varepsilon\varphi
(denoted here by
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
) is a smooth strictly shrinker mean convex hypersurface contained in
\Omega
.

We first construct barriers for the one-sided flow.
16
16

16

These barriers allows us to simplify certain arguments used in
[
CCMS20
]
.

Lemma 5.1

(Global barriers for one-sided flow)
.

There is
M=M(\Sigma)>0
sufficiently large and
\tau_{0}=\tau_{0}(\Sigma)
sufficiently negative so that
Me^{-\mu\tau_{0}}\leq 1/2
and so that defining functions

v_{\pm}(\mathbf{x},\tau):=(e^{-\mu\tau}\pm Me^{-2\mu\tau})\varphi(\mathbf{x}),

the hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{\pm}(\tau):=\Graph_{\Sigma}v_{\pm}(\cdot,\tau)
satisfy:

(-\infty,\tau_{0}]\ni\tau\mapsto\Sigma_{+}(\tau)

is a supersolution
17
17

17

Namely, a smooth solution to rescaled mean curvarure flow cannot make contact with
\Sigma_{+}
from
below
(with respect to the unit normal induced by
\Sigma
).
to rescaled mean curvature flow and

(-\infty,\tau_{0}]\ni\tau\mapsto\Sigma_{-}(\tau)

is a subsolution to rescaled mean curvature flow.

Proof.

We consider
\Sigma_{+}
for simplicity (the argument for
\Sigma_{-}
is essentially identical). We first compute

(\partial_{\tau}-L)v_{+}=-M\mu e^{-2\mu\tau}\varphi=M|\mu|e^{-2\mu\tau}\varphi

By Corollary
A.4
,

(\nu_{\Sigma_{+}}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma})^{-1}(\partial_{\tau}\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma}-\mathbf{H}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma})\cdot\nu_{\Gamma}=(\partial_{\tau}-L)u+E=M|\mu|e^{-2\mu\tau}\varphi+E,

at
\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{x}+v_{+}(\mathbf{x},\tau)\nu_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{x})
for
E=v_{+}E_{1}+E_{2}(\nabla v_{+},\nabla v_{+})
with
E_{1}=O(e^{-\mu\tau}),E_{2}(\cdot,\cdot)=O(1)
uniformly on
\Sigma
as
\tau\to-\infty
. Note that

\displaystyle|E(\mathbf{x})|

\displaystyle\leq v_{+}(\mathbf{x})|E_{1}(\mathbf{x})|+|E_{2}(\nabla v_{+}(\mathbf{x}),\nabla v_{+}(\mathbf{x}))|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}(e^{-\mu\tau}+Me^{-2\mu\tau})\varphi(\mathbf{x})+C(e^{-\mu\tau}+Me^{-2\mu\tau})^{2}|\nabla\varphi(\mathbf{x})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+M^{2}e^{-2\mu\tau})e^{-2\mu\tau}\varphi(\mathbf{x}).

The final estimate uses
\varphi^{-1}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
as proven in part (3) of Lemma
3.6
. Hence,

\displaystyle(\nu_{\Sigma_{+}}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma})^{-1}(\partial_{\tau}\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma}-\mathbf{H}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma})\cdot\nu_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\geq(M|\mu|-C-CM^{2}e^{-2\mu\tau})e^{-2\mu\tau}\varphi

Taking
M
large and then
\tau_{0}
sufficiently negative ensures that
M|\mu|-C-CM^{2}e^{-2\mu\tau}>0
for all
\tau\in(-\infty,\tau_{0})
. This proves the assertion.
∎

The following result is an extension of
[
BW17
, Proposition 4.4]
to allow for asymptotically cylindrical ends.
We will denote
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
the constant from Lemma
3.6
(denoted by
\varepsilon_{0}
there).

Proposition 5.2

(Existence of pre-ancient graphical one-sided flow)
.

There is
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\Sigma)\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}]
so that for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
there is a smooth mean curvature flow
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(t)
for
t\in[-1,-(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})^{1/|\mu|}]
with
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(-1)=\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
and
C=C(\Sigma)
with the following properties:

(1)

the flow
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(t)
remains strictly shrinker mean convex to the inside with the bound

2tH_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(t)}+\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(t)}\geq C^{-1}\varepsilon(1+|\mathbf{x}|^{2}+2n(t+1))^{\mu}

for all
t\in[-1,-(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})^{1/|\mu|}]
,

(2)

the rescaled surface
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(t)
is the normal exponential graph over
\Sigma
of a function
v_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)
with
v_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma\times[-1,-(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})^{1/|\mu|}])
and
\|v_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{0}(\Sigma)}\leq 2\varepsilon_{0}

for
t\in[-1,-(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})^{1/|\mu|}]
,

(3)

\|v_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{3}(\Sigma)}\leq C
for all
t\in[-1,-(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})^{1/|\mu|}]
, and

(4)

the following estimate

\frac{\varepsilon}{2}e^{-\mu\tau}\varphi(\mathbf{x})\leq v_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x},-e^{-\tau})\leq 2\varepsilon e^{-\mu\tau}\varphi(\mathbf{x})

holds for all
(\mathbf{x},\tau)\in\Sigma\times[0,\tfrac{1}{\mu}(\log(\varepsilon)-\log(\varepsilon_{0}))]
.

Proof.

Fix
\psi^{*}=\psi^{*}(\Sigma)
and
\psi_{0}=\psi_{0}(\Sigma)
as in Corollary
4.2
. Choose
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}]
(for
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}
the constant from Lemma
3.6
so that (i)
\varepsilon_{0}\leq\|\varphi\|_{C^{2}(\Sigma)}^{-1}\psi^{*}
, (ii)
\varepsilon_{0}\|\varphi\|_{C^{0}(\Sigma)}(1+M\varepsilon_{0})<\psi_{0}
for
M
fixed in Lemma
5.1
and (iii)
\varepsilon_{0}\leq e^{-\mu\tau_{0}}
, for
\tau_{0}
fixed in Lemma
5.1
.

Condition (i) above and (the first part of) Proposition
4.3
yield
T\in[1,\infty)
and a graphical rescaled mean curvature flow
[0,T]\ni\tau\mapsto v_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\tau)
with
v_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,0)=\varepsilon\varphi(\cdot)
and so that

(5.1)

\sup_{\Sigma}|v_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,T)|=\psi_{0}.

Choose
\bar{\tau}\leq\tau_{0}
so that
e^{-\mu\bar{\tau}}=\varepsilon
(possible by (iii) above). Then we have that

v_{-}(\mathbf{x},\bar{\tau})=\varepsilon(1-M\varepsilon)\varphi(\mathbf{x})\leq\varepsilon\varphi(\mathbf{x})\leq\varepsilon(1+M\varepsilon)\varphi(\mathbf{x})=v_{+}(\mathbf{x},\bar{\tau})

where
v_{\pm}
are defined in Lemma
5.1
. The Ecker–Huisken maximum principle
[
EH91
, Theorem 4.3]
and Lemma
5.1
yields

v_{-}(\mathbf{x},\bar{\tau}+\tau)\leq v_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x},\tau)\leq v_{+}(\mathbf{x},\bar{\tau}+\tau)

for
\tau\leq T_{0}:=\min\{T,\tau_{0}-\bar{\tau}\}
.

We claim that
T_{0}\geq\mu^{-1}\log(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})
. Indeed, by (iii) (and
\mu<0
) we note that

\mu^{-1}\log(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})\leq\tau_{0}-\bar{\tau}.

Thus, if
T_{0}<\mu^{-1}\log(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})
then
T_{0}=T
. Using (
5.1
) and (ii) above, we thus find

\psi_{0}=\sup_{\Sigma}|v_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,T)|\leq\sup|v_{+}(\cdot,\bar{\tau}+\mu^{-1}\log(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0}))|\leq\varepsilon_{0}\|\varphi\|_{C^{0}(\Sigma)}(1+M\varepsilon_{0})<\psi_{0}.

This is a contradiction. The graph of
v_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\tau)
yields the desired mean curvature flow (after rescaling), with the asserted existence time.

The shrinker mean convexity follows from the Ecker–Huisken maximum principle and Lemma
3.6
exactly as in
[
BW17
, Proposition 3.3]
. The remaining properties follow by construction. This finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.3

(Existence of ancient graphical one-sided flow)
.

Fix
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
. Set
\tau_{0}=|\mu|^{-1}\log\varepsilon_{0}
. There is an ancient graphical shrinker mean convex rescaled mean curvature flow
(-\infty,\tau_{0}]\ni\tau\mapsto\Graph_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,\tau)
with
\lambda(\Graph_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,\tau))\leq F(\Sigma)
,
v(\mathbf{x},\tau)>0
for all
(\mathbf{x},\tau)\in\Sigma\times(-\infty,\tau_{0}]
, as well as the following properties for
\tau\in(-\infty,\tau_{0}]
:

(1)

\|v(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{3}(\Sigma)}\leq C
,

(2)

\frac{1}{2}e^{-\mu\tau}\varphi\leq v(\cdot,\tau)\leq 2e^{-\mu\tau}\varphi
,

(3)

\|v(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{k}(\Sigma)}\leq C_{k}e^{-\mu\tau}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and

(4)

|e^{\mu\tau}\langle v(\cdot,\tau),\varphi\rangle_{W}-a|\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}
for some fixed
a>0
.

Proof.

For
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(t)
defined in Proposition
5.2
, we set

S_{\varepsilon}(\tau):=e^{(\tau-\tau(\varepsilon))/2}\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(-e^{-\tau+\tau(\varepsilon)})

for
\tau\in[\tau(\varepsilon),\tau_{0}]
,
\tau_{0}=|\mu|^{-1}\log\varepsilon_{0}
, and
\tau(\varepsilon)=|\mu|^{-1}\log\varepsilon
. Observe that
S_{\varepsilon}(\tau)
is a rescaled mean curvature flow and
S_{\varepsilon}(\tau)
is the graph of
\hat{v}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\tau):=v_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,-e^{-\tau+\tau(\varepsilon)})
, defined in Proposition
5.2
. Property (3) in Proposition
5.2
yields
\|\hat{v}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{3}(\Sigma)}\leq C
for
\tau\in[\tau(\varepsilon),\tau_{0}]
. Similarly, (4) in Proposition
5.2
yields

(5.2)

\frac{1}{2}e^{-\mu\tau}\varphi(\mathbf{x})\leq\hat{v}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x},\tau)\leq 2e^{-\mu\tau}\varphi(\mathbf{x})

for
(\mathbf{x},\tau)\in\Sigma\times[\tau(\varepsilon),\tau_{0}]
.

We can thus find
\varepsilon_{i}\to 0
so that
\hat{v}_{\varepsilon}
converges in
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}(\Sigma\times(-\infty,\tau_{0}])
to
v(\cdot,\tau)
with
\|v(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{3}(\Sigma)}\leq C
for
\tau\in(-\infty,\tau_{0}]
and so that
\Graph_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,\tau)
is rescaled mean curvature flow. This proves property (1). Property (2) follows by passing (
5.2
) to the limit. Property (3) follows from this, combined with interior Schauder estimates applied to the (non-rescaled) mean curvature flow equation.

Finally, to prove (4), we recall that by
A.4
,
v
satisfies

\partial_{\tau}u=Lu+E\,,

where (3) implies that
|E|\leq Ce^{-2\mu\tau}
. Since

\partial_{\tau}\langle v,\varphi\rangle_{W}=-\mu\langle v,\varphi\rangle_{W}+\langle E,\varphi\rangle_{W}\,,

the function
f(\tau):=e^{\mu\tau}\langle v,\varphi\rangle_{W}
satisfies

|\partial_{\tau}f|\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}\,.

Integrating this proves (4) (note that
a>0
by (2)).

The shrinker mean convexity follows from the Ecker–Huisken maximum principle and Lemma
3.6
exactly as in
[
BW17
, Proposition 3.3]
. The remaining properties follow by construction. This finishes the proof.
∎

5.1.
The flow for all times
t<0

In this section we extend the flow constructed previously to all (non-rescaled) times
t<0
. To do so, we must assume that
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}
has nice,
strictly
stable ends. Recall that this means that the first eigenfunction
\varphi
decays to
0
at spatial infinity.

Remark
.

We note that the strictly stable end condition excludes cylinders
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
,
\hat{\Sigma}\in\mathcal{S}_{n-1}
compact. In the exact cylindrical case one can construct the ancient flow coming out of
\hat{\Sigma}
(using the arguments discussed below) and then cross with
\mathbb{R}
. As such, we will not discuss this case further in this section.

For later reference we also note the following basic stability result.

Lemma 5.4

(Stability of cylinders)
.

For every
\delta>0
,
\Lambda>0
there exists a
D\geq\delta^{-1}
such that the following holds. Let
\mathcal{M}
be unit regular integral Brakke flow, such that
\lambda(\mathcal{M})\leq\Lambda
and
\mathcal{M}(-1)\lfloor B_{D}(\mathbf{0})
is a smooth graph over
\hat{\Sigma}_{j}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathbf{x}_{j}}
with the
C^{3}
-norm of the graph function bounded by
D^{-1}
. Then
\mathcal{M}(t)
remains a smooth graph over
(\hat{\Sigma}_{j})_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathbf{x}_{j}}
on
B_{\delta^{-1}}
for all
t\in[-1,-\delta^{2}]
with (rescaled)
C^{3}
-norm bounded by
\delta
.

Proof.

By a direct contradiction argument, this follows from the uniqueness of the evolution of
\hat{\Sigma}_{j}\times\mathbb{R}_{\mathbf{x}_{j}}
.
∎

We recall the definition of the parabolic dilation map

\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R},\qquad\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}:(\mathbf{x},t)\mapsto(\lambda\mathbf{x},\lambda^{2}t)

and also that
\mathfrak{t}(\mathbf{x},t)=t
is the projection onto the time coordinate.

In the following we will consider
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\Sigma)
as in Proposition
5.2

and
t_{0}=-\varepsilon_{0}^{-1/|\mu|}
.

Proposition 5.5

(Existence of a pre-ancient weak one-sided flow)
.

For
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
there exists unit-regular integral Brakke flows (resp. weak set flows
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
) with initial condition
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(-1)=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
(resp.
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}(-1)=K_{\varepsilon}
, the unique closed set with
K_{\varepsilon}\subset\Omega
and
\partial K_{\varepsilon}=\Sigma_{\varepsilon})

with the following properties:

(1)

\partial\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}\setminus\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(-1)\subset\supp\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}

(2)

for
t\in[-1,-(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})^{1/|\mu|}]
we have
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(t)=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(t)
, for
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(t)
the flow constructed in Proposition
5.2
.

Now, assume that
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}
has strictly stable ends and define the dilated flows

\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon^{-1/(2|\mu|)}}(\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon})\text{ resp. }\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon^{-1/(2|\mu|)}}(\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}).

There is a monotonically non-decreasing function
R(t)
on
(-\infty,0)
with
R(t)\equiv 0
for
t\in(-\infty,t_{0}]
independent of
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
so that

(5.3)

\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\varepsilon}(t)\lfloor(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus B_{R(t)})\text{ and }\partial\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{\varepsilon}(t)\cap(\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(t)\setminus B_{R(t)})

are the same, smooth, strictly shrinker mean convex mean curvature flow, which we will denote by
\hat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t)
; moreover
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\hat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t)
can be written as a small
C^{2}
-graph over a subset of
\Sigma
.

Proof.

Approximating
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
by smooth compact hypersurfaces whose level set flow does not fatten, we may use
[
Ilm94
]
to find a sequence of compact unit regular integral Brakke flows
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon,\rho}
and weak set flows
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon,\rho}
satisfying

(5.4)

\supp\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon,\rho}\cap\mathfrak{t}^{-1}((-1,\infty))=\partial\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon,\rho}\cap\mathfrak{t}^{-1}((-1,\infty))

with
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon,\rho}
converging to a Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}
as Brakke flows and
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon,\rho}
converging to a weak set flow
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
in the local Hausdorff sense (the flows
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon},\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
are easily seen to have the asserted initial conditions). See
[
CCMS20
, Proposition 7.3]
for the (standard) details of this construction.

We recall here the argument from
[
CCMS20
, Lemma 7.5]
proving (1). For
X\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}\setminus\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(-1)
, there is
X_{i}\in\partial\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon,\rho_{i}}\setminus\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(-1)=\supp\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon,\rho_{i}}\setminus\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(-1)
converging to
X
. The monotonicity formula guarantees that
X\in\supp\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}
. This proves the first inclusion in (1). For the second inclusion, take
X\in\supp\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}
. The same argument yields
X_{i}\in\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon,\rho_{i}}
with
X_{i}\to X
. Thus
X\in\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
since
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
is closed.

Using pseudolocality, unit-regularity and uniqueness of smooth solutions to mean curvature flow with bounded curvature
[
CY07
, Theorem 1.1]
, it follows that
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(t)=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(t)
as long as
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(t)
has bounded curvature (which holds at least until
t=-(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})^{1/|\mu|}
). Thus the flows satisfy (2) as well.

It thus remains to prove (
5.3
) under the assumption that
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}
has strictly stable ends. We note that

\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}(t_{0})=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor\hat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t_{0})

where
(-t_{0})^{-1/2}\hat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t_{0})=(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})^{-1/2|\mu|}\Sigma_{\varepsilon}((\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})^{1/|\mu|})=\Graph_{\Sigma}v_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})^{1/|\mu|})
where

v_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x},(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})^{1/2|\mu|})\leq 2\varepsilon(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{0})\varphi(\mathbf{x})=2\varepsilon_{0}\varphi(\mathbf{x})

(this follows from (4) in Proposition
5.2
). In particular, the strongly stable end hypothesis (and (3) in Lemma
3.6
) implies that for any
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\hat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t_{0})
,
(-t_{0})^{-1/2}\hat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t_{0})-\mathbf{x}_{i}
converges in
C^{3}_{\textrm{loc}}
(up to passing to a subsequence and applying a fixed rotation) to either
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(on the conical part) or
\hat{\Sigma}_{j}\times\mathbb{R}
(one of the finitely many cylindrical ends of
\Sigma
). In particular, by applying pseudolocality (in the conical case) or stability of cylinders Lemma
5.4
(in the cylindrical case) we find
R(t)
monotonically non-decreasing so that

\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\varepsilon}(t)\lfloor(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus B_{R(t)})

is a smooth mean curvature flow, which we will denote by
\hat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t)
; moreover
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\hat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t)
can be written as a small
C^{2}
-graph over a subset of
\Sigma
. Unit regularity at the level of the approximating flows
\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon,\rho}
guarantees that the same thing holds for
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
.

That
[-\varepsilon^{-1/|\mu|},t_{0}]\mapsto\hat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t)
is strictly shrinker mean convex follows from Proposition
5.2
. Finally, we can use Ilmanen’s localized avoidance principle (cf. Theorem
D.1
) and the Ecker–Huisken maximum principle as in
[
CCMS20
, Corollary 7.11]
to see that
\hat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t)
is weakly shrinker mean convex for all
t\in[-\varepsilon^{-1/|\mu|},0)
. Strict mean convexity then follows from the strong maximum principle and the barriers constructed in property (4) of Proposition
5.2
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark
.

In
[
CCMS20
, §7]
(i.e., for
\Sigma
with only asymptotically conical ends), the estimate
|\mathbf{x}||A|+|\mathbf{x}|^{2}|\nabla A|+|\mathbf{x}|^{3}|\nabla^{2}A|\leq C
for the flow
\hat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}
is proven and used in several places. Such an estimate will not hold for asymptotically cylindrical ends, so in any reference below to
[
CCMS20
, §7]
, such an estimate should be replaced by
|A|+|\nabla A|+|\nabla^{2}A|\leq C
. Fortunately, this does not affect the logic of any of the arguments referred to below.

Proposition 5.6

(Existence of ancient weak flow)
.

Fix
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{n}
. There exists an ancient unit regular integral Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}
and a corresponding weak set flow
\mathcal{K}
with the following properties:

(1)

\partial\mathcal{K}\subset\supp\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{K}
,

(2)

\lambda(\mathcal{M})\leq F(\Sigma)
,

(3)

there is a continuous, monotonically increasing function
R(t)
on
(-\infty,0)
, with
R(t)=0
for
t\in(-\infty,t_{0}]
,
t_{0}<0
, such that

\mathcal{M}(t)\lfloor(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus B_{R(t)}(\mathbf{0}))\text{ and }\partial\mathcal{K}\cap(\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(t)\setminus B_{R(t)}(\mathbf{0}))

are the same, smooth, strictly shrinker mean convex mean curvature flow, which we will denote by
\Sigma(t)
; moreover
(-t)^{-1/2}\Sigma(t)
can be written as a small
C^{3}
-graph over a subset of
\Sigma
,

(4)

for
t\in(-\infty,t_{0}]
,
(-t)^{-1/2}\Sigma(t)=\Graph_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,\tau)
,
t=-e^{-\tau}
, for
v
as in Proposition
5.3
.

Proof.

The proof of Proposition
5.3
yields
\varepsilon_{i}\to 0
so that
[-\varepsilon^{1/|\mu|},t_{0}]\ni t\mapsto\hat{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}(t)
converges in
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}
to
(-\infty,t_{0}]\ni t\mapsto(-t)^{-1/2}\Graph_{\Sigma}v(\cdot,-\log(-t)):=\Sigma(t)
for
v
as in Proposition
5.3
. Proposition
5.5
allows us to find
\Sigma(t)
a flow in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus B_{R(t)}(\mathbf{0})
so that (3) is satisfied.
18
18

18

A priori,
\Sigma(t)
must only be weakly shrinker mean convex, but by Proposition
5.3
it is strictly shrinker mean convex for
t\in(-\infty,t_{0}]
so the strong maximum principle guarantees strict shrinker mean convexity of
\Sigma(t)
.
Letting the weak flows
\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\varepsilon_{i}},\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{\varepsilon_{i}}
pass to a subsequential limit we find an ancient unit regular integral Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}
and weak set flow
\mathcal{K}
which (thanks to Proposition
5.5
) satisfy the various assertions.
∎

We can now combine the previous results with Ilmanen’s localised avoidance principle to extend the strict shrinker mean convexity to the whole flow for
t<0
(cf.
[
CCMS20
, Theorem 7.17]
). Recall that
\mathcal{K}^{\circ}
denotes the interior of
\mathcal{K}
.

Proposition 5.7

(Basic properties of ancient weak flow I)
.

The ancient unit regular integral Brakke flow and the corresponding weak set flow
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{K})
from the previous proposition have the following properties: let
\check{\mathcal{K}}=\mathcal{K}\cap\mathfrak{t}^{-1}((-\infty,0))
, then

(1)

we have
d((\mathbf{0},0),\check{\mathcal{K}})>0
,

(2)

for
\lambda>1
we have
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(\check{\mathcal{K}})\subset\check{\mathcal{K}}^{\circ}
and
\supp\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(\check{\mathcal{K}})=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Since the flow
t\mapsto\Sigma(t)
is strictly shrinker mean convex we have that
\Sigma(t)\cap\Sigma=\emptyset
for all
t<0
. Thus, using Ilmanen’s localised avoidance principle, Theorem
D.1
, we see that
\mathcal{M}(t)\cap\Sigma=\emptyset
for all
t<0
. Applying Ilmanen’s localised avoidance principle another time, we thus see that
d((\mathbf{0},0),\supp\mathcal{M})>0
, which by Proposition
5.6
implies
(1)
.

To prove (2), we note that strict shrinker mean convexity of the exterior flow
\Sigma(t)
guarantees that for
\lambda>1
,
\supp\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(\check{\mathcal{K}})
are disjoint outside of a set
D
in space-time which has
D\cap\mathfrak{t}^{-1}([a,b])
compact for any
a<b<0
. Thus, we may apply Ilmanen’s localized avoidance principle, Theorem
D.1
, to show that
\supp\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(\check{\mathcal{K}})
are indeed disjoint. Using again Proposition
5.6
this completes the proof of (2).
∎

We can replace
[
CCMS20
, Theorem 7.17]
with Proposition
5.6
and Proposition
5.7
for all the results in
[
CCMS20
, Section 8.1]
to carry over. This yields the following result, replacing
[
CCMS20
, Corollary 8.12]
.
19
19

19

Property (6) follows from
[
CM16
, Corollary 1.4]
.

Proposition 5.8

(Basic properties of ancient weak flow II)
.

For
n\leq 6
, fix
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{n}
. The flows
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{K})
constructed in Proposition
5.6
have the following properties for
t<0
:

(1)

\mathcal{M}(t)=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
,

(2)

\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\subset\sqrt{-t}\Omega
for all
t<0
,

(3)

\sing\mathcal{M}\cap\{t<0\}
has parabolic Hausdorff dimension
\leq n-1
and for
t<0
,
\sing\mathcal{M}(t)
has spatial Hausdorff dimension
\leq n-1
,

(4)

any limit flow at
X=(\mathbf{x},t)
with
t<0
is weakly convex on the regular part and all tangent flows are multiplicity one generalized cylinders,

(5)

any singular point has a (strict) mean-convex neighborhood, and

(6)

if
n=3,4
,
\mathfrak{t}(\sing\mathcal{M})\cap\{t<0\}
has measure zero.

5.2.
Conditional uniqueness results

Proposition
5.8
implies the following conditional uniqueness result.

Proposition 5.9

(Conditional uniqueness I)
.

For
n\leq 6
, fix
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{n}
. Let
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{K})
be as constructed in Proposition
5.6
. Let
(\check{\mathcal{M}}(t))_{-\infty<t<\infty}
be an ancient unit-regular integral Brakke flow such that there is
T<0
so that

(5.5)

\check{\mathcal{M}}(t)=\mathcal{M}(t)\text{ for all }t\leq T.

Then
\check{\mathcal{M}}(t)=\mathcal{M}(t)
for
t<0
.

Proof.

We can assume that

T<0
is sufficiently negative so that for
t\leq T
,
\mathcal{M}(t)=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor\Sigma(t)
, where
\Sigma(t)
is the smooth flow from Proposition
5.6
(3).

Below, we consider only times
t<0
. As in
[
CCMS20
, Proposition 7.10]
, Ilmanen’s localized avoidance principle (Theorem
D.1
) combined with Ecker–Huisken’s maximum principle at infinity
[
CCMS20
, Theorem D.1]
, we see that
(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{t}
is disjoint from
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(\supp\mathcal{M})
for
\lambda\not=1
. This implies that,
(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{t}\subset\supp\mathcal{M}
.

Finally, since
\reg\mathcal{M}
is connected for by (5) in Proposition
5.8
and
[
CCMS20
, Corollary F.5]
, we see that
\check{\mathcal{M}}(t)=\mathcal{M}(t)
in
(\sing\mathcal{M})^{c}
(using the unit-regularity of
\mathcal{M}
and
\check{\mathcal{M}}
). This completes the proof.
∎

Under a global graphicality assumption, we can also ensure uniqueness. Note that this result holds with just the (non-strict) stable end hypothesis.

Proposition 5.10

(Conditional uniqueness II)
.

For
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
, consider two ancient rescaled mean curvature flows
\Sigma_{i}(\tau)
for
\tau\in(-\infty,\tau_{0}),i=1,2
, such that
\Sigma_{i}(\tau)=\Graph_{\Sigma}u_{i}(\cdot,\tau)
. There exists
\eta=\eta(\Sigma)>0
such that if

(5.6)

\|u_{i}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{3}(\Sigma)}\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}

for
\tau\in(-\infty,\tau_{0})
and
i=1,2
as well as

\lim_{\tau\to-\infty}e^{\mu\tau}\|w\|_{W}=0\,,

where
w=u_{1}-u_{2}
, then
w\equiv 0
.

Proof.

The proof follows similarly as the proof of
[
CCMS20
, Corollary 5.2]
. Denoting

w:=u_{1}-u_{2},\ E^{w}:=E(u_{1})-E(u_{2})

we have

\big(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\tau}-L\big)w=E^{w}\,.

From Lemma
A.7
we see that we can write

E^{w}=wF+\nabla_{\Sigma}w\cdot\mathbf{F}+\nabla^{2}_{\Sigma}w\cdot\mathcal{F}\,.

We take the
L^{2}_{W}
dot product of (
A.13
) with
w
to find

\displaystyle\left\langle E^{w},w\right\rangle_{W}

\displaystyle=\left\langle wF,w\right\rangle_{W}+\left\langle\nabla_{\Sigma}w\cdot\mathbf{F},w\right\rangle_{W}+\left\langle\nabla^{2}_{\Sigma}w\cdot\mathcal{F},w\right\rangle_{W}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}\|w\|_{W,1}^{2}+\left\langle\nabla^{2}_{\Sigma}w\cdot\mathcal{F},w\right\rangle_{W},

where we recall that
\|f\|_{W,1}^{2}=\|f\|_{W}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\Sigma}f\|_{W}^{2}
is the weighted Sobolev norm. To handle the remaining term, we integrate by parts. We obtain

\left\langle\nabla^{2}_{\Sigma}w\cdot\mathcal{F},w\right\rangle_{W}=O(\|w\|_{W,1}^{2}\|\mathcal{F}\|_{C^{1}})+\int_{\Sigma}w\,(\mathbf{x}^{T}\otimes\nabla_{\Sigma}w)\cdot\mathcal{F}\,e^{-\tfrac{|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}{4}}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}.

Thanks to the estimate
\|\mathcal{F}\|_{C^{1}}\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}
, the first term is as desired. Again by Lemma
A.7
we see that

\left|\int_{\Sigma}w\,(\mathbf{x}^{T}\otimes\nabla_{\Sigma}w)\cdot\mathcal{F}\,e^{-\tfrac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}d\mathcal{H}^{n}\right|\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}\|w\|_{W,1}^{2}\,,

which combining with the previous estimates implies

|\langle w(\cdot,\tau),E^{w}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{W}|\leq C_{2}e^{-\mu\tau}\|w(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{W,1},\;\tau\leq\tau_{0},

This replaces
[
CCMS20
, (5.12)]
. The rest of the argument is identical to the remainder of the proof of
[
CCMS20
, Corollary 5.2]
.
∎

6.
Barriers along the cylindrical ends

In this section we consider
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}
. (Recall that this means that
\Sigma=\partial\Omega
and the ends of
\Sigma
are either asymptotic to smooth cones or cylinders over smooth compact shrinkers.) Angenent–Daskalopoulos–Šešum have recently
[
ADS19
]
, constructed a self-shrinker foliation inside and outside of the end of a round cylinder
\Sigma=\mathbb{S}^{n-1}(\sqrt{2(n-1)})\times\mathbb{R}
using ODE techniques (see also
[
KM14
]
). This foliation plays an important role in the study of ancient solutions with cylindrical tangent flow at
t=-\infty
in
[
ADS19
,
ADS20
,
BC19
,
BC21
,
CHH21
,
CHH22
,
CHHW22
]
. The goal of this section is to (partially) generalize this to the case of shrinkers with nice ends.

6.1.
Setup and statement of barrier construction

After a rotation, we will assume that
\Sigma
has a cylindrical end in the direction
\mathbf{e}=(0,\dots,0,1)
modeled on
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
. More precisely, we consider
\hat{\Sigma}^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
a smoothly embedded closed self-shrinker, so that the surfaces
\Sigma-s\mathbf{e}
converge smoothly on compact sets to
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
as
s\to\infty
. We will use
({\bm{\omega}},z)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
as coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. We assume that
\rho>0
is chosen such that
\hat{\Sigma}\subset B_{\rho}(\mathbf{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Fix
\hat{\Omega}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
so that
\partial\hat{\Omega}=\hat{\Sigma}
and
\Omega-s\mathbf{e}
converges to
\hat{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}
as
s\to\infty
. Set
\Omega_{Z}:=\Omega\cap\{z\geq Z\}
, and
\Sigma_{Z}=\Sigma\cap\{z\geq Z\}
. For
\delta>0
, we define

(6.1)

\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}:=\{{\bm{\omega}}+\delta\hat{\varphi}({\bm{\omega}})\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}}({\bm{\omega}}):{\bm{\omega}}\in\hat{\Sigma}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Let
\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}\subset\hat{\Omega}
denote the region (possibly unbounded) to the “inside” of
\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}
(as determined by the inwards pointing unit normal). Note that
\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}
is smooth and strictly shrinker mean convex (to the inside) and
F(\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta})<F(\hat{\Sigma})
for
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
, where
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(\hat{\Sigma})
.

By Lemma
3.2
, there is
\bar{Z}=\bar{Z}(\Sigma)
large and a smooth function
\tilde{v}:\hat{\Sigma}\times\{z\geq\bar{Z}\}\to\mathbb{R}
so that

(6.2)

\Sigma\cap(B_{2\rho}(\mathbf{0})\times[\bar{Z},\infty))=\{({\bm{\omega}},z)+\tilde{v}({\bm{\omega}},z)\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}}({\bm{\omega}}):({\bm{\omega}},z)\in\hat{\Sigma}\times[\bar{Z},\infty)\}\,.

We have
|\nabla^{k}_{\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}}\tilde{v}|=o(1)
as
z\to\infty
for all
k\geq 0
. Fix
\hat{\mu}<0
the first eigenvalue of
L_{\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}}
.

For
Z_{0}\geq\bar{Z}
we assume there is
[-\infty,T_{0}]\ni\tau\mapsto\tilde{v}_{\tau}\in C_{\textrm{loc}}^{\infty}(\hat{\Sigma}\times[Z_{0},\infty))
so that

(6.3)

\|\tilde{v}_{\tau}\|_{C^{3};\hat{\Sigma}\times[Z_{0},\infty)}=o(1)\textrm{ as $\tau\to-\infty$},

and so that

\Graph_{\hat{\Sigma}\times[Z_{0},\infty)}\tilde{v}_{\tau}(\cdot)\textrm{ is a rescaled mean curvature flow}.

(An important special case is
\tilde{v}_{\tau}\equiv\tilde{v}
, so
\Graph_{\hat{\Sigma}\times[Z_{0},\infty)}\tilde{v}_{\tau}(\cdot)
is the chosen end of
\Sigma
, but we will also consider the case that
\tilde{v}_{\tau}
is the end of the ancient flow constructed in Proposition
5.3
.)

Taking
Z_{0}
larger if necessary we can assume that there is
\vartheta_{0}>0
so that

(6.4)

\Sigma\cap\{z>Z_{0}\}\cap\{t\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0}),d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{e})<\vartheta_{0},t>0\}=\Sigma\cap(B_{2\rho}(\mathbf{0})\times(Z_{0},\infty)).

We will use this later to separate the chosen cylindrical ends from all other ends.

Theorem 6.1

(Barriers along cylindrical ends)
.

Consider the setup described above. There is
Z_{1}\geq Z_{0}
,
T_{1}\leq T_{0}
,
\alpha>2|\hat{\mu}|
,
\eta_{0}>0
, and
h_{0}>0
so that for
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
, there is
\vartheta=\vartheta(\eta)\in(0,\vartheta_{0})
with the following properties. For
T_{2}\leq T_{1}
, suppose that that
(-\infty,T_{2}]\ni\tau\mapsto\mathcal{M}(\tau)
is a rescaled Brakke flow in
\Omega\cap\{z>Z_{1}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
so that

(1)

for all
\tau\in(-\infty,T_{2}]
, it holds that

\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)\cap(B_{2\rho}(\mathbf{0})\times[Z_{1},2Z_{1}])\subset\\
\{({\bm{\omega}},z)+(\tilde{v}_{\tau}({\bm{\omega}},z)+s)\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}}({\bm{\omega}}):z\in[Z_{1},2Z_{1}],s\in(0,\eta z^{\alpha})\},

(2)

there is some
r\geq 0
so that

\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)\subset B_{r}(\mathbf{0})\cup\{tx:\mathbf{x}\in\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0}),d(\mathbf{e},\mathbf{x})<\vartheta,t\geq 0\}

for all
\tau\in(-\infty,T_{2}]
,
and

(3)

\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)
converges in the local Hausdorff sense to
\Sigma\cap(B_{2\rho}(\mathbf{0})\times[Z_{1},\infty))
as
\tau\to-\infty
.

Then, for all
\tau\in(-\infty,T_{2}]
it holds that

\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)\cap\{Z_{1}<z<\eta^{-\kappa}\}\subset\\
\{({\bm{\omega}},z)+(\tilde{v}_{\tau}({\bm{\omega}},z)+s)\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}}({\bm{\omega}}):z\in[Z_{1},\eta^{-\kappa}],s\in[0,\eta z^{\alpha})\},

for
\kappa=(1+\alpha)^{-1}\in(0,1)
.

The construction of these barriers will follow by welding a barrier (constructed using the linearized shrinker equation) that covers a long portion of the end of
\Sigma
to a “translator” that approximates a one-sided rescaled mean curvature flow coming out of
\hat{\Sigma}
. We will prove Theorem
6.1
in Section
6.4
after constructing these pieces.

6.2.
Translator barriers

As we recall in Appendix
C
, Hershkovits–White have recently defined a notion of Brakke flow with an ambient vector field (cf.
[
HW18
, Section 13]
). In particular, a rescaled mean curvature flow is a
\frac{\mathbf{x}}{2}
-flow in their framework. Below, we will consider a related object, a
\frac{{\bm{\omega}}}{2}
-flow. These arise in the elliptic regularization construction of rescaled mean curvature flow.

Lemma 6.2

(Weighted area estimates for
\mathbf{X}
-translators)
.

Consider a compact Riemannian manifold
(M^{n},g)
, a gradient vector field
\mathbf{X}=\nabla f
on
M
, and a hypersurface
\Gamma^{n}\subset M\times[0,\infty)
so that
\mathbf{H}+\mathbf{X}^{\perp}+\lambda\mathbf{e}^{\perp}=0
, where
\mathbf{e}=\partial_{z}
is the unit vector field on
M\times\mathbb{R}
in the
\mathbb{R}
direction. Assume that
\Gamma\cap M\times\{0\}=\partial\Gamma
. For any
0\leq a<b
so that
\Gamma
intersects
\{z\in\{a,b\}\}
transversely, we have

\int_{\Gamma\cap\{a\leq z\leq b\}}e^{-f}\leq(b-a+\lambda^{-1})\int_{\partial\Gamma}e^{-f}.

and

\int_{\Gamma(a,b)}|\mathbf{e}^{\perp}|_{g}^{2}e^{-f}\leq\lambda^{-1}\int_{\partial\Gamma}e^{-f}.

Proof.

Set
\Gamma(a,b):=\Gamma\cap\{a\leq z\leq b\}
and
\Gamma(s)=\Gamma\cap\{z=s\}
. By Sard’s theorem,
\Gamma(a,b)
is a smooth hypersurface with boundary for a.e.
0<a<b
. We now follow the proof of local mass bounds for the translators obtained in elliptic regularization
[
Ilm94
, §5]
(see also
[
Whi15
, §9.3]
).

We will abuse notation slightly and write
g
for the product metric on
(M,g)\times\mathbb{R}
. Then, for
a<b
with
\Gamma
transversal to
\{z=a\}\cup\{z=b\}
, we find

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma(a,b)}g(-\mathbf{X}^{T},\mathbf{e})e^{-f}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma(a,b)}\Div_{\Gamma,g}(e^{-f}\mathbf{e})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma(a,b)}g(-\mathbf{H},\mathbf{e})e^{-f}+\int_{\Gamma(b)}|\mathbf{e}^{T}|_{g}e^{-f}-\int_{\Gamma(a)}|\mathbf{e}^{T}|_{g}e^{-f}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma(a,b)}g(\mathbf{X}^{\perp}+\lambda\mathbf{e}^{\perp},\mathbf{e})e^{-f}+\int_{\Gamma(b)}|\mathbf{e}^{T}|_{g}e^{-f}-\int_{\Gamma(a)}|\mathbf{e}^{T}|_{g}e^{-f}.

Observe that

g(\mathbf{X}^{\perp}+\mathbf{X}^{T},e)=g(\mathbf{X},e)=0,

so, we find

(6.5)

\int_{\Gamma(a)}|\mathbf{e}^{T}|_{g}e^{-f}=\int_{\Gamma(a,b)}\lambda|\mathbf{e}^{\perp}|_{g}^{2}e^{-f}+\int_{\Gamma(b)}|\mathbf{e}^{T}|_{g}e^{-f}.

In particular
s\mapsto\int_{\Gamma(s)}|\mathbf{e}^{T}|_{g}e^{-f}
is non-increasing so

\int_{\Gamma(s)}|\mathbf{e}^{T}|e^{-f}\leq\int_{\partial\Gamma}e^{-f}

for
s>0
so that
\Gamma
intersects
\{z=s\}
transversely. Combined with (
6.5
) we find

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma(a,b)}|\mathbf{e}^{\perp}|_{g}^{2}e^{-f}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\int_{\Gamma(a,b)}\lambda|\mathbf{e}^{\perp}|^{2}_{g}e^{-f}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{-1}\int_{\Gamma(a)}|\mathbf{e}^{T}|_{g}e^{-f}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{-1}\int_{\partial\Gamma}e^{-f}

proving the second equation. Similarly,

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma(a,b)}e^{-f}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma(a,b)}|\mathbf{e}^{\perp}|_{g}^{2}e^{-f}+\int_{\Gamma(a,b)}|\mathbf{e}^{T}|_{g}^{2}e^{-f}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{-1}\int_{\partial\Gamma}e^{-f}+\int_{\Gamma(a,b)}|\mathbf{e}^{T}|_{g}^{2}e^{-f}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\int_{\partial\Gamma}e^{-f}+\int_{a}^{b}\int_{\Gamma(s)}|\mathbf{e}^{T}|_{g}e^{-f}

\displaystyle\leq(b-a+\lambda^{-1})\int_{\partial\Gamma}e^{-f},

proving the first equation. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.3

(Existence of translators)
.

For
\lambda>0
and
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
, there exists a smooth hypersurface
\Gamma_{\delta}^{\lambda}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
\partial\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\delta}=\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}\times\{0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
so that
\mathring{\Gamma}_{\delta}^{\lambda}\subset\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}\times[0,\infty)
and the mean curvature of
\Gamma_{\delta}^{\lambda}
satisfies

\mathbf{H}+\tfrac{{\bm{\omega}}^{\perp}}{2}+\lambda\mathbf{e}^{\perp}=0.

The hypersurfaces
\mathring{\Gamma}^{\lambda}_{\delta}
are graphical over
\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}\times\{0\}
. Furthermore, the hypersurfaces

\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\delta}(t):=\Gamma_{\delta}^{\lambda}-\lambda t\mathbf{e}.

have normal velocity
\mathbf{H}+\tfrac{{\bm{\omega}}^{\perp}}{2}
and are thus
\tfrac{{\bm{\omega}}}{2}
-flows. As
\lambda\to\infty
, the flows converge in the sense of Brakke
\tfrac{{\bm{\omega}}}{2}
-flows to
\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor(\partial^{*}\Omega_{\delta}(t)\times\mathbb{R})
where
\Omega_{\delta}(t)
is the interior of the rescaled mean curvature flow of
\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}
.

Proof.

We consider the case that
\hat{\Omega}
is unbounded (the bounded case is similar but easier). For
R_{j}\to\infty
, consider
(M,g_{j})
where
M=\mathbb{S}^{n}
and
g_{j}
contains a region isometric to
B_{2R_{j}}(\mathbf{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We assume that
\hat{\Sigma}\subset B_{R_{j}}(\mathbf{0})
. Choose a gradient vector field
\mathbf{X}_{j}
on
(M,g)
so that
\mathbf{X}_{j}=\tfrac{1}{2}{\bm{\omega}}
on the region isometric to
B_{R_{j}}(\mathbf{0})
(recalling that
{\bm{\omega}}
is the coordinate on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
). Note that
(M,g_{j},\mathbf{X}_{j},\mathbf{0})
converges in a pointed sense to
(\mathbb{R}^{n},g_{\mathbb{R}^{n}},\tfrac{1}{2}{\bm{\omega}},\mathbf{0})
.

Consider the region
\hat{\Omega}_{\delta,j}\subset M
that agrees with
\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}
in the
B_{R_{j}}(\mathbf{0})
region and
\partial\hat{\Omega}_{\delta,j}=\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}
and currents
\Gamma
with support in the closure of
\hat{\Omega}_{\delta,j}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\partial\Gamma=\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}\times\{0\}
. Note that by construction,
\hat{\Omega}_{\delta,j}
is strictly
\mathbf{X}_{j}
-mean convex to the inside and thus
\partial\hat{\Omega}_{\delta,j}\times\mathbb{R}
acts as barrier for constrained minimization. Hence, by modifying the arguments used in elliptic regularization to
\mathbf{X}
-flows (cf.
[
ES91
,
CGG91
,
Ilm93
,
Ilm94
,
HH18
,
HW18
,
HW19
]
) for each
\lambda>0
, there exists a smooth hypersurface
\Gamma_{j}\subset M\times[0,\infty)
with (i)
\partial\Gamma_{j}=\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}\times\{0\}
, (ii)
\Gamma_{j}
is graphical over
\hat{\Omega}_{\delta,j}\times\{0\}
and (iii)
\Gamma_{j}
satisfies the
\mathbf{X}_{j}
-translator equation
\mathbf{H}+\mathbf{X}^{\perp}_{j}+\lambda\mathbf{e}^{\perp}=0
.

Choose
f_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M)
so that
\nabla f_{j}=\mathbf{X}_{j}
and
f_{j}=\tfrac{1}{4}|{\bm{\omega}}|^{2}
in
B_{R_{j}}(\mathbf{0})
. Lemma
6.2
implies that for almost every
a<b
, it holds that

\int_{\Gamma_{j}\cap\{a\leq z\leq b\}}e^{-f_{j}}d\mu_{g_{j}}\leq(b-a+\lambda^{-1})\int_{\partial\Gamma}e^{-\frac{|{\bm{\omega}}|^{2}}{4}}d\mu_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}.

In particular, for all
R>0
, it holds that

\limsup_{j\to\infty}|\Gamma_{j}\cap(B_{R}(\mathbf{0})\times\{0\leq z\leq R\})|_{g_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}<\infty.

We can now pass to a subsequential limit as
j\to\infty
by following the arguments in
[
HIMW19
, Appendix]
(one should replace convexity in
[
HIMW19
, Appendix]
by the fact that the region
\hat{\Omega}_{j}
is mean convex with respect to the metric
e^{-2f_{j}/n}g^{(j)}
and that
\Gamma_{j}
is a constrained minimizer in
\hat{\Omega}_{\delta,j}\times\mathbb{R}
) but otherwise the argument is unchanged; the Frankel property for self shrinkers (see for example
[
CCMS20
, Corollary C.4]
) prevents cylinders from forming as in
[
HIMW19
, Theorem 12.2(2)]
). This yields the smooth hypersurface
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\delta}
graphical over
\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}\times\{0\}
with
\mathbf{H}+\frac{{\bm{\omega}}^{\perp}}{2}+\lambda\mathbf{e}^{\perp}=0
.

Using Lemma
6.2
, the remaining assertions follow exactly as in the construction of (mean convex) mean curvature flow via elliptic regularization (cf.
[
Ilm94
, §8]
or
[
Whi15
, Theorem 9.11]
). Indeed,
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\delta}-\lambda t\mathbf{e}
defines an integral unit regular Brakke
\frac{{\bm{\omega}}}{2}
-flow and using the local area estimates that follow from Lemma
6.2
, we can take a subsequential limit as
\lambda\to\infty
. Since
{\bm{\omega}}
is
z
-translation invariant, it is easy to show that the limit is of the form
\mu(t)\times\mathbb{R}
where
\mu(t)
is an integral unit regular Brakke
\frac{{\bm{\omega}}}{2}
-flow on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Finally, writing
\pi:\mathbb{S}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{S}^{n}
for the projection to
\mathbb{S}^{n}
, we compute

\displaystyle\int_{\pi(\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\delta,j}\cap\{0<z<b\})}e^{-f_{j}}d\mu_{g^{(j)}}\leq\int_{\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\delta,j}\cap\{0<z<b\}}|\mathbf{e}^{\perp}|_{g^{(j)}}e^{-f_{j}}d\mu_{g^{(j)}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\delta,j}\cap\{0<z<b\}}|\mathbf{e}^{\perp}|_{g^{(j)}}^{2}e^{-f_{j}}d\mu_{g^{(j)}}\right)^{1/2}(b+\lambda^{-1})^{1/2}\left(\int_{\partial\hat{\Omega}_{\delta,j}}e^{-f_{j}}d\mu_{g^{(j)}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{-1/2}(b+\lambda^{-1})^{1/2}\left(\int_{\partial\hat{\Omega}_{\delta,j}}e^{-f_{j}}d\mu_{g^{(j)}}\right).

Sending
j\to\infty
and then
\lambda\to\infty
, we can follow
[
Whi15
, Theorem 9.11]
or
[
Ilm94
, 8.11 Slice Convergence]
to find that
\mu(0)=\mathcal{H}^{n-1}\lfloor\partial^{*}\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}
. Since
\partial^{*}\Omega_{\delta}
is
\tfrac{{\bm{\omega}}}{2}
-mean convex to the inside, any integral unit regular Brakke
\tfrac{{\bm{\omega}}}{2}
-flow starting at
\partial^{*}\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}
agrees with the rescaled mean curvature flow of
\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}
.
∎

Lemma 6.4

(Linear growth of translators)
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
and
\lambda>\tfrac{1}{2}Z_{0}
sufficiently large so that
\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}\times[2\lambda,\infty)\subset\Omega
.
Then, there is
\vartheta=\vartheta(\lambda,\delta)>0
,
R=R(\lambda,\delta)>0
so that

(\Gamma_{\delta}^{\lambda}+2\lambda\mathbf{e})\cap\{t\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0}),d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{e})<\vartheta,t>R\}=\emptyset

Proof.

We begin by assuming that
\hat{\Omega}
is unbounded. (We will consider the easier case when
\hat{\Omega}
is bounded below.)

Note that the shrinker mean curvature of
\Gamma_{\delta}^{\lambda}+2\lambda\mathbf{e}
satisfies

(6.6)

\mathbf{H}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{\perp}=(\tfrac{1}{2}z-\lambda)\mathbf{e}^{\perp}

at
({\bm{\omega}},z)\in\Gamma_{\delta}^{\lambda}+2\lambda\mathbf{e}
and such a point must have
z\geq 2\lambda
. Consider the open set
Q\subset\{z>2\lambda\}
with

\partial Q=(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\hat{\Omega}_{\delta})\times\{2\lambda\}\cup(\Gamma_{\delta}^{\lambda}+2\lambda\mathbf{e}).

Note that
Q
is weakly shrinker mean convex to the inside in the sense of Meeks–Yau
[
MY82
, §1]
. This is obvious along the planar boundary, while along the translator portion of the boundary we can use (
6.6
) and the fact that
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\delta}
is graphical over
\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}\times\{0\}
as proven in Lemma
6.3
.

Following
[
Bre16
, Proposition 12]
we can choose a sequence of Riemannian metrics
g_{k}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
converging in
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
to
e^{-\frac{|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}{2n}}g_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}
so that
Q\cap B_{2k}(\mathbf{0})
is mean convex with respect to
g_{k}
for
k
large and
\Sigma\cap Q\cap B_{2k}(\mathbf{0})
is a minimal surface with respect to
g_{k}
. Write
A_{k}:=(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus\overline{\Omega})\cap Q\cap B_{2k}(\mathbf{0})
for the “inside” of
\Sigma\cap Q\cap B_{2k}(\mathbf{0})
. Choose a Caccioppoli set
B_{k}
in
Q\cap B_{2k}(\mathbf{0})
with (i)
A_{k}\subset B_{k}
, (ii)
\overline{B}_{k}\cap\partial(Q\cap B_{2k}(\mathbf{0}))=\overline{A}_{k}\cap\partial(Q\cap B_{2k}(\mathbf{0}))
and (iii)
B_{k}
minimizes
g_{k}
-perimeter among Caccioppoli sets satisfying (i) and (ii). Set
\Upsilon_{k}:=\partial^{*}B_{k}
.

Taking a subsequence, we can thus find
\Upsilon=\partial^{*}B
for
B\subset Q
locally finite perimeter with
\overline{B}\cap\{z=\lambda\}=(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus\Omega)\cap\{z=\lambda\}
, so that
\Upsilon
has at worst a co-dimension
7
singular set. It is thus straightforward to prove that
t\mapsto\sqrt{-t}\Upsilon
is a (integral) Brakke flow. Recall that
\sqrt{0}\Upsilon
is a conical closed set (not necessarily smooth) and satisfies

\sqrt{0}\Upsilon\cap\mathbf{e}[1,\infty)=\emptyset

by Ilmanen’s avoidance principle (Theorem
D.1
) applied to the disjoint flows
\sqrt{-t}\Upsilon
and
\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
. In particular, there is
\vartheta_{1}
so that

\sqrt{0}\Upsilon\cap\{t\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0}),d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{e})<\vartheta_{1},t>0\}=\emptyset.

This is easily seen to imply the assertion (when
\hat{\Omega}
is unbounded). In the bounded case, a similar proof works with the conclusion that
\Upsilon
(and thus
\Gamma
) is bounded. This completes the proof.
∎

6.3.
Long barriers

We continue with the setup of Theorem
6.1
.

For constants
h_{0}>0
to be fixed small below and
\alpha>\max\{2|\hat{\mu}|,Z_{0}\}
to be fixed large below (depending on
\Sigma
and the function
\tau\mapsto\tilde{v}_{\tau}(\cdot)
), for any
\eta\in(0,\alpha^{-\alpha}h_{0})
fixed we consider the function

U({\bm{\omega}},z)=\eta z^{\alpha}\hat{\varphi}({\bm{\omega}}).

Define
R=R(h_{0},\eta,\alpha)>\alpha
so that
\eta R^{\alpha}=h_{0}
. Then define

(-\infty,T_{1}]\ni\tau\mapsto\Xi_{\tau}:=\{({\bm{\omega}},z)+(\tilde{v}_{\tau}({\bm{\omega}},z)+U({\bm{\omega}},z))\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}}({\bm{\omega}}):({\bm{\omega}},z)\in\hat{\Sigma}\times[\alpha,R]\}.

As long as
T_{1}\leq T_{0}
is sufficiently negative,
h_{0}
is small, and
\alpha
is large (depending only on
\Sigma
and
\tilde{v}_{\tau}
), we see that
\Xi_{\tau}
is a smooth hypersurface (with boundary) contained in
\Omega
. We will write
\nu_{\Xi_{\tau}}
for the “upwards pointing” unit normal, i.e.,
\nu_{\Xi_{\tau}}\cdot\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}}>0
. Define

v_{\Xi_{\tau}}=(\nu_{\Xi_{\tau}}\cdot\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}})^{-1}

along
\hat{\Sigma}\times[\alpha,R]
, where
\nu_{\Xi_{\tau}}
is evaluated at
({\bm{\omega}},z)+(\tilde{v}_{\tau}({\bm{\omega}},z)+U({\bm{\omega}},z))\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}}({\bm{\omega}})
, for
({\bm{\omega}},z)\in\hat{\Sigma}\times[\alpha,R]
, as usual. Note that the normal speed of
\Xi_{\tau}
is given by

v_{\Xi_{\tau}}^{-1}(\partial_{\tau}\tilde{v}_{\tau})

and the scalar (rescaled) mean curvature satisfies

\mathbf{H}_{\Xi_{\tau}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{\perp}=(H_{\Xi_{\tau}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Xi_{\tau}})\nu_{\Xi_{\tau}}.

As such, the next proposition says that
\tau\mapsto\Xi_{\tau}
is a barrier for rescaled mean curvature flows pinched between
\Xi_{\tau}
and
\Sigma
.

Proposition 6.5

(Verifying long barrier property)
.

There is
T_{1}^{\prime}\leq T_{0}
,
h_{0}
small, and
\alpha_{0}>\max\{2|\hat{\mu}|,Z_{0}\}
large so that for
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
and
\eta\in(0,\alpha^{-\alpha}h_{0})
, it holds that

v_{\Xi_{\tau}}^{-1}(\partial_{\tau}\tilde{v}_{\tau})-(H_{\Xi_{\tau}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Xi_{\tau}})>0.

Proof.

We begin by observing that,

(6.7)

|\nabla_{\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}}^{k}U|({\bm{\omega}},z)\leq C_{k}\eta z^{\alpha}\hat{\varphi}({\bm{\omega}})

for
z\geq\alpha
and
k\geq 0
, (recall that
|\hat{\mu}|\geq 1
). This will be used in several places below to control various error terms.

Combining (
6.3
) and (
6.7
) we see that Lemma
A.7
applies,
20
20

20

Here we just use the part of Lemma
A.7
estimating
F,\mathbf{F},\mathcal{F}
, not the second half of the lemma.
yielding

(6.8)

v_{\Xi_{\tau}}(H_{\Xi_{\tau}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Xi_{\tau}})-v_{\Graph\tilde{v}_{\tau}}(H_{\Graph\tilde{v}_{\tau}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Graph\tilde{v}_{\tau}})=L_{\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}}U+E

where

(6.9)

|E(\mathbf{x})|\leq C(|U(\mathbf{x})|+|\nabla_{\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}}U(\mathbf{x})|+|\nabla^{2}_{\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}}U(\mathbf{x})|)

and
v_{\Graph\tilde{v}_{\tau}}=(\nu_{\Graph\tilde{v}_{\tau}}\cdot\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}})^{-1}
. Because
\tau\mapsto\Graph\tilde{v}_{\tau}
is a rescaled mean curvature flow, we find

(6.10)

\partial_{\tau}\tilde{v}_{\tau}=v_{\Graph\tilde{v}_{\tau}}(H_{\Graph\tilde{v}_{\tau}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Graph\tilde{v}_{\tau}}),

so combining (
6.8
) and (
6.10
) we find

\partial_{\tau}\tilde{v}_{\tau}-v_{\Xi_{\tau}}(H_{\Xi_{\tau}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Xi_{\tau}})=-L_{\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}}U-E.

We now compute

-L_{\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}}U=\left(\tfrac{\alpha}{2}-\alpha(\alpha-1)z^{-2}-|\hat{\mu}|\right)\eta z^{\alpha}\hat{\varphi}({\bm{\omega}}),

so when combined with (
6.9
) and (
6.7
) we find

-L_{\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}}U-E\geq\left(\tfrac{\alpha}{2}-\alpha(\alpha-1)z^{-2}-|\hat{\mu}|-C\right)\eta z^{\alpha}\hat{\varphi}({\bm{\omega}})

for
C
independent of
z,\alpha,\tau
. Recalling that
z\geq\alpha
on the region defining
\Xi_{\tau}
, we can thus take
\alpha
sufficiently large to complete the proof.
∎

6.4.
The proof of Theorem
6.1

We continue with the setup of Theorem
6.1
.

We fix
\alpha_{0},h_{0}>0
,
T_{1}^{\prime}\leq T_{0}
using Proposition
6.5
. It follows that for any
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
and
\eta\in(0,\alpha^{-\alpha}h_{0})
, defining
R
by
\eta R^{\alpha}=h_{0}
, the family of hypersurfaces

(-\infty,T_{1}^{\prime}]\ni\tau\mapsto\Xi_{\tau}:=\{({\bm{\omega}},z)+(\tilde{v}_{\tau}({\bm{\omega}},z)+U({\bm{\omega}},z))\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}}({\bm{\omega}}):({\bm{\omega}},z)\in\hat{\Sigma}\times[\alpha,R]\}\subset\Omega.

is a supersolution to mean curvature flow.

Define
\delta>0
so that if
\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}(t)
is the rescaled mean curvature flow in
\hat{\Omega}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}(0)=\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}
(defined in (
6.1
)) then
[0,3]\ni t\mapsto\Sigma_{\delta}(t)
is smooth and

(6.11)

\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}(t)\cap\hat{\Omega}_{h_{0}}=\emptyset

The existence of such a
\delta>0
follows from the fact that as
\delta\to 0
, the flows
t\mapsto\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}(t)
converge smoothly (on compact subsets of space-time) to the static flow given by
\hat{\Sigma}
(since
\hat{\Sigma}
is a shrinker). We now fix
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
so that

(6.12)

h_{0}2^{-\alpha}<\delta/2

and
\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}\times\{\alpha\}\subset\Omega
.

Now recall the surfaces
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\delta}
as defined in Lemma
6.3
. We set

(6.13)

\Gamma:=\Gamma^{R/4}_{\delta}+\tfrac{R}{2}\mathbf{e}.

We choose the unit normal to
\Gamma
so that
\nu_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{e}>0
. Note that the shrinker mean curvature of
\Gamma
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{\Gamma}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=(\tfrac{1}{2}z-\tfrac{R}{4})\mathbf{e}^{\perp}

so

H_{\Gamma}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Gamma}=(\tfrac{1}{2}z-\tfrac{R}{4})\mathbf{e}\cdot\nu_{\Gamma}\geq 0.

Remark
.

Below, we will choose a family of regions
Q_{\tau}
so that parts of
\Xi_{\tau}
and
\Gamma
are contained in
\partial Q_{\tau}
. The unit normal that
\Xi_{\tau}
inherits from its graphical nature will be
outwards pointing
relative to
Q_{\tau}
.

There is a unique choice of open set
Q_{\Gamma}
so that

\partial Q_{\Gamma}=((\Omega\cap\{z=\tfrac{R}{2}\})\setminus(\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}\times\{\tfrac{R}{2}\}))\cup(\Sigma\cap(B_{2\rho}(\mathbf{0})\times[\tfrac{R}{2},\infty)))\cup\Gamma

and so that
Q_{\Gamma}
is shrinker mean convex (to the inside) in the generalized sense allowing for the corners of
\partial Q_{\Gamma}
(cf.
[
MY82
]
). Similarly, there is a unique choice of families of open sets
\tau\mapsto Q_{\Xi_{\tau}}
so that

\partial Q_{\Xi_{\tau}}=\{({\bm{\omega}},z)+(\tilde{v}_{\tau}({\bm{\omega}},z)+sU({\bm{\omega}},z))\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}}({\bm{\omega}}):{\bm{\omega}}\in\hat{\Sigma},z\in\{\alpha,R\},s\in[0,1]\}\\
\cup(\Sigma\cap(B_{2\rho}(\mathbf{0})\times[\alpha,R]))\cup\Xi_{\tau}.

We now consider the boundaries of
\Xi_{\tau},\Gamma
at
z=R,z=R/2
respectively and show that they do not affect the barrier argument if
\Xi,\Gamma
are welded together appropriately. Define

\tau\mapsto Q_{\tau}=(Q_{\Xi_{\tau}}\cap(Q_{\Gamma}\cup\{z\leq\tfrac{R}{2}\}))\cup(Q_{\Gamma}\cap(Q_{\Xi_{\tau}}\cup\{z\geq R\})).

This will be the fundamental barrier region. The following proposition will be used to verify that as long as the parameter
\eta
is chosen small in the definition of
\Xi_{\tau}
then
\partial Q_{\tau}
will serve as a barrier for a rescaled mean curvature flow in
Q_{\tau}
. In the next lemma it is important to recall that
\nu_{\Xi_{\tau}}
was chosen to be the graphical unit normal, so it points
out
of
Q_{\tau}
.

Proposition 6.6

(Geometric properties used to weld the translator to the long barrier)
.

There is
\eta_{0}\in(0,\alpha^{-\alpha}h_{0})
sufficiently small,
T_{1}\leq T_{1}^{\prime}
sufficiently negative, and
\gamma_{0}>0
so that if
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
,
\tau\in(-\infty,T_{1}]
and
\mathbf{x}\in B_{\gamma_{0}}(\Xi_{\tau})\cap Q_{\tau}
, writing
\mathbf{y}\in\Xi_{\tau}
a closest point to
\mathbf{x}
, then

(1a)

\mathbf{y}\not\in\partial\Xi_{\tau}\cap\{z>\tfrac{R}{2}\}
, and

(1b)

(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})\cdot\nu_{\Xi_{\tau}}(\mathbf{y})>0
.

Similarly, if
\mathbf{x}\in B_{\gamma_{0}}(\Gamma)\cap Q_{\tau}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\Gamma
is a closest point, then

(2a)

\mathbf{y}\not\in\partial\Gamma
, and

(2b)

(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{e}<0
.

Proof.

Consider
\eta\to 0
and
T_{1}\to-\infty
along some sequence (not labeled). The corresponding parameter
R\to\infty
. Hence
\partial\Xi_{\tau}-R\mathbf{e}
converges in
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}
to
\hat{\Sigma}_{h_{0}}\times\{0\}
uniformly with respect to
\tau\in(-\infty,T_{1}]
(thanks to (
6.3
)). On the other hand, recall that
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\delta}-\lambda t\mathbf{e}
converges weakly to the flow defined by
\partial^{*}\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}(t)\times\mathbb{R}
and
[0,3]\to\hat{\Sigma}_{\delta}(t)
is smooth we find that

\Gamma-R\mathbf{e}=\Gamma^{R/2}_{\delta}-\tfrac{R}{2}2\mathbf{e}\to\partial^{*}\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}(2)\times\mathbb{R}

again in
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}
(and
\Gamma
is independent of
\tau
) so

Q_{\tau}-R\mathbf{e}\to((\hat{\Omega}\setminus{\overline{\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}(2)}})\times\mathbb{R})

in the local Hausdorff sense (along with
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}
convergence of the boundary). By (
6.11
) we can thus choose
T_{1}\ll 0
,
\gamma_{0}>0
, and
\eta_{0}>0
small so that

B_{\gamma_{0}}(\partial\Xi_{\tau}\cap\{z>\tfrac{R}{2}\})\cap Q_{\tau}=\emptyset

for all
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
,
\tau\in(-\infty,T_{1}]
. This ensures (1a) holds. Similarly, we note that as
T_{1}\to-\infty
,
\eta\to 0
,
\Xi_{\tau}-\tfrac{R}{2}\mathbf{e}
converges to
\hat{\Sigma}_{2^{-\alpha}h_{0}}\times\mathbb{R}
uniformly with respect to
\tau
. Thus, after taking
T_{1}\ll 0
more negative and
\gamma_{0}>0,\eta_{0}>0
smaller, condition (2a) holds (thanks to (
6.12
)).

We now consider condition (1b). Observe that if
\mathbf{x}\in Q_{\Xi_{\tau}}
then (1b) holds by the graphicality of
\Xi_{\tau}
over (a portion of)
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
. Observe that

Q_{\tau}\setminus Q_{\Xi_{\tau}}=Q_{\Gamma}\cap\{z\geq R\}.

On the other hand, as
\eta\to 0
,
T_{1}\to-\infty
, we find that
B_{\gamma_{0}}(\Xi_{\tau})-R\mathbf{e}
converges to
B_{\gamma_{0}}(\hat{\Sigma}_{h_{0}}\times(-\infty,0])
(uniformly in
\tau
) and
Q_{\Gamma}\cap\{z\geq R\}-R\mathbf{e}
converges to
((\hat{\Omega}\setminus{\overline{\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}(2)}})\times[0,\infty))
(where both convergence statements hold in the local Hausdorff sense). These sets are disjoint by choice of
\gamma_{0}
above, so we have verified (1b).

We finally consider (2b). As above,
\mathbf{e}
-graphicality of
\Gamma
over
\hat{\Omega}_{\delta}\times\{\tfrac{R}{2}\}
allows us to consider only the case of
\mathbf{x}\in B_{\gamma_{0}}(\Gamma)\cap(Q_{\tau}\setminus Q_{\Gamma})
. As before, we have already guaranteed this intersection is empty. This completes the proof.
∎

We can now complete the proof of the main barrier construction.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Fix
\eta_{0},\gamma_{0},T_{1}
as in Proposition
6.6
.

Suppose that for
T_{2}\leq T_{1}
,
(-\infty,T_{2}]\ni\tau\mapsto\mathcal{M}(\tau)
is a rescaled Brakke flow in
\Omega\cap\{z>Z_{1}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
so that for
\Xi_{\tau},Q_{\tau}
defined based on some
\eta\in(0,\eta_{0})
, it holds that

(1)

for all
\tau\in(-\infty,T_{2}]
, it holds that

\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)\cap(B_{2\rho}(\mathbf{0})\times(Z_{1},2Z_{1}))\subset\\
\{({\bm{\omega}},z)+(\tilde{v}_{\tau}({\bm{\omega}},z)+s)\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}}({\bm{\omega}}):z\in[Z_{1},2Z_{1}],s\in(0,\eta z^{\alpha})\},

(2)

there is some
r\geq 0
so that

\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)\subset B_{r}(\mathbf{0})\cup\{tx:\mathbf{x}\in\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0}),d(\mathbf{e},\mathbf{x})<\theta,t\geq 0\}

for all
\tau\in(-\infty,T_{2}]
,
and

(3)

\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)
converges in the local Hausdorff sense to
\Sigma\cap(B_{2\rho}(\mathbf{0})\times[Z_{1},\infty))
as
\tau\to-\infty
.

We claim that
\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)\subset Q_{\tau}
for all
\tau\in(-\infty,T_{2}]
. Note that since
\eta^{-\kappa}\ll R
, it holds that

Q_{\tau}\cap\{Z_{1}<z<\eta^{-\kappa}\}=\{({\bm{\omega}},z)+(\tilde{v}_{\tau}({\bm{\omega}},z)+s)\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}}({\bm{\omega}}):z\in[Z_{1},\eta^{-\kappa}],s\in(0,\eta z^{\alpha})\},

so this will suffice to prove Theorem
6.1
.

To prove that
\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)\subset Q_{\tau}
for all
\tau\in(-\infty,T_{2}]
we can modify the argument in
[
Ilm94
, 10.5]
as follows. First, by combining properties (2) and (3) with Lemma
6.4
(linear growth of translators) we find that there is some maximal
\tau_{2}\in(-\infty,T_{2}]
so that
\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)\subset Q_{\tau}
for all
\tau\in(-\infty,\tau_{2})
and moreover, if
\tau_{2}<T_{2}
there exists

\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau_{2})\cap(\partial Q_{\tau_{2}}\cap\Omega\cap\{z>Z_{1}\}).

(Note that small spherical barriers rule out points of
\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau_{2})
lying in the interior of the complement of
Q_{\tau_{1}}
). Note that
\mathbf{e}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{2}>2Z_{1}
by (1).

It must hold that
\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\Xi_{\tau_{2}}
or
\Gamma
. In each case we reach a contradiction using a similar argument. We first consider
\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\Xi_{\tau_{2}}
. Consider

\phi(\mathbf{x},\tau)=\begin{cases}(\gamma-d(\mathbf{x},\Xi_{\tau}))^{\beta}&d(\mathbf{x},\Xi_{\tau})<\gamma\\
0&d(\mathbf{x},\Xi_{\tau})\geq\gamma\end{cases}

for
\gamma\in(0,\gamma_{0})
and
\beta>2
. Taking
\gamma\in(0,\gamma_{0})
sufficiently small, we can arrange that for
\tau\in[\tau_{2}-1,\tau_{2}]
,
\supp\phi(\cdot,\tau)\cap\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)\cap\{z\in[Z_{1},2Z_{1}]\}=\emptyset
by property (1). Thus, even though
\phi
is not compactly supported in
\Omega
, it is an allowable test function in the Brakke flow condition for
\mathcal{M}(\tau)
. Furthermore, (after passing to the non-rescaled flow) the calculations in
[
Ilm94
, 10.5]
carry over essentially without change. The only potential problem is that
\Xi_{\tau}
has nontrivial boundary and is a supersolution to (rescaled) mean curvature flow (see Proposition
6.5
) as opposed to being a solution. To handle this, we can rely on Proposition
6.6
. Indeed, for
\tau\in[\tau_{2}-1,\tau_{2}]
, at any point of

\mathbf{x}\in\supp\phi(\cdot,\tau)\cap\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau),

the closest point
\mathbf{y}\in\Xi_{\tau}
is not in
\partial\Xi_{\tau}
and
\mathbf{x}
lies “below”
y
with respect to
\nu_{\Xi_{\tau}}
(in the sense of Proposition
6.6
). Using this, we readily check that the remainder of the argument in
[
Ilm94
, 10.5]
yields a contradiction. Similarly, when
\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\Gamma
, we can use (2) and Lemma
6.4
to show
\Gamma
and
\supp\mathcal{M}(\tau)
are well separated at infinity. The argument is then essentially the same as before (replacing
\Xi_{\tau}
by
\Gamma
in the definition of
\phi
). This completes the proof.
∎

7.
Uniqueness in the ancient past

The goal of this section is to show that if we are given
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
and an arbitrary one-sided ancient Brakke flow converging to
\Sigma
with multiplicity one as
\tau\to-\infty
, then the Brakke flow satisfies the hypothesis of Theorem
5.9
, thus proving an unconditional uniqueness result for such flows.

Because certain parts of the argument below hold without the nice, stable end hypothesis on
\Sigma
, we will specify the specific situation considered at the beginning of each subsection. However, we will never weaken a hypothesis in later sections after imposing it earlier, so any result derived earlier in the section will always be applicable.

7.1.
Nearly sharp upper bound in the compact region

In this section consider a smooth shrinker
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
(with no condition at infinity). Consider
(\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau))_{\tau<T}
an ancient (integral unit regular) rescaled Brakke flow with
(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{\tau}\subset\Omega
for all
\tau<T
. Assume that
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
limits smoothly to
\Sigma
with multiplicity one as
\tau\to-\infty
.

Choose
\Sigma_{0}\subset\Sigma
compact domain with smooth boundary. Let
\mu_{0}
denote the first Dirichlet eigenvalue of
L_{\Sigma}
on
\Sigma_{0}
. Note that
\inf\mu_{0}=\mu\leq-1
, where
\inf\mu_{0}
denotes the the infimum of
\mu_{0}
over all such
\Sigma_{0}
and
\mu
is the first eigenvalue of
L_{\Sigma}
on the entire
\Sigma
.

By time translation we can assume that
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
is the normal graph of a positive function
\check{u}:\Sigma^{\prime}\times(-\infty,0]\to\mathbb{R}
for some
\Sigma^{\prime}\Supset\Sigma_{0}
with
\Sigma^{\prime}\supset\Sigma\cap B_{2R}(\mathbf{0})
, where
R
is sufficiently large depending on
\Sigma
. To be precise,
R
should satisfy (
B.9
), (
B.10
), and (
B.11
).

Our goal is to prove the following nearly sharp upper bound for the graphical function. We expect this result and the method of proof to be useful in other contexts.

Theorem 7.1

(Nearly sharp upper bound)
.

There are
C>0,\bar{\tau}<0
depending on
\Sigma,\Sigma_{0},R,\check{\mathcal{M}}
satisfying

\check{u}(\mathbf{x},\tau)\leq Ce^{-\mu_{0}\tau}

for
(\mathbf{x},\tau)\in\Sigma_{0}\times(-\infty,\bar{\tau})
.

Denote by
\varphi_{0}>0
the first Dirichlet eigenfunction of
L_{\Sigma}
on
\Sigma_{0}
with
\|\varphi_{0}\|_{\infty}=1
. Note that the Hopf boundary lemma implies that

(7.1)

\inf_{\partial\Sigma_{0}}|\nabla\varphi_{0}|:=\delta_{0}>0.

Consider
\Sigma^{a}_{-}(\tau)
defined to be the normal graph of

v_{-}^{a}(\mathbf{x},\tau)=ae^{-\mu_{0}\tau}(1-aMe^{-\mu_{0}\tau})\varphi_{0}(\mathbf{x})

over
\Sigma_{0}
, where
M=M(\Sigma_{0})
is the constant given in the next proposition.

Proposition 7.2

(Barrier verification)
.

There are constants
\eta=\eta(\Sigma_{0})\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
and
M=M(\Sigma_{0})\geq 1
with the following significance. Suppose
a>0
and
\tau_{0}\leq 0
are chosen to satisfy

(1)

aMe^{-\mu_{0}\tau_{0}}\leq\eta
, and

(2)

v_{-}^{a}(\mathbf{x},\tau_{0})<\check{u}(\mathbf{x},\tau_{0})
for
\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{0}
.

Then, for
\tau_{0}\leq\tau\leq 0
with
aMe^{-\mu_{0}\tau}\leq\eta
, it holds that
v_{-}(\mathbf{x},\tau)\leq\check{u}(\mathbf{x},\tau)
for
\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
C_{2}=20nR^{2}
denote the constant in Proposition
B.5
. We claim there is
\delta=\delta(\Sigma,\Sigma_{0},\Sigma^{\prime})
so that

(7.2)

|\nabla\log v_{-}^{a}|^{2}-2\partial_{\tau}\log v_{-}^{a}>C_{2}

on
(\partial\Sigma_{0})_{2\delta}\cap\Sigma_{0}
, where
(\partial\Sigma_{0})_{2\delta}
denotes the
2\delta
-neighborhood of
\partial\Sigma_{0}
, for all times
\tau
satisfying
aMe^{-\mu_{0}\tau}\leq\eta
. Indeed, remembering
aMe^{-\mu_{0}\tau}\leq\eta<\tfrac{1}{2}
, we can compute

|\nabla\log v_{-}^{a}|^{2}-2\partial_{\tau}\log v_{-}^{a}=\varphi_{0}^{-2}|\nabla\varphi_{0}|^{2}+2\mu_{0}\left(1-\frac{aMe^{-\mu_{0}\tau}}{1-aMe^{-\mu_{0}\tau}}\right)>\varphi_{0}^{-2}|\nabla\varphi_{0}|^{2}.

In addition,
|\nabla\varphi_{0}|
is bounded away from zero at
\partial\Sigma_{0}
by the Hopf boundary point lemma (
7.1
), but
\varphi_{0}\to 0
as
\mathbf{x}\to\partial\Sigma_{0}
. Thus, we can take
\delta
small enough (independent of
M
) so that (
7.2
) holds. This fixes
\delta
.

Next, we can observe

(\partial_{\tau}-L)v_{-}^{a}=a^{2}M\mu_{0}e^{-2\mu_{0}\tau}\varphi_{0}.

Thus, by Corollary
A.4
, the graph
\Sigma^{a}_{-}(\tau)
satisfies

(\nu_{-}^{a}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma})^{-1}(\partial_{\tau}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{H}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x})\cdot\nu_{-}^{a}=(\partial_{\tau}-L)v_{-}^{a}+E=a^{2}Me^{-2\mu_{0}\tau}\mu_{0}\varphi_{0}+E,

where
\nu_{-}^{a}
is a unit normal vector to
\Sigma^{a}_{-}(\tau)
and

|E(\mathbf{x})|\leq Ca^{2}e^{-2\mu_{0}\tau}(1+a^{2}M^{2}e^{-2\mu_{0}\tau})(\varphi_{0}(\mathbf{x})^{2}+|\nabla\varphi_{0}(\mathbf{x})|^{2}+|\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0}(\mathbf{x})|^{2})

as long as
v_{-}^{a}\leq\eta^{\prime}
for small
\eta^{\prime}=\eta^{\prime}(\Sigma_{0})
and large
C=C(\Sigma_{0})
. Hence, there is
N=N(\Sigma_{0})
so that

|E(\mathbf{x})|\leq Na^{2}e^{-2\mu_{0}\tau}(1+a^{2}M^{2}e^{-2\mu_{0}\tau}).

Therefore,

(\nu_{-}^{a}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma})^{-1}(\partial_{\tau}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{H}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x})\cdot\nu_{-}^{a}\leq(\mu_{0}M\varphi_{0}+N+Na^{2}M^{2}e^{-2\mu_{0}\tau})a^{2}e^{-2\mu_{0}\tau}.

We now fix
M=M(\Sigma_{0})\geq 1
by the requirement that
21
21

21

Note that we make essential use of the fact that
\delta>0
here, since
\varphi_{0}\to 0
as
\mathbf{x}\to\partial\Sigma_{0}
.

\mu_{0}M\varphi_{0}+N\leq-1

on
\Sigma_{0}\setminus(\partial\Sigma_{0})_{\delta}
. Finally we fix
\eta=\eta(\Sigma_{0})
by

\eta=\min\{N^{-\frac{1}{2}},\tfrac{1}{2},\eta^{\prime}\}.

Note that
aMe^{-\mu_{0}\tau}\leq\eta
and
M\geq 1
imply that
v_{-}^{a}\leq\eta\leq\eta^{\prime}
, so the above estimates are valid. Combining the above inequalities yields

(7.3)

(\nu_{-}^{a}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma})^{-1}(\partial_{\tau}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{H}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x})\cdot\nu_{-}^{a}=\partial_{\tau}-L)v_{-}^{a}+E\leq(-1+N\eta^{2})a^{2}e^{-2\mu_{0}\tau}\leq 0

over
\Sigma_{0}\setminus(\partial\Sigma_{0})_{\delta}
.

Now consider
(\mathbf{x},\tau)
the first time of contact with
aMe^{-\lambda_{0}\tau}\leq\eta
towards a contradiction. We have

\check{u}=v_{-}^{a},\qquad\nabla\check{u}=\nabla v_{-}^{a},\qquad\partial_{\tau}\check{u}\leq\partial_{\tau}v_{-}^{a}

at
(\mathbf{x},\tau)
. This implies that
\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{0}\setminus(\partial\Sigma_{0})_{\delta}
. Indeed, if
\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{0}\cap(\partial\Sigma_{0})_{2\delta}
, then thanks to (
7.2
) we find that

|\nabla\log\check{u}|^{2}-2\partial_{\tau}\log\check{u}\geq|\nabla\log v_{-}^{a}|^{2}-2\partial_{\tau}\log v_{-}^{a}>C_{2}

at
(\mathbf{x},\tau)
. This contradicts Proposition
B.5
.

On the other hand, by (
7.3
), we see that at the point of contact
\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{0}\setminus(\partial\Sigma_{0})_{\delta}
, the graph of
v_{-}^{a}
is a subsolution. This is a contradiction.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

We first note that by the parabolic Harnack inequality it suffices to prove that there is
C>0,\tau_{0}<0
so that

\inf_{\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{0}}\check{u}(\mathbf{x},\tau)\leq Ce^{-\mu_{0}\tau}

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
.

Assume this fails. Then, there are
\tau_{k}\to-\infty,C_{k}\to\infty
so that

(7.4)

\inf_{\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{0}}\check{u}(\mathbf{x},\tau_{k})\geq C_{k}e^{-\mu_{0}\tau_{k}}.

Set

a_{k}:=\min\{\tfrac{1}{2}C_{k},\eta M^{-1}e^{\mu_{0}\tau_{k}}\}\to\infty,

where
C_{k}
is defined as in (
7.4
) and
M,\eta
are fixed in Proposition
7.2
. Consider
v_{k}(\mathbf{x},\tau):=v_{-}^{a_{k}}(\mathbf{x},\tau)
. Note that
v_{k}(\mathbf{x},\tau_{k})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}C_{k}e^{-\mu_{0}\tau_{k}}<\inf_{\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{0}}\check{u}(\mathbf{x},\tau_{k})
by (
7.4
). Using Proposition
7.2
, we thus find that
v_{k}(\mathbf{x},\tau)<u(\mathbf{x},\tau)
as long as
\tau_{k}\leq\tau\leq 0
and
a_{k}Me^{-\mu_{0}\tau}\leq\eta
. Note that

a_{k}Me^{-\mu_{0}\tau}\leq\eta\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\tau\leq\tfrac{1}{\mu_{0}}(\log M+\log a_{k}-\log\eta).

Set

\tilde{\tau}_{k}=\tfrac{1}{\mu_{0}}(\log M+\log a_{k}-\log\eta)\to-\infty,

namely
a_{k}Me^{-\mu_{0}\tilde{\tau}_{k}}=\eta
. Note that
\tilde{\tau}_{k}\geq\tau_{k}
by definition of
a_{k}
.

As explained above, Proposition
7.2
thus yields

M^{-1}\eta(1-\eta)\varphi_{0}(\mathbf{x})=v_{k}(\mathbf{x},\tilde{\tau}_{k})\leq\check{u}(\mathbf{x},\tilde{\tau}_{k})

On the other hand, since
\tau_{k}\to-\infty
, we have

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{0}}\check{u}(\mathbf{x},\tilde{\tau}_{k})\to 0

as
k\to\infty
. This is a contradiction, completing the proof.
∎

7.2.
Spatial sub-linear growth of ancient solutions

In this section we consider
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}
. We will prove that an ancient one-sided Brakke flow has a weak sublinear growth property along the ends of
\Sigma
. (Compare with the stronger conclusion
[
CCMS20
, Lemma 7.18]
in the conical case.)

By Lemma
3.2
, we can fix
R>0
sufficiently large so that
\Sigma\setminus B_{R}(\mathbf{0})=\Sigma_{\textrm{cyl}}\cup\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}
. For
\vartheta
small, set

U_{\textrm{con}}^{\vartheta}:=\{t\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0}),d(\mathbf{x},\sqrt{0}\Sigma_{\textrm{con}})<\vartheta,t\geq 0\}

and

U_{\textrm{cyl}}^{\vartheta}:=\cup_{j=1}^{N_{\textrm{cyl}}}\{t\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0}),d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{j})<\vartheta,t\geq 0\}

where
\mathbf{x}_{j}
are the asymptotic directions of the cylindrical ends.

Proposition 7.3

(Sublinear growth estimates)
.

Fix an ancient unit regular integral Brakke flow
\check{\mathcal{M}}(t)
so that
(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{t}\subset\sqrt{-t}\Omega
for all
t<0
and
\Theta(\check{\mathcal{M}},-\infty)<\infty
. Then, for any
\vartheta>0
, there is
R=R(\vartheta)\geq R_{0}
so that for
t\leq-1
,

\frac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{t}\subset B_{R}(\mathbf{0})\cup U_{\textnormal{con}}^{\vartheta}\cup U_{\textnormal{cyl}}^{\vartheta}.

Proof.

By the Frankel property for self-shrinkers, all tangent flows
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
at
t=-\infty
to
\check{\mathcal{M}}
satisfy

\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(t)=k\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma

for all
t<0
, where
22
22

22

Density considerations imply that
k
is independent of the tangent flow, implying uniqueness of the tangent flow, although this will not be relevant in the sequel.

k\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular, if
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
has
\Theta((\mathbf{x},0),\tilde{\mathcal{M}})>0
then
\mathbf{x}\in\sqrt{0}\Sigma
.

Now, we assume that the assertion fails. That is, there is
(\mathbf{x}_{k},t_{k})\in\supp\check{\mathcal{M}}
with
t_{k}\leq-1
, so that
\mathbf{x}_{k}\not\in U_{\textrm{con}}^{\vartheta}\cup U_{\textrm{cyl}}^{\vartheta}
and
|\mathbf{x}_{k}|/\sqrt{-t_{k}}\to\infty
. Note that
|\mathbf{x}_{k}|\to\infty
, since
t_{k}\leq-1
. Define
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{k}(t)
and
(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{k},\tilde{t}_{k})
by dilating around
(\mathbf{0},0)
so that
|\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{k}|=1
. Passing to a subsequence,
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{k}(t)\rightharpoonup\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(t)
a tangent flow to
\check{\mathcal{M}}
at
t=-\infty
and
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{k}\to\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
. Note that
\tilde{t}_{k}\to 0
by construction. In particular, we find that

\Theta((\mathbf{x},0),\tilde{\mathcal{M}})>0,

so
\mathbf{x}\in\sqrt{0}\Sigma
. However, by construction,

\sqrt{0}\Sigma\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\subset U_{\textnormal{con}}^{\vartheta}\cup U_{\textnormal{cyl}}.

This is a contradiction.
∎

7.3.
Graphicality and estimates along the conical ends

In this section we consider
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}
and
(\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau))_{\tau}
an ancient (integral unit regular) rescaled Brakke flow with
(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{\tau}\in\Omega
for all
t<0
. Assume that
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
limits in
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}
to
\Sigma
with multiplicity one as
\tau\to-\infty
.

By Lemma
3.2
, we can fix
R_{0}>0
sufficiently large so that for
R\geq R_{0}
,
\Sigma\setminus B_{R}(\mathbf{0})=\Sigma_{\textrm{cyl}}\cup\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}
. For
\vartheta
small, set

U_{\textrm{con}}^{\vartheta}:=\{s\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0}),d(\mathbf{x},\sqrt{0}\Sigma_{\textrm{con}})<\vartheta,s\geq 0\}

and

U_{\textrm{cyl}}^{\vartheta}:=\cup_{j=1}^{N_{\textrm{cyl}}}\{s\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0}),d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{j})<\vartheta,s\geq 0\}

where
\mathbf{x}_{j}
are the asymptotic directions of the cylindrical ends. Assume that
\vartheta
is chosen sufficiently small and
R
sufficiently large so that (i) there is exactly one component of
\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}
in each component of
U^{\vartheta}_{\textrm{con}}\setminus B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
and (ii) for
\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}
it holds that

\mathbf{x}\pm\tfrac{1}{2}|A_{\Sigma}|\nu_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x})\not\in U_{\textrm{con}}.

(This second condition can be verified by considering the blow-down of
\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}
, a smooth cone by assumption, and it will guarantee that graphs over
\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}
that remain in
U_{\textrm{con}}^{\theta}
lie within a tubular neighborhood of
\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}
).

We first recall the weighted norms from
[
CCMS20
]
along the conical ends are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\|f\|^{(d)}_{k;\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}}|\mathbf{x}|^{-d+i}|\nabla^{i}_{\Sigma}f(\mathbf{x})|

\displaystyle[f]^{(d)}_{\alpha;\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{y}\in\Sigma_{\textrm{cyl}}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}|^{d-\alpha}+|\mathbf{y}|^{d-\alpha}}\frac{|f(\mathbf{x})-f(\mathbf{y})|}{d_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\|f\|^{(d)}_{k,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}}

\displaystyle:=\|f\|^{(d)}_{k;\Sigma_{\textrm{cyl}}}+[\nabla^{k}_{\Sigma}f]^{(d-k)}_{\alpha;\Sigma_{\textrm{cyl}}}.

Lemma 7.4

(Decay along conical ends)
.

Fix
\vartheta
satisfying (i) and (ii) above. For
R\geq R_{0}
, there is
T<0
so that for
\tau<T
,

\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)\lfloor(U_{\textnormal{con}}^{\vartheta}\cup B_{R})

agrees with the multiplicity-one graph of
\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)
defined on some subset of
\Sigma
. Furthermore, for any integer
k\geq 1
,

\lim_{\tau\to-\infty}(\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{k;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}+\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{k}(B_{2R}(\mathbf{0}))})=0

Proof.

As in
[
CCMS20
, Lemma 7.18]
, this follows by rescaling the fact that the tangent cone to the un-rescaled flow at
-\infty
is
t\mapsto\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
(with multiplicity-one).
∎

Proposition 7.5

(Controlling decay along conical ends by the interior)
.

There exists
R_{1}\geq R_{0}
and
C>0
such that for all
R\geq R_{1}
and
\tau_{0}\leq T
,

\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau_{0})\|_{2,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}\leq C\sup_{\tau\leq\tau_{0}}\|\check{u}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{0}(\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}\cap B_{2R}(\mathbf{0}))}\,.

Proof.

We construct barriers for the rescaled flow. To do this, we consider a smooth function
\varphi
defined on
\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}\cap\{|\mathbf{x}|\geq R\}
to be chosen below. Set

\Gamma_{\varphi}=\{\mathbf{x}+\varphi(\mathbf{x})\nu_{\Sigma^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x}):\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{\textrm{con}},|\mathbf{x}|\geq R\}\,.

Note that if
|\varphi(\mathbf{x})||A_{\Sigma}|(\mathbf{x})<1
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}\cap\{|\mathbf{x}|\geq R\}
, then
\Gamma_{\varphi}
will be a smooth hypersurface.

We would like to choose
\varphi
so that
\mathbf{H}+\mathbf{x}^{\perp}/2
points towards
\Sigma
, or equivalently, we would like that
H+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu\leq 0
. Expanding this equation as in Appendix
A
, we can write this as

L\varphi+E(\varphi)\leq 0,\,

where
E
is as in Lemma
A.5
(cf. Corollary
A.4
and
[
CCMS20
, Corollary 3.7]
). Choosing
\varphi:=\alpha|\mathbf{x}|-\beta
as in
[
CS21
, Lemma 3.15]
we have

(7.5)

L\varphi\leq-\tfrac{1}{2}(1+O(r^{-2}))\beta+O(r^{-1})\alpha\,.

Using Lemma
A.5
, we can write

\displaystyle|E(\varphi)(\mathbf{x})|

\displaystyle\leq C(|\mathbf{x}|^{-2}|\varphi(\mathbf{x})|+|\mathbf{x}|^{-1}|\nabla\varphi(\mathbf{x})|)(|\mathbf{x}|^{-1}|\varphi(\mathbf{x})|+|\nabla\varphi(\mathbf{x})|+|\mathbf{x}||\nabla^{2}\varphi(\mathbf{x})|)

\displaystyle\leq C|\mathbf{x}|^{-1}(\alpha+\beta|\mathbf{x}|^{-1})^{2}

For
R>0
large we choose
\beta=\alpha(R-1)
. Thus
\varphi(\mathbf{x})=\alpha
on
\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}\cap\{|\mathbf{x}|=R\}
, and
|\varphi(\mathbf{x})|\leq\alpha(|\mathbf{x}|+1-R)
on
\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}\cap\{|\mathbf{x}|\geq R\}
. In particular, since
|A_{\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}}|=O(|\mathbf{x}|^{-1})
(by scaling), we see that
\Gamma_{\varphi}
is indeed a smooth hypersurface, for
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
small.

In particular, we find that

(7.6)

|E(\varphi)(\mathbf{x})|=O(\alpha^{2}|\mathbf{x}|^{-1})

on
\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}\cap\{|\mathbf{x}|\geq R\}
. Combining (
7.5
) and (
7.6
) we find that for
R
sufficiently large (independent of
\alpha\leq\alpha_{0}
),

L\varphi+E(\varphi)\leq 0

on
\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}\cap\{|\mathbf{x}|\geq R\}
, as desired.
Combining the fact that
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
limits smoothly to
\Sigma
together with Proposition
7.3
this implies

\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau_{0})\|_{0;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}\leq C\sup_{\tau\leq\tau_{0}}\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{0}(\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}\cap B_{2R}(\mathbf{0}))}\,.

Finally, the assertion follows from the Schauder estimates stated in Proposition
7.6
below.
∎

The following estimates are proven in
[
CCMS20
, §3]
.

Proposition 7.6

(Schauder estimates along conical ends)
.

For
\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)
as defined in Lemma
7.4
, it holds that

\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}\leq\sup_{\tau\leq\tau_{0}}C\left(\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{0}(\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}\cap B_{2R}(\mathbf{0}))}+\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau_{0})\|_{0;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}\right)

Proof.

We give the references to the relevant results from
[
CCMS20
, §3]
. By
[
CCMS20
, Lemma 3.5]
(Schauder estimates for the linearized equation
Lu=h
), we find that

(7.7)

\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}\leq C\sup_{\tau\leq\tau_{0}}\left(\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{0}(\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}\cap B_{2R}(\mathbf{0}))}+\|E(\check{u})(\cdot,\tau_{0})\|_{0,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(-1)}\right)

where
E(\check{u})
is the nonlinear error term satisfying the following weighted Hölder estimates proven in
[
CCMS20
, Corollary 3.7]
:

\|E(\check{u})(\cdot,\tau_{0})\|_{0,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(-1)}\leq C\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau_{0})\|_{1,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau_{0})\|_{2,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}.

Using Lemma
7.4
, we can thus absorb this term into the left-hand side of (
7.7
), completing the proof.
∎

7.4.
Upgrading closeness to graphicality along the cylindrical ends

In this section we consider
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}
and
