(\check{\mathcal{M}}(t))_{t}
an ancient (integral unit regular) non-rescaled Brakke flow with
(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{t}\in\sqrt{-t}\Omega
for all
t<0
. Assume that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\check{\mathcal{M}}(t)
limits in
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}
to
\Sigma
with multiplicity one as
t\to-\infty
.

Definition 7.7

(Barrier estimate radius along cylindrical ends)
.

Fix
\vartheta
as in Lemma
7.4
.
For
T<0
fixed, consider smooth positive strictly monotone functions
b,\varepsilon:(-\infty,T)\to(0,\infty)
with
\lim_{t\to-\infty}b(t)=\infty
and
\lim_{t\to-\infty}\varepsilon(t)=0
. We say that
(b(t),\varepsilon(t))
forms a
barrier estimate
for
\check{\mathcal{M}}
along the cylindrical ends if

\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{t}\cap\big(U^{\vartheta}_{\text{cyl}}\setminus B_{2R_{0}}(\mathbf{0})\big)\cap B_{b(t)}(\mathbf{0})\subset U_{\varepsilon(t)}(\Sigma_{\text{cyl}}),

for
t\in(-\infty,T)
. Here,
U_{r}(\Sigma_{\text{cyl}})
is the
r
-neighborhood of
\Sigma_{\text{cyl}}
.

Definition 7.8

(Graphical radius along cylindrical ends)
.

For
\varepsilon_{0}>0
to be fixed in Proposition
7.9
, define the
graphical radius

\rho(t)
to be supremum of
\rho>0
so that
(\frac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\check{\mathcal{M}}(t))\lfloor\big((U^{\vartheta}_{\text{cyl}}\setminus B_{2R_{0}}(\mathbf{0}))\cap B_{\rho}(\mathbf{0})\big)
is the graph of some function defined on a subset of
\Sigma_{\text{cyl}}
, with
C^{5}
-norm
\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

Note that we do not claim that
\rho(t)
is continuous (it would be possible to prove that
\rho(t)
has certain partial continuity properties, but we will not need that below). However, it is clear that in the setting considered here
\lim_{t\to-\infty}\rho(t)=\infty
.

Proposition 7.9

(Barrier estimates imply graphical estimates)
.

Suppose that
(b(t),\varepsilon(t))
forms a barrier estimate for
\check{\mathcal{M}}
. Then, we can take
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\Sigma)>0
small and
T=T(\Sigma,\check{\mathcal{M}},b(t),\varepsilon(t))<0
sufficiently negative so that
\rho(t)\geq b(t)
for
t<T
.

Proof.

Assume (for contradiction) that there is
t_{i}\to-\infty
so that
\rho(t_{i})\leq b(t_{i})
. Because
\rho(t)\to\infty
as
t\to-\infty
,

\inf\{s\leq t_{i}:\rho(s)\leq\rho(t_{i})\}>-\infty.

Without some continuity property of
\rho(t)
we cannot ensure that the infimum is attained, but we can find

t_{i}^{\prime}\in\{s\leq t_{i}:\rho(s)\leq\rho(t_{i})\}

with

t_{i}^{\prime}\leq\inf\{s\leq t_{i}:\rho(s)\leq\rho(t_{i})\}+1.

Note that for
s\leq t_{i}^{\prime}-1
, we have
\rho(s)\geq\rho(t_{i})\geq\rho(t_{i}^{\prime})
. Furthermore, we have
\rho(t_{i}^{\prime})\leq\rho(t_{i})\leq b(t_{i})\leq b(t_{i}^{\prime})
(since
b(t)
is strictly decreasing) and
t_{i}^{\prime}\to-\infty
(since
\rho(t)\to\infty
as
t\to-\infty
).

By assumption, there are points
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\supp\check{\mathcal{M}}\cap\overline{B_{\rho(t_{i}^{\prime})}(\mathbf{0})}
so that
\check{\mathcal{M}}(t_{i}^{\prime})
is not locally written as a graph over a subset of
\Sigma
with
C^{5}
-norm
\leq\varepsilon_{0}
(if this held at each point in
\supp\check{\mathcal{M}}\cap\overline{B_{\rho(t_{i}^{\prime})}(\mathbf{0})}
we could patch these graphs together to write
\supp\check{\mathcal{M}}\cap B_{\rho(t_{i}^{\prime})+\delta_{0}}(\mathbf{0})
—for some
\delta_{0}>0
small—as a graph over a subset of
\Sigma
with
C^{5}
-norm
\leq\varepsilon_{0}
; note that there can only be a single sheet by the multiplicity one covergence). It is clear that
\mathbf{x}_{i}\to\infty
as
i\to\infty
.

We first suppose that (after passing to a subsequence),
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in U_{\textrm{con}}^{\vartheta}
, where
\vartheta
is fixed as in Lemma
7.4
. Applying Lemma
7.4
with
k=5
, we find a contradiction.

Thus, we may pass to a subsequence and assume that
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in U_{\textrm{cyl}}^{\vartheta}
. Up to passing to a subsequence and rotating we assume that
\mathbf{x}_{i}/|\mathbf{x}_{i}|\to\mathbf{e}
and
\Sigma
has a cylindrical end (modeled on
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
) in the
\mathbf{e}
direction.

Write
\mathbf{x}_{i}=({\bm{\omega}}_{i},z_{i})
. Consider the flows

\check{\mathcal{M}}_{i}(t):=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{|t_{i}^{\prime}|}}\check{\mathcal{M}}(|t_{i}^{\prime}|t)-z_{i}\mathbf{e}.

Passing to a subsequence, these flows converge to a Brakke flow
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(t)
with

\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}(\supp\tilde{\mathcal{M}})_{t}\subset\overline{\hat{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}}.

Now, fix
t_{0}<-1
and consider
t\leq t_{0}
. Observe that by definition of the graphical radius,

\Big(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-|t_{i}^{\prime}|t}}\check{\mathcal{M}}(|t_{i}^{\prime}|t)\Big)\lfloor B_{\rho(|t_{i}^{\prime}|t)}(\mathbf{0})

is graphical over some subset of
\Sigma
(with
C^{5}
norm
\leq\varepsilon_{0}
). Note that (since
t\leq t_{0}<-1
)

|t_{i}^{\prime}|t<t_{i}^{\prime}-1

for
i
sufficiently large (depending on
t_{0}
), so for such
i
, we find that

\rho(|t_{i}^{\prime}|t)\geq\rho(t_{i}^{\prime}).

Hence,

\Big(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-|t_{i}^{\prime}|t}}\check{\mathcal{M}}(|t_{i}^{\prime}|t)\Big)\lfloor B_{\rho(t_{i}^{\prime})}(\mathbf{0})

is graphical over some subset of
\Sigma
(with
C^{5}
norm
\leq\varepsilon_{0}
). Translating in the
\mathbf{e}
direction, we find

\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\check{\mathcal{M}}_{i}(t)\lfloor B_{\rho(t_{i}^{\prime})}(-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}z_{i}\mathbf{e})

is graphical over some subset of
\Sigma-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}z_{i}\mathbf{e}
(with
C^{5}
norm
\leq\varepsilon_{0}
). Because
t<-1
, we note that
B_{\rho(t_{i}^{\prime})}(-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}z_{i}\mathbf{e})
limits to
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
as
i\to\infty
. Passing this information to the limit (taking
\varepsilon_{0}
sufficiently small so that we can use interior estimates) and then sending
t_{0}\nearrow-1
, we thus find that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(t)
is graphical over
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
with
C^{5}
norm
\leq\varepsilon_{0}
for all
t<-1
.

On the other hand, we have that

\Big(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t_{i}^{\prime}}}(\supp\mathcal{M})_{t_{i}^{\prime}}\Big)\cap B_{b(t_{i}^{\prime})}(\mathbf{0})\subset U_{\varepsilon(t_{i}^{\prime})}(\Sigma),

because
(b(t),\varepsilon(t))
forms a barrier estimate. Translating this as above, we find
t<-1
,

\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}}_{i})_{t}\cap B_{b(t_{i}^{\prime})}(-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}z_{i}\mathbf{e})\subset U_{\varepsilon(t_{i}^{\prime})}(\Sigma-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}z_{i}\mathbf{e}).

Since
b(t_{i}^{\prime})\geq\rho(t_{i}^{\prime})\geq z_{i}
and
\varepsilon(t_{i}^{\prime})\to 0
, we can conclude as above that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}(\supp\tilde{\mathcal{M}})_{t}\subset\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
for all
t<-1
. Using the integral unit regular property of
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
, we thus find that

\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(t)=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}(\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R})

for all
t<0
. Note that we have unit multiplicity here, due to the fact that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(t)
is graphical over
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
with
C^{5}
norm
\leq\varepsilon_{0}
for all
t<-1
.

By unit regularity, we thus find that for
i
sufficiently large,
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{i}(-1)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|t_{i}^{\prime}|}}\check{\mathcal{M}}(t_{i}^{\prime})-z_{i}\mathbf{e}
is graphical near
({\bm{\omega}}_{i},0)
(with
C^{5}
norm
\leq o(1)
). This contradicts our assumption about
\mathbf{x}_{i}
, completing the proof.
∎

7.5.
Global decay estimates

We now impose the stable end hypothesis on
\Sigma
. More precisely, in this section we consider
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
and
(\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau))_{\tau}
an ancient (integral unit regular) rescaled Brakke flow with
(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{\tau}\in\Omega
for all
\tau<T
. Assume that
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
limits in
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}
to
\Sigma
with multiplicity one as
\tau\to-\infty
.

We define the following norm, adapted to the graphical radius along the cylindrical ends

(7.8)

\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha;\text{cyl}}(\rho)=\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha;\Sigma\cap B_{2R_{0}}(\mathbf{0})}+\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}\cap B_{\rho}(\mathbf{0})}\,.

We now assume there is
(-\infty,\tau_{0}]\ni\tau\mapsto\tilde{v}_{\tau}\in C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}(\Sigma)
so that

\|\tilde{v}_{\tau}\|_{C^{3};\hat{\Sigma}\times[Z_{0},\infty)}=o(1)\textrm{ as $\tau\to-\infty$},

and so that

\tilde{\Sigma}_{\tau}=\Graph_{\Sigma}\tilde{v}_{\tau}(\cdot)\textrm{ is a rescaled mean curvature flow}.

(An important special case is
\tilde{v}_{\tau}\equiv 0
, so that
\tilde{\Sigma}_{\tau}\equiv\Sigma
, but we will also consider the case that
\tilde{v}_{\tau}
is the ancient flow constructed in Proposition
5.2
).

Let
\tilde{\Omega}_{\tau}\subset\Omega
be the open set such that
\partial\tilde{\Omega}_{\tau}=\Sigma_{\tau}
. We assume that
\check{\mathcal{M}}
lies on one side of
\tilde{\Sigma}_{\tau}
, i.e.
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)\subset\tilde{\Omega}_{\tau}
for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. We have the following graphicality and decay estimate. Fix
\alpha>0
as in Theorem
6.1
.

Proposition 7.10

(Applying barriers to obtain decay along cylindrical ends)
.

There exists
\hat{\delta}>0,\vartheta>0,R_{1}\geq 2R_{0}
and depending only
\tilde{\Sigma}_{\tau},\Sigma
with the following property: for every
R\geq R_{1}
there exists
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{0}
such that for
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
,
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)\lfloor(B_{8R}(\mathbf{0}))
is the graph of
\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)
defined on some subset of
\Sigma
(including
\Sigma\cap B_{4R}(\mathbf{0})
) with

\delta(\tau):=\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\leq\tau}\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau^{\prime})-\tilde{v}(\cdot,\tau^{\prime})\|_{4,\alpha;\Sigma\cap B_{4R}(\mathbf{0})}\leq\hat{\delta}\,.

Then either
\delta(\tau^{\prime})=0
for some
\tau^{\prime}<\tau_{1}
and
\check{M}(\tau)=\tilde{\Sigma}_{\tau}
with multiplicity one for all
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
, or

\delta(\tau)>0
for all
\tau<\tau_{1}
and there exists
\bar{\tau}\leq\tau_{1}
such that for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}

\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)\lfloor\big((U^{\vartheta}_{\text{cyl}}\setminus B_{2R_{0}}(\mathbf{0}))\cap B_{\tfrac{1}{2}\delta(\tau)^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}}(\mathbf{0})\big)

is the graph of
\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)
defined on some subset of
\Sigma_{\text{cyl}}
such that

(1)

\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)-\tilde{v}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4,\alpha;\textnormal{cyl}}(\tfrac{1}{2}\delta(\tau)^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}})\leq C\delta(\tau)^{\theta}
for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}
and any
\theta\in(0,\frac{1}{1+\alpha})
where
C
depends on
\theta
, and

(2)

rotating
\Sigma
so that some cylindrical end
\Sigma^{\prime}
points in the
\mathbf{e}
direction, then

\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)-\tilde{v}(\cdot,\tau)\leq C\delta(\tau)z^{\alpha}

for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau},R\leq z\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\delta(\tau)^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}
.

Proof.

By taking
R
sufficiently large, we can assume that the following property holds. For any cylindrical end
\Sigma^{\prime}
, after rotating to point in the
\mathbf{e}
-direction, applying Theorem
6.1
(with
w_{\tau}\equiv w
), then for
Z_{1}
as defined in Theorem
6.1
, it holds that
\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\{z=Z_{1}\}\subset B_{R/2}(\mathbf{0})
.

We assume first that
\delta(\tau)>0
for all
\tau<\tau_{1}
. We claim that by increasing
R
if necessary, for
\tau_{1}
sufficiently negative and
\delta_{0}
sufficiently small, Theorem
6.1
(with
\overline{v}_{\tau}\equiv\overline{v}
the graphical function of the end
\Sigma^{\prime}
over
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
, cf. (
6.2
), or
\overline{v}_{\tau}
the graphical function of the end of
\Sigma_{\tau}
over
\hat{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
), with
\eta^{\prime}=2\delta(\tau^{\prime})
and
T_{2}=\tau^{\prime}
applies to

\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}(\tau):=\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)\lfloor(\Omega\cap\{z>Z_{1}\}\cap\{t\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0}),d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{e})<\vartheta_{0},t>0\})

for
\vartheta_{0}
defined as in (
6.4
). Note that (
6.4
) and Proposition
7.3
guarantee that after taking
\tau_{1}
even more negative,
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}(\tau)
is
23
23

23

The point here being that there might be other components of
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
in
\Omega\cap\{z>Z_{1}\}
corresponding to some other conical/cylindrical end that extends into
\{z>Z_{1}\}
, but for sufficiently negative times these components are disjoint and can be dropped without affecting the Brakke flow property.
a (rescaled) Brakke flow in
\Omega\cap\{z>Z_{1}\}
.

Property (1) in Theorem
6.1
follows from the choice of
\eta^{\prime}
by taking
Z_{1}
sufficiently large (i.e. increasing
R
) and choosing
\delta_{0}>0
sufficiently small. Property (2) in Theorem
6.1
follows from (
6.4
) and Proposition
7.3
. Finally, (3) in Theorem
6.1
holds by assumption. Thus, we find that

(7.9)

\supp\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime})\cap\{Z_{1}<z<(\eta^{\prime})^{-\kappa}\}\subset\\
\{({\bm{\omega}},z)+(w_{\tau^{\prime}}({\bm{\omega}},z)+s)\nu_{\hat{\Sigma}}({\bm{\omega}}):z\in[Z_{1},(\eta^{\prime})^{-\kappa}],s\in[0,(\eta^{\prime})z^{\alpha})\},

where
\kappa=(1+\alpha)^{-1}
is as in Theorem
6.1
and this holds for all
\tau^{\prime}\leq\bar{\tau}
.

For

b(-e^{-\tau})=(2\delta(\tau))^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}},\qquad\varepsilon(-e^{-\tau})=(2\delta(\tau))^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}},

we claim that
(b(t),\varepsilon(t))
forms a barrier estimate for
\check{\mathcal{M}}
in the sense of Definition
7.7
. Indeed, we observe that as
t\to-\infty
,
b(t)\to\infty
and
\varepsilon(t)\to 0
since
\delta(\tau)\to 0
as
\tau\to-\infty
. Finally, the assumption

\supp\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)\cap\big(U^{\vartheta}_{\text{cyl}}\setminus B_{2R_{0}}(\mathbf{0})\big)\cap B_{b(t)}(\mathbf{0})\subset U_{\varepsilon(t)}(\Sigma_{\text{cyl}})

follows from (
7.9
). As such, Proposition
7.9
yields
\bar{\tau}<0
so that along the cylindrical ends
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)\cap B_{\tfrac{1}{2}\delta(\tau)^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}}(\mathbf{0})
is graphical with
C^{5}
norm bounded by
\varepsilon_{0}
(fixed in Proposition
7.9
) for all
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}
.

The estimate
\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)-\tilde{v}(\cdot,\tau)\leq C\delta(\tau)z^{\alpha}
then follows from the height bounds from the barrier (
7.9
). This proves (2). Interpolation yields (1).

Now consider the case
\delta(\tau^{\prime})=0
for some
\tau^{\prime}<\tau_{1}
. Note that we can repeat the first steps of the previous barrier arguments with a sequence
\eta_{i}>0
,
\eta_{i}\to 0
to see that the support of
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
is contained in
\tilde{\Sigma}_{\tau}
for all
\tau\leq\tau^{\prime}
. Since
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
agrees with
\tilde{\Sigma}_{\tau}
on
B_{2R}(\mathbf{0})
for all
\tau\leq\tau^{\prime}
, the constancy Lemma implies that
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)=\tilde{\Sigma}_{\tau}
with multiplicity one for a.e.
\tau\leq\tau^{\prime}
. Unit regularity then implies that
\check{M}(\tau)=\tilde{\Sigma}_{\tau}
with multiplicity one for all
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
.
∎

We extend (
7.8
) to the following semi-global norm

(7.10)

\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha}(\rho)=\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha;\text{cyl}}(\rho)+\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}\,.

The previous proposition and the decay along the core yields the following graphicality and decay estimate. Recall that
\mu
is the first eigenvalue of
L_{\Sigma}
.

Corollary 7.11

(Semi-global decay estimate)
.

For
\varepsilon\in(0,|\mu|)
, there is
\delta_{0}>0
,
\vartheta>0
,
\tau_{1}\ll 0
depending on
\Sigma,\check{\mathcal{M}},\varepsilon
so that for
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
,

\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)\lfloor(B_{3e^{-2\delta_{0}\tau}}(\mathbf{0})\cup U^{\vartheta}_{\textnormal{con}})

is the graph of
\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)
defined on some subset of
\Sigma
with:

(1)

\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4,\alpha}(2e^{-\delta_{0}\tau})\leq e^{2\delta_{0}\tau}
, and

(2)

rotating
\Sigma
so that some cylindrical end
\Sigma^{\prime}
points in the
\mathbf{e}
direction, then
\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\leq Ce^{-(\mu-\varepsilon)\tau}z^{\alpha}
for
\tau\leq\tau_{1},R\leq z\leq 2e^{-\delta_{0}\tau}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.4
, for any
R>0
we can find
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{0}
sufficiently negative and a function
\check{u}
defined on
\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}\cup B_{4R}(\mathbf{0})
for
\tau\leq\tau_{1}<0
so that

(7.11)

\lim_{\tau\to-\infty}\big(\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{k;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}+\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{k}(B_{5R}(\mathbf{0}))}\big)=0

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

On the other hand, taking
R
even larger and
\tau_{1}
even more negative, Theorem
7.1
, together with interpolation, yields

(7.12)

\delta(\tau)\leq e^{(-\mu-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{8})\tau}

on
\Sigma\cap B_{4R}(\mathbf{0})
for
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
. We combine (
7.12
) with Proposition
7.5
to see that (taking
\tau_{1}
even more negative) for
\tau\leq\tau_{1}

\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{2,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}\leq e^{(-\mu-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{4})\tau}\,.

Interpolating with (
7.11
) we thus find (taking
\bar{\tau}
even more negative) for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}

(7.13)

\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4,\alpha;\Sigma\cap B_{2R}(\mathbf{0})}+\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}\leq e^{(-\mu-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2})\tau}\,.

After increasing
R
such that
R\geq R_{1}
(where
R_{1}
is from Proposition
7.10
) we can decrease
\tau_{1}
further such that
\tau_{1}\leq\bar{\tau}
where
\bar{\tau}
is as in Proposition
7.10
. The estimate (
7.13
) combined with Proposition
7.10
(applied with
\tilde{v}_{\tau}\equiv 0
) yield the asserted bounds. This completes the proof.
∎

We furthermore record the following decay estimate for the weighted
L^{2}
-norm. We denote

\Sigma_{\rho}=\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}\cup(\Sigma\cap B_{2R}(\mathbf{0}))\cup(\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}\cap B_{\rho}(\mathbf{0}))\,.

Proposition 7.12

(Integral decay estimate)
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
there is
C>0
and
\bar{\tau}\ll 0
such that

\int_{\Sigma_{2e^{-\delta_{0}\tau}}}\check{u}^{2}e^{-\tfrac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\leq Ce^{2(-\mu-\varepsilon)\tau}\,.

Proof.

Using Theorem
7.1
and Proposition
7.5
(decreasing
\bar{\tau}
if necessary) we see that for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}

\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{0;\Sigma\cap B_{2R}(\mathbf{0})}+\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{0;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}\leq Ce^{(-\mu-\varepsilon)\tau}\,.

By Corollary
7.11
we have along each cylindrical end
\Sigma^{\prime}
that (after rotating to point in the
\mathbf{e}
-direction),

\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\leq Ce^{(-\mu-\varepsilon)\tau}z^{\alpha}

for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}
,
R\leq z\leq 2e^{-\delta_{0}\tau}
. Combining these estimates yields the stated integral estimate.
∎

7.6.
Spectral dynamics

In this section we continue to consider
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
and
(\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau))_{\tau}
an ancient integral unit regular rescaled Brakke flow with
(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{\tau}\subset\Omega
for all
\tau<T<0
. Assume that
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
limits in
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}
to
\Sigma
with multiplicity one as
\tau\to-\infty
.

Recall that we denote with
\varphi>0
the first eigenfunction (corresponding to the eigenvalue
\mu
) of the operator
L
along
\Sigma
, normalized such that
\|\varphi\|_{W}=1
. The following is the main result of this section.

Theorem 7.13

(Pointwise dynamics on the compact part)
.

There exists a unique constant
\check{a}=\check{a}(\check{\mathcal{M}})\geq 0
with the following significance. Given any
R\gg 1
, there exist
C=C(\check{\mathcal{M}},R)>0
and
\bar{\tau}=\bar{\tau}(\check{\mathcal{M}},R)\ll-1
such that

\sup_{\Sigma\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0})}\left|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)-\check{a}e^{-\mu\tau}\varphi(\cdot)\right|\leq Ce^{\big(-\mu+\tfrac{\delta_{0}}{4}\big)\tau}

holds for all
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}
, where
\check{u}
and
\delta_{0}>0
are as in Corollary
7.11
.

Recall that from Corollary
A.4
we have that
\check{u}
satisfies

(7.14)

\partial_{\tau}\check{u}=L\check{u}+E

where
E=\check{u}E_{1}+E_{2}(\nabla\check{u},\nabla\check{u})
satisfy the estimates given in Corollary
A.4
and in Lemma
A.5
along the conical ends.

We consider a smooth cut-off function
\eta\geq 0
satisfying
\eta(r)=1
for
|r|\leq 1,\eta(r)=0
for
|r|\geq 2,|\eta^{\prime}|\leq 2
, and
|\eta^{\prime\prime}|\leq 2
. We define on
\Sigma

(7.15)

\hat{u}(\mathbf{x},\tau)=\check{u}(\mathbf{x},\tau)\,\hat{\eta}(\mathbf{x},\tau)\,,

where
\hat{\eta}=1
on
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}
, and
\hat{\eta}(\mathbf{x},\tau)=\eta(e^{\delta_{0}\tau}|\mathbf{x}|)
on
\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}
. Then we have

(7.16)

\partial_{\tau}\hat{u}=L\hat{u}+\hat{E}=L\hat{u}+\hat{u}E_{1}+E_{2}(\nabla\hat{u},\nabla\check{u})+F

where

F=-2\nabla\hat{\eta}\cdot\nabla\check{u}-\check{u}\,\Delta\hat{\eta}+\tfrac{1}{2}\check{u}\,(\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\nabla\hat{\eta})-\check{u}\,E_{2}(\nabla\hat{\eta},\nabla\check{u})+\check{u}\,\partial_{\tau}\hat{\eta}\,.

Lemma 7.14

(Cutoff error estimate I)
.

There exists
\bar{\tau}=\bar{\tau}(\check{\mathcal{M}})\ll-1
so that for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}

\|F(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}\leq Ce^{5\delta_{0}\tau}\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-2\delta_{0}\tau}\right)\,.

Proof.

Combining Corollary
A.4
and Proposition
7.10
we have

E_{2}(\nabla\hat{\eta},\nabla\check{u})\leq C|\nabla\hat{\eta}||\nabla\check{u}|
and thus

|F(\mathbf{x},\tau)|\leq C\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{1}(\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}\cap\{e^{-\delta_{0}\tau}\leq|\mathbf{x}|\leq 2e^{-\delta_{0}\tau}\})}\chi(\mathbf{x},\tau)\,,

where
\chi=1
for
\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}\cap\{e^{-\delta_{0}\tau}\leq|\mathbf{x}|\leq 2e^{-\delta_{0}\tau}\}
and
\chi=0
otherwise. Thus Proposition
7.10
yields

\|F(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}\leq Ce^{4\delta_{0}\tau}\int_{\Sigma}\chi^{2}e^{-\tfrac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\leq Ce^{4\delta_{0}\tau}\int_{e^{-\delta_{0}\tau}}^{\infty}e^{-\tfrac{1}{4}r^{2}}\,dr\leq Ce^{5\delta_{0}\tau}\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-2\delta_{0}\tau}\right)\,.

This completes the proof. ∎

Lemma 7.15

(Cutoff error estimate II)
.

There exists
\bar{\tau}=\bar{\tau}(\check{\mathcal{M}})\ll-1
so that for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}

\big|\langle\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),\hat{E}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{W}\big|\leq Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}\|\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}^{2}+Ce^{5\delta_{0}\tau}\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-2\delta_{0}\tau}\right)\|\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}\,.

Proof.

Combining Corollary
A.4
and Proposition
7.10
we find

\big|\langle\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\,E_{1}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{W}\big|\leq C\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4}(2e^{-\delta_{0}\tau})\|\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{W}\leq Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}\|\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{W}\,.

To control the second error term, we note that using again Corollary
A.4
, and Proposition
7.10

\begin{split}\big|\langle\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),\,&E_{2}(\nabla\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))\rangle_{W}\big|=\frac{1}{2}\left|\int_{\Sigma}E_{2}(\nabla\hat{u}^{2}(\cdot,\tau),\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))\ e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\right|\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\left|\int_{\Sigma}\hat{u}^{2}\Div\!\Big({E}^{\#}_{2}(\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))\,e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\Big)\right|\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|\int_{\Sigma}\hat{u}^{2}\Div\!\big({E}^{\#}_{2}(\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))\big)\,e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\right|+\frac{1}{4}\left|\int_{\Sigma}\hat{u}^{2}{E}_{2}(\mathbf{x}^{T},\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))\big)\,e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\right|\\
&\leq Ce^{2\delta_{0}\tau}\|\hat{u}\|_{W}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\left|\int_{\Sigma}\hat{u}^{2}{E}_{2}(\mathbf{x}^{T},\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))\big)\,e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\right|\end{split}

where
{E}^{\#}_{2}
is the
(1,1)
-tensor obtained via the natural isomorphism
\#:T^{*}\Sigma\to T\Sigma
induced via
g
, acting on the first entry of
E_{2}(\cdot,\cdot)
. Note that along the conical ends we have from Lemma
A.5
and Proposition
7.10
that

|E_{2}(\mathbf{x}^{T},\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))|\leq C|\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)|\leq Ce^{2\delta_{0}\tau}\,,

whereas along the cylindrical ends, since
|\mathbf{x}^{T}|\leq 2e^{-\delta_{0}\tau}
on the support of
\hat{u}
, we have with Corollary
A.4
and (2) in Proposition
7.10
(recalling that
\delta_{0}<\tfrac{|\mu|-\varepsilon}{\alpha}
).

|E_{2}(\mathbf{x}^{T},\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))|\leq Ce^{-\delta_{0}\tau}|\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)|\leq Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}\,.

This implies

\big|\langle\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),\,E_{2}(\nabla\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))\rangle_{W}\big|\leq Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}\|\hat{u}\|_{W}^{2}\,.

Using Lemma
7.14
together with

\big|\langle\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),F(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{W}\big|\leq\|\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}\|F(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}

yields the desired result.
∎

We define orthogonal projections
P_{i}:L^{2}_{W}(\Sigma)\to L^{2}_{W}(\Sigma)
via

P_{1}(v):=\langle v,\varphi\rangle_{W}\varphi\qquad\text{and}\qquad P_{2}(v):=v-P_{1}(v)\,.

Lemma 7.16

(Cutoff error estimate III)
.

There exists
\bar{\tau}=\bar{\tau}(\check{\mathcal{M}})\ll-1
such that for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}
and
i=1,2

(7.17)

\big|\langle P_{i}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),\hat{E}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{W}\big|\leq Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}\|\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}^{2}+Ce^{5\delta_{0}\tau}\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-2\delta_{0}\tau}\right)\|\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}\,.

Proof.

Due to Lemma
7.15
it is sufficient to prove (
7.17
) for
i=1
. Note that as in the proof of Lemma
7.15
we have

\big|\langle P_{1}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\,E_{1}(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{W}\big|\leq Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}\|P_{1}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{W}

and

\big|\langle P_{1}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),F(\cdot,\tau)\rangle_{W}\big|\leq Ce^{5\delta_{0}\tau}\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-2\delta_{0}\tau}\right)\|\hat{P}_{1}u(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}\,.

Furthermore, we have

\begin{split}\langle P_{1}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),E_{2}(\nabla\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))\rangle_{W}&=-\int_{\Sigma}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\Div\!\Big(P_{1}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\,E_{2}^{\#}(\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))\ e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\Big)\\
&=-\int_{\Sigma}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)P_{1}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\,\Div\!\Big(E_{2}^{\#}(\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))\ e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\Big)\\
&\quad-\int_{\Sigma}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)E_{2}(\nabla P_{1}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\end{split}

The first term on the right hand side can be estimates as in the proof of Lemma
7.15

\left|\int_{\Sigma}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)P_{1}\hat{u}\,\Div\!\Big(E_{2}^{\#}(\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))\ e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\Big)\right|\leq Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}\|P_{1}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}\|\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}\,.

The second term satisfies

\left|\int_{\Sigma}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)E_{2}(\nabla P_{1}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau),\nabla\check{u}(\cdot,\tau))e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\right|\leq Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}\|\nabla P_{1}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}\|\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}\,.

Since

\|\nabla P_{1}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}\leq\|\nabla\varphi\|_{W}\|P_{1}\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}\leq C\|\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{W}

for some constant
C
depending on
\varphi
, combining these inequalities yields the stated estimate.
∎

We recall that the operator
L
has a discrete spectrum on
H^{1}_{W}(\Sigma)
and that we denote by
\mu=\mu_{1}<-1
the bottom of the spectrum. We write
-1\geq\mu_{2}>\mu
for the next eigenvalue.

Proposition 7.17

(Projected spectral dynamics)
.

There exists
\bar{\tau}=\bar{\tau}(\check{\mathcal{M}})\ll-1
so that for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}
the quantities
\hat{U}_{1}:=\|P_{1}\hat{u}\|_{W}^{2}+\exp(-\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-2\delta_{0}\tau})
and
\hat{U}_{2}:=\|P_{2}\hat{u}\|_{W}^{2}
satisfy

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\hat{U}_{1}

\displaystyle\geq-2\mu\hat{U}_{1}-Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}(\hat{U}_{1}+\hat{U}_{2})

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\hat{U}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq-2\mu_{2}\hat{U}_{2}+Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}(\hat{U}_{1}+\hat{U}_{2})\,.

Proof.

We compute

(7.18)

\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\hat{U}_{1}=\langle P_{1}\hat{u},\partial_{\tau}\hat{u}\rangle_{W}+\tfrac{\delta_{0}}{2}e^{-2\delta_{0}\tau}\exp(-\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-2\delta_{0}\tau})\,,

and

(7.19)

\langle P_{1}\hat{u},\partial_{\tau}\hat{u}\rangle_{W}=\langle P_{1}\hat{u},L\hat{u}+\hat{E}\rangle_{W}=-\mu\|P_{1}\hat{u}\|_{W}^{2}+\langle P_{1}\hat{u},\hat{E}\rangle_{W}\,.

Applying Lemma
7.16
yields the first inequality. Similarly we can obtain

\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\hat{U}_{2}=\langle P_{2}\hat{u},\partial_{\tau}\hat{u}\rangle_{W}=\langle P_{2}\hat{u},L\hat{u}+\hat{E}\rangle_{W}\leq-\mu_{2}\|P_{2}\hat{u}\|_{W}^{2}+\langle P_{2}\hat{u},\hat{E}\rangle_{W}\,.

Hence Lemma
7.16
also yields the second inequality.
∎

Lemma 7.18

(Integrated spectral dynamics I)
.

There exists
\bar{\tau}=\bar{\tau}(\check{\mathcal{M}})\ll-1
so that for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}
it holds that

\|P_{2}\hat{u}\|^{2}_{W}\leq Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}\left(\|P_{1}\hat{u}\|^{2}_{W}+\exp(-\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-2\delta_{0}\tau})\right)\,

and
\|\hat{u}\|_{W}\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}
.

Proof.

Consider again
\hat{U}_{1}:=\|P_{1}\hat{u}\|_{W}^{2}+\exp(-\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-2\delta_{0}\tau})
and
\hat{U}_{2}:=\|P_{2}\hat{u}\|_{W}^{2}
and define

f(\tau)=e^{(\mu+\mu_{2})\tau}\hat{U}_{2}-Me^{(\mu+\mu_{2}+\delta_{0})\tau}\hat{U}_{1}\,,

where
M
is a positive constant to be determined. Proposition
7.17
implies that

\begin{split}f^{\prime}(\tau)&\leq-(\mu_{2}-\mu)e^{(\mu+\mu_{2})\tau}\hat{U}_{2}-M(\delta_{0}+\mu_{2}-\mu)e^{(\mu+\mu_{2}+\delta_{0})\tau}\hat{U}_{1}\\
&\quad+C(1+Me^{\delta_{0}\tau})e^{(\mu+\mu_{2}+\delta_{0})\tau}(\hat{U}_{1}+\hat{U}_{2})\,.\end{split}

We now choose
\bar{\tau}
such that

2Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}\leq\mu_{2}-\mu

for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}
and let
M=2C(\mu_{2}-\mu)^{-1}
. This implies

f^{\prime}(\tau)\leq-(\mu_{2}-\mu)e^{(\mu+\mu_{2})\tau}\hat{U}_{2}-2Ce^{(\mu+\mu_{2}+\delta_{0})\tau}\hat{U}_{1}+2Ce^{(\mu+\mu_{2}+\delta_{0})\tau}(\hat{U}_{1}+\hat{U}_{2})\leq 0\,.

By Proposition
7.12
we have
\lim_{\tau\to-\infty}f(\tau)=0
and thus
f(\tau)\leq 0
for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}
, which implies

\hat{U}_{2}\leq 2C(\mu_{2}-\mu)^{-1}e^{\delta_{0}\tau}\hat{U}_{1}

for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}
. This yields the first estimate.

Returning to Proposition
7.17
we thus find

\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\log e^{2\mu\tau}\hat{U}_{1}\geq-(C+1)e^{\delta_{0}\tau}

Because the right hand side is integrable on
(-\infty,\bar{\tau}]
, this implies that
\hat{U}_{1}\leq C^{\prime}e^{-2\mu\tau}
. Combining this with the estimate for
\hat{U}_{2}
yields the second estimate.
∎

Theorem 7.19

(Integrated spectral dynamics II)
.

There exists
\check{a}\geq 0
,
C>0
, and
\bar{\tau}=\bar{\tau}(\check{\mathcal{M}})\ll-1
such that for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}

\|\hat{u}-\check{a}e^{-\mu\tau}\varphi\|_{W}^{2}\leq Ce^{(2|\mu|+\delta_{0})\tau}\,.

Proof.

As in (
7.18
), (
7.19
), we find

\left|\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\left(e^{2\mu\tau}\|P_{1}\hat{u}\|^{2}_{W}\right)\right|\leq 2e^{2\mu\tau}\langle P_{1}\hat{u},\hat{E}\rangle_{W}.

Combining Lemma
7.16
with Lemma
7.18
, we thus find that

\left|\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\left(e^{2\mu\tau}\|P_{1}\hat{u}\|^{2}_{W}\right)\right|\leq Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}\,

for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}\ll 0
. Thus, the limit

\lim_{\tau\to-\infty}e^{2\mu\tau}\|P_{1}\hat{u}\|^{2}_{W}=\check{a}^{2}

exists, and

\left|e^{2\mu\tau}\|P_{1}\hat{u}\|^{2}_{W}-\check{a}^{2}\right|\leq Ce^{\delta_{0}\tau}\,

for
\tau\leq\bar{\tau}\ll 0
. Combined with Lemma
7.18
, this proves the assertion.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.13
.

This follows from Theorem
7.19
and interpolation.
∎

Remark
.

We emphasize that for fixed
\check{\mathcal{M}}
, the constant
\check{a}
is the
same
in both Theorem
7.19
and Theorem
7.13
.

7.7.
Global lower barrier

In this section we continue to consider
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime}
and
(\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau))_{\tau}
an ancient (integral unit regular) rescaled Brakke flow with
(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{\tau}\subset\Omega
for all
\tau<T<0
. Assume that
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
limits in
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}
to
\Sigma
with multiplicity one as
\tau\to-\infty
.

In this section, we additionally impose the requirement that Theorem
7.19
holds with constant
\check{a}>0
. Because we are concerned with an optimal
lower barrier
, the case that
\check{a}=0
will be trivially handled by choosing
\bar{M}(\tau)\equiv\Sigma
for all
\tau
.

We denote by
\bar{v}:\Sigma\times(-\infty,\tau_{0})\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
the function such that

\tau\mapsto\bar{M}(\tau)=\Graph_{\Sigma}(\bar{v}(\cdot,\tau))

is the smooth ancient rescaled mean curvature flow on one side of
\Sigma
constructed in Proposition
5.6
. By shifting time, we can assume that

(7.20)

\lim_{\tau\to-\infty}e^{\mu\tau}\langle\bar{v}(\cdot,\tau),\varphi\rangle_{W}=1\,.

Note that this condition together with Proposition
5.10
implies uniqueness under the decay assumption (
5.6
). We also consider the general solution
\mathcal{M}
on one side of
\mathcal{M}_{\Sigma}
, and denote the corresponding rescaled flow by
\tau\mapsto M(\tau)
.

Since we assumed that
\check{a}>0
, we can shift (rescaled) time in the given flow
\check{\mathcal{M}}
to ensure that
\check{a}=1
. In other words, we arrange that

(7.21)

\lim_{\tau\to-\infty}e^{\mu\tau}\langle\hat{u},\varphi\rangle_{W}=1\,,

where
\hat{u}
is the cutoff graphical function defined in (
7.15
).

Applying Theorem
7.13
to both
24
24

24

We use that the constant
\check{a}
is the same in the point-wise estimate and integrated estimate.
the unknown flow
\check{\mathcal{M}}
and the graphical flow
\bar{M}
, we can choose a sequence
\tau_{m}\to-\infty
such that

(7.22)

(1-\tfrac{1}{m})e^{-\mu\tau_{m}}\varphi\leq\min\{\bar{v}(\cdot,\tau_{m}),\hat{u}(\cdot,\tau_{m})\}

on
B_{2m}(\mathbf{0})\cap\Sigma
. We consider a smooth cut-off function
\eta
with values in
[0,1]
such that

\eta(r)=1\quad\text{for}\ r\leq 1,\qquad\eta(r)=0\quad\text{for}\ r\geq 2\,,

and define
\bar{w}^{m}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Sigma)
by

\bar{w}^{m}(\mathbf{x})=(1-\tfrac{1}{m})e^{-\mu\tau_{m}}\eta(|\mathbf{x}|/m)\varphi(\mathbf{x})\,.

Recall (cf. Proposition
5.6
) that we may assume that
\bar{v}(\cdot,\tau)\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}
for some
C>0
and
\tau\in(-\infty,\tau_{0})
.

Proposition 7.20

(Partial lower barrier)
.

There exists
\bar{\tau}=\bar{\tau}(\check{\mathcal{M}})\in(-\infty,\tau_{0})
and
C>0
such that for all
m
sufficiently large there exists a smooth rescaled ancient mean curvature flow

[\tau_{m},\bar{\tau}]\ni\tau\mapsto\bar{M}^{m}(\tau)=\Graph_{\Sigma}(\bar{v}^{m}(\cdot,\tau))

such that

(1)

\bar{v}^{m}(\cdot,\tau)\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
and
\bar{v}^{m}(\cdot,\tau)\geq 0
for all
\tau\in[\tau_{m},\bar{\tau}]
,

(2)

\bar{v}^{m}(\cdot,\tau_{m})=\bar{w}^{m}
,

(3)

\bar{v}^{m}\leq v
holds for
\tau\in[\tau_{m},\bar{\tau}]
,

(4)

(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{\tau}
avoids
\bar{M}^{m}(\tau)
in the sense that

(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{\tau}\subset\Omega\setminus\{\mathbf{x}+s\bar{v}^{m}(\mathbf{x},\tau):\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma,s\in[0,1]\}

for
\tau\in[\tau_{m},\bar{\tau}]
,

(5)

\|\bar{v}^{m}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{4}(\Sigma)}\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}
for
\tau\in[\tau_{m},\bar{\tau}]
.

Proof.

Note that we can apply Corollary
4.2
with initial datum
\bar{w}^{m}
to obtain a solution
\tau\mapsto\bar{v}^{m}(\cdot,\tau)
to graphical rescaled mean curvature flow for
\tau\in[\tau_{m},\bar{\tau}_{m}]
. We note that the Ecker-Huisken maximum principle implies that

(7.23)

v^{m}(\cdot,\tau)\leq v(\cdot,\tau)\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}

for some
C>0
for
\tau\in[\tau_{m},\bar{\tau}_{m}]
. Corollary
4.2
then ensures the existence of a uniform lower bound
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\tau}_{m}
, when
m
is sufficiently large. This establishes points (1) through (3). Point (4) follows from Proposition
E.1
. Note that Proposition
4.2
implies that
\|\bar{v}^{m}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{3}(\Sigma)}
is uniformly controlled for all
\tau\in[\tau_{n},\bar{\tau}]
. Point (5) then follows by considering the unrescaled mean curvature flow and applying standard interior parabolic Schauder estimates to the mean curvature flow equation for graphs with controlled, small
C^{1,\alpha}
-norm over
\Sigma
, combined with (
7.23
).
∎

Proposition 7.21

(Global lower barrier)
.

Under the assumptions (
7.20
) and (
7.21
) it holds that
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
lies above
\tau\mapsto\bar{M}(\tau)
for
\tau\in(-\infty,\bar{\tau})
in the sense that

(\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})_{\tau}\subset\Omega\setminus\{\mathbf{x}+s\bar{v}(\mathbf{x},\tau)\nu_{\Sigma}:\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma,s\in[0,1)\}.

Proof.

Recall that from Corollary
A.4
we have that
\bar{v}^{m}
satisfies

(7.24)

\partial_{\tau}\bar{v}^{m}=L\bar{v}^{m}+E

where
E=\bar{v}^{m}E_{1}+E_{2}(\nabla\bar{v}^{m},\nabla\bar{v}^{m})
satisfy the estimates given in Corollary
A.4
. Proposition
7.20
, (5) then implies

|\partial_{\tau}\bar{v}^{m}-L\bar{v}^{m}|\leq Ce^{-2\mu\tau}\,.

This yields

\big|\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\langle\bar{v}^{m},\varphi\rangle_{W}+\mu\langle\bar{v}^{m},\varphi\rangle_{W}\big|\leq Ce^{-2\mu\tau}\,,

and thus for
\tau\in[\tau_{m},\bar{\tau}]

\left|e^{\mu\tau}\langle\bar{v}^{m}(\cdot,\tau),\varphi\rangle_{W}-e^{\mu\tau_{m}}\langle\bar{w}^{m},\varphi\rangle_{W}\right|\leq\int_{\tau_{m}}^{\tau}e^{-\mu s}\,ds\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}e^{-\mu s}\,ds\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}\,.

By (
7.20
), (
7.21
) and (
7.22
) we have

\lim_{m\to\infty}e^{\mu\tau_{m}}\langle\bar{w}^{m},\varphi\rangle_{W}=1.

Let
\tilde{v}
be any subsequential limit of the sequence
\bar{v}^{m}
. Note that
(-\infty,\bar{\tau})\ni\tau\mapsto\tilde{v}(\cdot,\tau)
is a entire graphical solution to rescaled mean curvature flow over
\Sigma
, lying below
\bar{v}
, which satisfies

\left|e^{\mu\tau}\langle\tilde{v},\varphi\rangle_{W}-1\right|\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}

as well as

\|\tilde{v}(\cdot\,\tau)\|_{C^{3}(\Sigma)}\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}\,.

Proposition
5.10
thus implies that
\tilde{v}\equiv\bar{v}
. The assertion then follows by combining this with (4) in Proposition
7.20
.
∎

7.8.
Global uniqueness

In this section we consider
25
25

25

If one is content with uniqueness in the ancient past, the condition

suffices. The strictly stable end (as opposed to stable end) condition is only used to apply Proposition

to upgrade uniqueness in the ancient past to uniqueness for all

.

(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and

for all

Let

be given by Theorem

.

We establish the following global uniqueness theorem.

Theorem 7.22

(Global uniqueness result)
.

(1)

If
\check{a}>0
then up to a shift in time,

coincides with the rescaling of the flow constructed in Proposition

for all

(2)

If

.

Proof.

We first consider case (1). By shifting time we can assume that (
7.21
) holds. Thus by Proposition
7.21
there exists
\bar{\tau}
, such that for
\tau\in(-\infty,\bar{\tau})
,
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
lies above
26
26

26

Here, we mean in the sense of Proposition
7.21
.

\bar{M}(\tau)
, where
\tau\mapsto\bar{M}(\tau)
is the smooth ancient rescaled mean curvature flow on one side of

constructed in Proposition

, satisfying (
7.20
). We denote with
\bar{v}:\Sigma\times(-\infty,\tau_{0}^{\prime})\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
the function such that

\tau\mapsto\bar{M}(\tau)=\Graph_{\Sigma}(\bar{v}(\cdot,\tau))\,.

We can assume that
\tau_{0}^{\prime}\leq\bar{\tau}
.

Our goal is to prove that there is some
\tau_{0}\in(-\infty,\tau_{0}^{\prime}]
with
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)=\bar{M}(\tau)
for all
\tau\in(-\infty,\tau_{0})
. Given this, the uniqueness for all (rescaled) time
27
27

27

and thus all negative non-rescaled time
follows from Proposition

. Below, we will repeatedly (but only finitely many times) take

more negative until we achieve this goal.

Fix
R=R_{1}
as in Proposition
7.10
. Note that Theorem
7.13
together with (

) and (
7.21
) implies that for
\tau_{0}\ll 0
,

(7.25)

\sup_{\Sigma\cap B_{5R}(\mathbf{0})}|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau)-\bar{v}(\cdot,\tau)|\leq e^{\big(-\mu+\frac{\delta_{0}}{4}\big)\tau}

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. For
\tau\leq\tau_{0}\ll 0
, we find

(7.26)

\delta(\tau):=\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\leq\tau}\|\check{u}(\cdot,\tau^{\prime})-\bar{v}(\cdot,\tau^{\prime})\|_{4,\alpha;\Sigma\cap B_{4R}(\mathbf{0})}\leq\hat{\delta}\,,

where
\hat{\delta}
is as in Proposition
7.10
.
By Proposition
7.10
we can assume that
\delta(\tau)>0
for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
.

We now define
w=\check{u}-\bar{v}
(on the domain of definition of
\check{u}
). Observe that the fact that
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
lies above
\bar{M}(\tau)
implies that
w\geq 0
. We set

W(\tau)^{2}:=\sup_{s\leq\tau}\int_{\Sigma\cap B_{5R}(\mathbf{0})}w(\cdot,s)^{2}e^{-\tfrac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}.

We define

(7.27)

\gamma:=\sup\{\gamma^{\prime}>0\,|\,W(\tau)\leq e^{(1+2\alpha)\gamma^{\prime}\tau}\ \text{for}\ \tau\leq\tau_{0}\}\,.

We assume (for contradiction) that
\gamma<\infty
for all
\tau_{0}\ll 0
. Note that if for some
\tau_{0}
fixed,
\gamma=\infty
, then
W(\tau)=0
for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Thus, (
7.30
) together with Proposition
7.10
implies that
\check{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)=\bar{M}(\tau)
for all

, proving the assertion.

To begin, we note that (
7.25
) implies that

(7.28)

W(\tau)\leq Ce^{\big(-\mu+\frac{\delta_{0}}{4}\big)\tau}

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. This implies that

(7.29)

\gamma\geq\gamma_{0}:=(1+2\alpha)^{-1}\left(-\mu+\tfrac{\delta_{0}}{8}\right)>0\,

for
\tau_{0}\ll 0
.

We now show derive pointwise estimates on
w
from the given estimates on
W
. Interior parabolic Schauder estimates imply that there is a constant
C>0
, such that

(7.30)

\delta(\tau)\leq CW(\tau)

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Recall the definition of the semi-global norm as in (
7.10
)

\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha}(\rho)=\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha;\,\text{cyl}}(\rho)+\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}\,.

Proposition
7.10
implies that there is a constant
C_{0}\geq 2
such that we can choose

(7.31)

\rho(\tau):=C_{0}^{-1}W(\tau)^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}

with

\|w(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4,\alpha;\,\text{cyl}}(\rho(\tau))\leq CW(\tau)^{\theta}

for any
\theta\in(0,\frac{1}{1+\alpha})
. In particular, by interpolating Lemma
7.4
with the estimate that follows by using (
7.30
) in Proposition
7.5
,

(7.32)

\|w(\cdot,\tau)\|_{4,\alpha}(\rho(\tau))\leq W(\tau)^{\frac{1}{1+2\alpha}}

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}\ll 0
. We note that

(7.33)

\rho(\tau)\geq 3e^{-\gamma\tau}\,

for
\tau_{0}
sufficiently negative. This will be useful below.

In the following part of the argument, we will keep decreasing
\tau_{0}
, but will emphasize that all constants
C
will be uniformly bounded independently of
\tau_{0}\to-\infty
(and in particular will not depend on the value of
\gamma
). This will allow us to derive a contradiction to the assumption that
\gamma<\infty
(as long as we take
\tau_{0}
sufficiently negative).

We now show how to convert estimates on
W(\tau)
into (potentially) optimal estimates on
\check{u}
. Propostion
5.6
ensures that for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, we have

\|\bar{v}\|_{C^{5}(\Sigma)}\leq Ce^{-\mu\tau}\,,

and combining this with (
7.32
) we find

\|\check{u}\|_{4,\alpha}(\rho(\tau))\leq Ce^{\gamma_{\mu}\tau}\,,

where we set

\gamma_{\mu}:=\min\{-\mu,\gamma\}\,.

Using these estimates in Corollary
A.7
, we find that
w
satisfies

(7.34)

\partial_{\tau}w=L_{\Sigma}w+E^{w}

where

E^{w}(\mathbf{x})=w(\mathbf{x})F(\mathbf{x})+\nabla w(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{x})+\nabla^{2}w(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x})\,,

with the estimate

|F|+|\mathbf{F}|+|\mathcal{F}|+|\nabla\mathcal{F}|\leq Ce^{\gamma_{\mu}\tau}

on
\Sigma_{\text{con}}\cup(\Sigma_{\text{cyl}}\cap B_{\rho(\tau)}(\mathbf{0}))
for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
.

We consider a smooth cut-off function
\eta\geq 0
satisfying
\eta(r)=1
for
|r|\leq 1,\eta(r)=0
for
|r|\geq 2,|\eta^{\prime}|\leq 2
, and
|\eta^{\prime\prime}|\leq 2
. Using (
7.33
), we can define on
\Sigma

(7.35)

\hat{w}(\mathbf{x},\tau)=w(\mathbf{x},\tau)\,\hat{\eta}(\mathbf{x},\tau)\,,

where
\hat{\eta}=1
on
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}
, and
\hat{\eta}(\mathbf{x},\tau)=\eta(10^{-1}e^{\gamma\tau}|\mathbf{x}|)
on
\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}
. We introduce

(7.36)

\hat{W}(\tau):=\sup_{s\leq\tau}\|\hat{w}(\cdot,s)\|_{W}

and note that for
\tau_{0}
sufficiently negative Proposition
7.10
(as in the proof of Proposition
7.12
) combined with (
7.30
) implies that

(7.37)

\hat{W}(\tau)\leq Ce^{(1+2\alpha)\gamma\tau}\leq Ce^{\big(-\mu+\frac{\delta_{0}}{8}\big)\tau}

and we have

W(\tau)\leq\hat{W}(\tau)\ .

The evolution of
\hat{w}
is given by

(7.38)

\partial_{\tau}\hat{w}=L_{\Sigma}\hat{w}+\hat{E}^{w}=L_{\Sigma}\hat{w}+\hat{w}F+\nabla\hat{w}\cdot\mathbf{F}+\nabla^{2}\hat{w}\cdot\mathcal{F}+G

where

\begin{split}G&=-2\nabla\hat{\eta}\cdot\nabla w-w\,\Delta\hat{\eta}+\tfrac{1}{2}w\,(\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\nabla\hat{\eta})-w\nabla\hat{\eta}\cdot\mathbf{F}\,-(\nabla w\otimes\nabla\hat{\eta}+\nabla\hat{\eta}\otimes\nabla w)\cdot\mathcal{F}\\
&\quad-w\nabla^{2}\hat{\eta}\cdot\mathcal{F}+w\,\partial_{\tau}\hat{\eta}\,.\end{split}

Note that
|\nabla\hat{\eta}|,|\nabla^{2}\hat{\eta}|\leq C\chi_{A}e^{\gamma\tau}
where
\chi_{A}
is the characteristic function of the annulus
A=\{\mathbf{x}\,|\,e^{-\gamma\tau}\leq|\mathbf{x}|\leq 2e^{-\gamma\tau}\}
. Note that by (
7.33
),
2e^{-2\gamma\tau}<\rho(\tau)
for
\tau_{0}
sufficiently negative. Thus we can estimate using (
7.32
) and (
7.27
)

|G|\leq C\chi_{A}e^{\gamma\tau}\big((|w|+|\nabla w|)+(1+\gamma)|\mathbf{x}||w|)\big)\leq C\chi_{A}.

We emphasize that the final constant
C
is independent of
\gamma
(which depends on
\tau_{0}
). Indeed,

\chi_{A}e^{\gamma\tau}\gamma|\mathbf{x}||w|=\chi_{A}((|\mathbf{x}|e^{\gamma\tau})(\gamma|w|))\leq\chi_{A}(2\gamma e^{\gamma\tau})

Since
\tau_{0}<0
,
\gamma e^{\gamma\tau}
is bounded independently of
\gamma>0
, so
C
can be chosen as claimed.

Because
e^{-\frac{|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}{4}}\leq\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-2\gamma\tau}\big)
on
A
, we can derive the following coarse estimates

(7.39)

\displaystyle|\langle\hat{w},G\rangle_{W}|

\displaystyle\leq e^{(2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}

(7.40)

\displaystyle\|G\|_{W}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{(2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}\,,

valid for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}\ll 0
.

Combining (
7.38
), (
7.39
), (
7.40
) with Corollary
A.7
and with integration by parts (off of the
\nabla^{2}\hat{w}
term), we see that
28
28

28

To handle the error terms arising after integrating by parts, we can use the estimate for
\nabla\mathcal{F}
as well as (
A.12
) in Corollary
A.7
(to control the terms when the derivative hits the Gaussian weight).

(7.41)

|\langle\hat{w},\hat{E}^{w}\rangle_{W}|\leq Ce^{\gamma_{\mu}\tau}\|\hat{w}\|^{2}_{W,1}+e^{(2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}\,.

Thus we can compute

(7.42)

\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\|\hat{w}\|^{2}_{W}=\langle\hat{w},L_{\Sigma}\hat{w}+\hat{E}^{w}\rangle_{W}\leq-\mu\|\hat{w}\|^{2}_{W}+Ce^{\gamma_{\mu}\tau}\|\hat{w}\|^{2}_{W,1}+e^{(2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}\,,

and therefore

(7.43)

\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\big(e^{2\mu\tau}\|\hat{w}\|^{2}_{W}\big)\leq Ce^{(2\mu+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}\|\hat{w}\|^{2}_{W,1}+e^{(2\mu+2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}\,.

We recall from
[
CCMS20
, (5.17)]
the identity

(7.44)

\int_{\Sigma}(\Delta_{\Sigma}\hat{w}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w})^{2}e^{-\tfrac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}d\mathcal{H}^{n}\\
=\int_{\Sigma}(|\nabla^{2}_{\Sigma}\hat{w}|^{2}-A^{2}(\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w},\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w})+\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w}|^{2})\,e^{-\tfrac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}d\mathcal{H}^{n}

and the identity following on that, which in the present situation can be written as

\begin{split}\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\|\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w}\|_{W}^{2}&=-\|\Delta_{\Sigma}\hat{w}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w}\|_{W}^{2}+\|(\tfrac{1}{2}+|A_{\Sigma}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w}\|_{W}^{2}\\
&\qquad+\langle\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w},\hat{w}\,\nabla_{\Sigma}|A_{\Sigma}|^{2}\rangle_{W}-\langle\Delta_{\Sigma}\hat{w}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w},\hat{E}^{w}\rangle_{W}.\end{split}

We can thus estimate, using (
7.38
), (
7.40
), (
7.44
), as follows

(7.45)

\begin{split}\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\|\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w}\|_{W}^{2}&\leq-\tfrac{1}{2}\|\Delta_{\Sigma}\hat{w}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w}\|_{W}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|\hat{E}^{w}\|_{W}^{2}+C\|w\|_{W,1}^{2}\\
&\leq-\tfrac{1}{2}\|\nabla^{2}_{\Sigma}\hat{w}\|_{W}^{2}+2\|\nabla^{2}\hat{w}\cdot\mathcal{F}\|_{W}^{2}+C\|w\|_{W,1}^{2}+e^{(2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}\\
&\leq C\|w\|_{W,1}^{2}+e^{(2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}\end{split}

Alternatively, we can integrate by parts rather than using the spectral estimate in (
7.42
) to find (decreasing
\tau_{0}
if necessary and using (
7.41
))

(7.46)

\begin{split}\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\|\hat{w}\|^{2}_{W}&=-\|\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w}\|_{W}^{2}+\langle\hat{w},(\tfrac{1}{2}+|A_{\Sigma}|^{2})\hat{w}+\hat{E}^{w}\rangle_{W}\\
&\leq-\|\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w}\|_{W}^{2}+C\|\hat{w}\|_{W}^{2}+Ce^{\gamma_{\mu}\tau}\|\hat{w}\|^{2}_{W,1}+e^{(2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}\\
&\leq-\tfrac{1}{2}\|\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w}\|_{W}^{2}+C\|\hat{w}\|_{W}^{2}+e^{(2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}\,.\end{split}

Combining (
7.45
) and (
7.46
) yields

(7.47)

\tfrac{d}{d\tau}(\|\nabla_{\Sigma}\hat{w}\|_{W}^{2}+C\|\hat{w}\|^{2}_{W})\leq C\|\hat{w}\|_{W}^{2}+Ce^{(2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}\leq C\hat{W}(\tau)^{2}+Ce^{(2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}

By (
7.37
) find that

\hat{W}(\tau)^{2}\leq Ce^{2(1+2\alpha)\gamma\tau}\,,

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}\ll 0
, so by integrating (
7.47
), we find

\|\hat{w}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{W,1}\leq C\gamma^{-1}e^{2(1+2\alpha)\gamma\tau}+C\gamma^{-1}e^{(2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}\leq Ce^{2(1+2\alpha)\gamma\tau}\,

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. In the final inequality we used
\gamma_{\mu}>0
and
\gamma^{-1}\leq\gamma_{0}^{-1}<\infty
(where we emphasize that
\gamma_{0}
is fixed in (
7.29
) and thus is independent of
\tau_{0}
).

We can now return to (
7.43
) to find

\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\big(e^{2\mu\tau}\|\hat{w}\|^{2}_{W}\big)\leq Ce^{(2\mu+2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\gamma_{\mu})\tau}\,.

Note that
2\mu+2(1+2\alpha)\gamma\geq\tfrac{\delta_{0}}{4}>0
, so we can integrate this to find

\|\hat{w}(\cdot,\tau)\|^{2}_{W}\leq e^{\big(2(1+2\alpha)\gamma+\frac{\gamma_{\mu}}{2}\big)\tau}

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}\ll 0
. Thus,

W(\tau)\leq e^{\big((1+2\alpha)\gamma+\frac{\gamma_{\mu}}{4}\big)\tau}

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. But this contradicts the extremality of
\gamma
in (
7.27
) (for
\tau_{0}
fixed sufficiently negative so that each step above is justified) and thus
\gamma=\infty
and
W(\tau)\equiv 0
for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
.

In case (2) we can argue as above, replacing
\tau\mapsto\bar{M}(\tau)
by the static flow
\tau\mapsto\Sigma
.
∎

Part II
Application to mean curvature flow in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

8.
Genus drop for ancient one-sided flows in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

In this section we consider
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathcal{S}_{2}^{*}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{\textrm{gen}}_{2}
. In other words, we consider
\Sigma
a non-flat self-shrinker in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
that is not a sphere or cylinder, and
\Omega
a chosen side of
\Sigma
. Recall that by Proposition
3.5
it holds that
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

Consider the (one-sided) ancient weak set flow
\mathcal{K}
and associated Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}
as considered in Proposition
5.6
(cf. Corollary
5.8
). The next lemma follows by combining
[
CM16
, Corollary 1.4]
with Proposition
5.6
.

Lemma 8.1
.

\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
is smooth for a.e.,
t<0
.

In this section we will show that
\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
loses genus as
t\to 0
(recall that Brendle has shown
[
Bre16
]
that
\Sigma
has positive genus). We will later use this in the global perturbation argument to ensure that we only need to perturb the initial conditions finitely many times.

We recall the following definitions:

Definition 8.2
.

If
M
is an oriented compact connected surface without boundary, decomposing
M=\cup_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}
into connected components, then we set

\genus(M):=\sum_{\alpha}\genus(M_{\alpha})=\tfrac{1}{2}b_{1}(M).

Definition 8.3
.

If
M^{2}
is an oriented compact surface with boundary, the genus of
M
is defined to be the genus of the oriented surface
\tilde{M}
formed by capping off each circle in
\partial M
by a disk.

Definition 8.4

(Simple flow
[
CCMS20
, Appendix G]
)
.

For
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
an open set with smooth boundary, a
simple flow
in
U\times I
is a closed subset of space-time
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}
so that there is a compact
2
-manifold with boundary and a continuous map
f:M\times I\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
so that

(1)

\mathcal{M}(t)\cap\overline{U}=f(M\times\{t\})
(where
\mathcal{M}(t)=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:(\mathbf{x},t)\in\mathcal{M}\}
)

(2)

f
is smooth on
M^{\circ}\times I
where
M^{\circ}=M\setminus\partial M

(3)

f(\cdot,t)
,
t\in I
is an embedding of
M^{\circ}
into
U

(4)

t\mapsto f(M^{\circ}\times\{t\})
is a smooth mean curvature flow

(5)

f|_{\partial M\times I}
is a smooth family of embeddings of
\partial M
into
\partial U
.

We can now state the main result.

Proposition 8.5

(Genus drop for one-sided flows in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
)
.

Let
(\Sigma,\Omega)
,
\mathcal{K}
be as above. There is
t_{0}<t_{1}<0
,
\kappa_{0}\in(0,t_{1}-t_{0})
,
\kappa_{1}\in(0,-t_{1})
and
R>0
with the following properties.

(1)

for any
t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\kappa_{0}]
so that
\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
is smooth,
\genus(\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))>0
,

(2)

for any
t\in[t_{1},t_{1}+\kappa_{1}]
so that
\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
is smooth,
\genus(\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))=0
,

(3)

t\mapsto\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
is a simple flow in
\{R/2<|\mathbf{x}|<3R\}\times[t_{0},t_{1}+\kappa_{1}]
,

(4)

and
for
(\mathbf{x},t)\in\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\cap\{R/2<|\mathbf{x}|<3R\}\times[t_{0},t_{1}+\kappa_{1}]
, we have
|\mathbf{x}^{T}|\geq\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{x}|
, for
\mathbf{x}^{T}
the projection of
\mathbf{x}
to
T_{\mathbf{x}}\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
.

Remark
.

We first sketch the idea of the proof. For simplicity let us assume that
\Sigma
is compact (in reality one must be somewhat careful with the cylindrical ends, but the main idea is the same). Write
\Omega(\tau)
for the inside of the rescaled one-sided flow (so
\Omega(\tau)\subset\Omega(\tau^{\prime})\subset\Omega
for
\tau>\tau^{\prime}
). Suppose that
\partial\Omega(\tau)
has non-zero genus for all
\tau
large. Note that
d(\Sigma,\Omega(\tau))
is exponentially increasing as
\tau\to\infty
(since the distance between the non-rescaled flows is non-decreasing).

Thus, the non-trivial situation is when
\Omega
is the unbounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\Sigma
. If
\partial\Omega(\tau)
has positive genus, then by a result in algebraic topology (cf. Lemma
F.4
below), there is a non-zero element
[\sigma]\in H_{1}(\Omega(\tau))
. Because the flows are shrinker mean convex, we find
\sigma\subset\Omega
and
d(\sigma,\Sigma)
is arbitrarily large. However, it is easy to see that when
d(\sigma,\Sigma)
is sufficiently large, then
[\sigma]=0\in H_{1}(\Omega)
. This contradicts White’s topological monotonicity result
[
Whi95
, Theorem 1(iii)]
, which says that a
1
-cycle in the complement of a (
2
-dimensional) weak set flow cannot suddenly cease to bound a
2
-chain.

Remark
.

The argument used here avoids the need for a potentially delicate analysis of the star-shaped properties of the flow (along cylindrical ends) near
t=0
(cf.
[
CCMS20
, Theorem 9.1]
).

The following is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.2
.

Lemma 8.6
.

We can take
R_{0}
sufficiently large so that for
R\geq R_{0}
,
\Sigma
intersects
\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
transversely and
\Sigma\setminus B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
(resp.
\Omega\setminus B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
) is diffeomorphic to
\Sigma\cap\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})\times[R,\infty)
(resp.
\Omega\cap\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})\times[R,\infty)
).

Note that
\Sigma\cap\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
is a finite union of pairwise disjoint embedded loops in
\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})\approx\mathbb{S}^{2}
and
\Omega\cap\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
is a finite union of regions in
\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
bounded by said loops.

Lemma 8.7
.

There is
r>0
and
s_{1}<0
so that
\mathcal{K}(t)\cap B_{r}(\mathbf{0})=\emptyset
for
t\in[s_{1},0)
.

Proof.

This follows from (1) in Proposition
5.7
.
∎

As such, taking
t_{1}\in[s_{1},0)
sufficiently close to
0
, we find that for all
t\in[t_{1},0)
,

(8.1)

\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\mathcal{K}(t)\cap B_{R_{0}}(\mathbf{0})=\emptyset,

wheres
R_{0}
is fixed in Lemma
8.6
above. This fixes
t_{1}
. Now we fix an arbitrary
\kappa_{1}\in(t_{1},0)
.

Lemma 8.8
.

There is
t_{0}<t_{1}
sufficiently negative,
\kappa_{0}\in(0,t_{1}-t_{0})
and
R_{1}\geq R_{0}
so that for any
R\geq R_{1}
and
t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\kappa_{0})
,
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
is a smooth surface meeting
\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
transversally with
\genus(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))>0
and
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\setminus B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
is diffeomorphic to
(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\cap\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))\times[R,\infty)

Proof.

Combine Lemmas
F.3
and
8.6
, property (4) in Proposition
5.6
, and
[
Bre16
, Theorem 2]
.
∎

Lemma 8.9
.

There is
R_{2}\geq R_{1}
sufficiently large so that:

(1)

For
R\geq R_{2}
and
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}+\kappa_{1}]
we have that
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
is smooth in a neighborhood of
\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
and these surfaces have transverse intersection.

(2)

For any
t>t_{0}
, each component of
\overline{(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\mathcal{K}(t)\setminus\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t_{0}}}\mathcal{K}(t_{0}))}\cap\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})

is an annulus.

Proof.

By (3) in Proposition
5.6
there is
R(t)
monotonically increasing on
(-\infty,0)
so that

\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\setminus B_{R(t)}(\mathbf{0})

is a smooth strictly shrinker mean convex flow that can be written as a small
C^{3}
-graph over a subset of
\Sigma
. Take
R_{2}>\max_{t\in[t_{0},t_{1}+\kappa_{1}]}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}R(t)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-(t_{1}+\kappa_{1})}}R(t_{1}+\kappa_{1})
. This yields (1). To prove (2), we combine the above reasoning with the geometric reformulation of shrinker mean convexity:
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\mathcal{K}(t)\subset\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-s}}\mathcal{K}(s)
for
t>s
.
∎

Loosely speaking, the next lemma shows that if for a time
t
close to zero
(-t)^{-1/2}\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
still has genus in a large ball, then this can be detected by a homologically nontrivial loop in
(-t)^{-1/2}\mathcal{K}(t)
(relative to the homology created by the ends of the shrinker) that was trivial in the ancient past. Note that we must take care to avoid loops that are homologically nontrivial only because they wrap around the ends of the shrinker/flow.

Lemma 8.10
.

Suppose that for some
t\in[t_{1},t_{1}+\kappa_{1}]
and
R\geq R_{2}
it holds that
\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
is smooth and
\genus(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))>0
. Then, there is a loop
\sigma\subset\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\mathcal{K}(t)\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
so that

•

for any
1
-cycle
\beta\subset\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})\cap\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\mathcal{K}(t)
, it holds that
[\sigma]\neq[\beta]
in
H_{1}(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\mathcal{K}(t)\cap\overline{B_{R}(\mathbf{0})};\mathbb{Z})
, but

•

there is a
1
-cycle
\beta\subset\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})\cap\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t_{0}}}\mathcal{K}(t_{0})
so that
[\sigma]=[\beta]
in
H_{1}(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t_{0}}}\mathcal{K}(t_{0})\cap\overline{B_{R}(\mathbf{0})};\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
F.5
to the set
\tilde{\Omega}
formed by smoothing out the corners of
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\mathcal{K}(t)\cap B_{R+\varepsilon}(\mathbf{0})
and ball
B=B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
yields
\sigma\subset\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\mathcal{K}(t)\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
satisfying the first bullet point (since

\genus(\partial\tilde{\Omega}\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))>0

by assumption).

Now, we observe that by shrinker mean convexity and (
8.1
)

\sigma\subset\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t_{0}}}\mathcal{K}(t_{0})\cap(B_{R}(\mathbf{0})\setminus B_{R_{0}}(\mathbf{0})).

By Lemma
8.8
we have

\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t_{0}}}\mathcal{K}(t_{0})\cap(\overline{B_{R}(\mathbf{0})}\setminus B_{R_{0}}(\mathbf{0}))

deformation retracts to

\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t_{0}}}\mathcal{K}(t_{0})\cap\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})

so the second bullet point follows.
∎

Lemma 8.11
.

Recall that
\partial\Omega=\Sigma
. For some
R\geq R_{2}
fixed, suppose that there is a loop
\sigma\subset\Omega\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
and a
1
-cycle
\beta\subset\Omega\cap\partial B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
so that
[\sigma]=[\beta]
in
H_{1}(\Omega\cap\overline{B_{R}(\mathbf{0})};\mathbb{Z})
. Then, there is a connected smooth embedded self-shrinker
\Gamma\subset\Omega
with
\partial\Gamma=\sigma
. Moreover,

\sqrt{0}\Gamma\cap\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0})\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\sqrt{0}\Sigma\cap\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0})

are disjoint.

Proof.

As in
[
Bre16
, Proposition 12]
, we can choose a sequence of Riemannian metrics
g_{k}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
converging in
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
to
e^{-\frac{|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}{2n}}g_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}
so that
\Omega\cap B_{2k}(\mathbf{0})
is (weakly) shrinker mean convex in the sense of Meeks–Yau
[
MY82
, §1]
. By Lemma
8.6
, we can choose
\beta_{k}\subset\Omega\cap\partial B_{2k}(\mathbf{0})
homologous to
\beta
in
\Omega
.

Let
\Gamma_{k}
minimize
g_{k}
-area among oriented embedded surfaces
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\Omega
with
\partial\Gamma^{\prime}=\sigma-\beta_{k}
. Using standard interior regularity for minimizers (and
[
HS79
]
) we can pass a subsequence and obtain a connected smooth embedded self-shrinker with
\Gamma\subset\Omega
(thanks to the strong maximum principle) and
\partial\Gamma=\sigma
. The statement about the asymptotic cones follows from Ilmanen’s avoidance principle (Theorem
D.1
) as in Lemma
6.4
. Indeed, for
t<0
,
t\mapsto\sqrt{-t}\Gamma,t\mapsto\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
are disjoint mean curvature flows in any small ball around any point in
\partial B_{1}(\mathbf{0})
. Applying Ilmanen’s avoidance principle, we find that they remain a definite distance apart at
t=0
(in a smaller ball). This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 8.12
.

Fix
\sigma,\Gamma
as in Lemma
8.11
. Assume that
\sigma\subset\frac{1}{\sqrt{-t^{\prime}}}\mathcal{K}(t^{\prime})
for some
t^{\prime}<0
. Then
\Gamma\subset\frac{1}{\sqrt{-t^{\prime}}}\mathcal{K}(t^{\prime})

Proof.

Combining Lemma
8.11
with the sublinear growth estimates in Proposition
7.3
applied to
\mathcal{M}(t)
(recall that
\mathcal{M}(t)
is the one-sided Brakke flow with
\supp\mathcal{M}(t)=\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
), we have that

d((\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\partial\mathcal{K}(t))\setminus B_{R}(\mathbf{0}),\Gamma\setminus B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))\to\infty

as
R\to\infty
, uniformly for
t\leq t^{\prime}
. Using this, together with the fact that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\mathcal{K}(t)
exhausts
\Omega
as
t\to-\infty
and the avoidance principle, we find that
(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\partial\mathcal{K}(t))\cap\Gamma=\emptyset
for all
t\leq t^{\prime}
. This proves the assertion (because
\Gamma
is connected, if there was a point in
\Gamma\setminus\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\mathcal{K}(t)
then there would be a point in
\Gamma\cap\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
).
∎

With these preparations we can now prove the genus drop result.

Proof of Proposition
8.5
.

Fix
t_{1},\kappa_{1}
as in (
8.1
) and
t_{0},\kappa_{0}
as in Lemma
8.8
. Then, as long as we fix
R\geq\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-(t_{1}+\kappa_{1})}}R_{2}
sufficiently large, we can ensure that (3) and (4) hold by using stability of cylinders (Lemma
5.4
) and pseudolocality (along the conical ends).

Property (1) follows from Lemma
8.8
(with
\sqrt{-t}R\geq R_{1}
in place of
R
). To prove property (2), we assume that for some
t\in[t_{1},t_{1}+\kappa_{1}]
,
\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
is smooth and
\genus(\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))>0
. Hence,

\genus(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\cap B_{R^{\prime}}(\mathbf{0}))>0

for
R^{\prime}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}R\geq R_{2}
. Fix
\sigma
as in Lemma
8.10
(with
R^{\prime}
in place of
R
) and then
\Gamma
as in Lemma
8.11
. By Corollary
8.12
(with
t=t^{\prime}
) we find that

\Gamma\subset\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\mathcal{K}(t).

We can perturb
\Gamma
slightly to find a surface with
\Gamma\subset\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\mathcal{K}(t)
and
\Gamma
transversal to
\partial B_{R^{\prime}}(\mathbf{0})
. This yields

\sigma=\partial(\Gamma\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))-\beta

for some
\beta\subset(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-t}}\mathcal{K}(t))\cap\partial B_{R^{\prime}}(\mathbf{0})
. This contradicts the first bullet point in Lemma
8.10
, completing the proof.
∎

9.
The first non-generic time for flows in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

Definition 9.1

(Generic singularities and first non-generic time)
.

Consider
\mathcal{M}
a cyclic integral unit-regular Brakke flow of surfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

•

We define
\sing_{\textnormal{gen}}\mathcal{M}\subset\sing\mathcal{M}
to be the set of singular points for
\mathcal{M}
so that one tangent flow (and thus all
[
CM15
]
) is a multiplicity-one shrinking sphere or cylinder.

•

Then, we define
T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}):=\inf\mathfrak{t}(\sing\mathcal{M}\setminus\sing_{\textrm{gen}}\mathcal{M})
, i.e., the first time that there is a “non-generic” singularity.

•

Finally, we set
\mathfrak{S}_{\textrm{first}}(\mathcal{M}):=(\sing\mathcal{M}\setminus\sing_{\textrm{gen}}\mathcal{M})\cap\{t=T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})\}
, i.e., this is the set of “non-generic” singularities at the first time that they exist.

The following is our main result.

Theorem 9.2
.

Suppose that
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a closed embedded surface. Then, there exist arbitrarily small
C^{\infty}
graphs
M^{\prime}
over
M
and corresponding cyclic integral unit-regular Brakke flows
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(0)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M^{\prime}
so that either:

(1)

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
has only generic singularities:
\sing\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sing_{\textnormal{gen}}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, or

(2)

multiplicity occurs: there is
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
so that any tangent flow to
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
at the space-time point
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}))
is
k\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
for
k\geq 2
and
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
a smooth shrinker.

Compare with
[
CCMS20
, Theorem 11.1]
which also left open the possibility that a non-cylindrical shrinker with a cylindrical end could occur. The proof here follows a similar strategy (granted the stronger one-sided uniqueness result Theorem
7.22
) but differs in some minor, but technically important ways.

In this section we will establish various preliminary results that lead up to the proof of Theorem
9.2
.

Lemma 9.3
.

If
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
are cyclic integral unit-regular Brakke flows in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\mathcal{M}(0)=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(0)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M
for a closed embedded surface
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
then
T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M})=T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{M}(t)=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(t)
for
t<T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M})
, and
\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})=\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By definition of
T_{\textnormal{gen}}
and
[
CCMS20
, Corollary F.5]
, we find that
\reg\mathcal{M}\cap\{t<T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})\}
is connected. Thus, the assertion follows from unit-regularity and the resolution of the mean convex neighborhood conjecture
[
CHH22
, Theorem 1.9]
(cf.
[
HW20
]
).
∎

Definition 9.4
.

Consider
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
a closed embedded surface. Let
U
denote the unique compact region bounded by
M
. Write
M(t)
for the level set flow of
M
and
U(t)
for the level set flow of
U
.

Consider a cyclic integral unit-regular Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\mathcal{M}(0)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M
. We say
the set of times
\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{reg}}(\mathcal{M})
is the complement of the set of singular times
\mathfrak{t}(\sing\mathcal{M})
. Note that by upper semi-continuity of density
\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{reg}}(\mathcal{M})
is open. Furthermore, for
t\in\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{reg}}(\mathcal{M})
the level set flow
M(t)
of
M
at time
t
is a smooth embedded surface
M(t)
with
M(t)=\partial U(t)
and
\mathcal{M}(t)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M(t)
.

The next result follows from combining several important results in the literature (cf.
[
CCMS20
, Proposition 11.3]
).

Proposition 9.5
.

Consider a cyclic integral unit-regular Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\mathcal{M}(0)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M
for a closed embedded surface
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Write
U
for the unique compact set bounded by
M
and
U(t)
for the level set flow of
U
. Then:

•

the level set flow
M(t)
of
M
does not fatten before
T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M})
,

•

almost every
t\in[0,T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M})]
is a regular time for the flow, and

•

t\mapsto\genus(M(t))
is non-increasing for
t\in\mathcal{T}_{\textnormal{reg}}
.

Now, assume that
T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M})<\infty
. Then:

•

Every tangent flow at
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\in\sing\mathcal{M}
is of the following form for
t<0

t\mapsto k\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma

where
k\in\mathbb{N}
is the multiplicity and
\Sigma
a smooth embedded self-shrinker and

•

the self-shrinker
\Sigma
in the previous bullet point satisfies

\genus(\Sigma)\leq\lim_{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{reg}}\ni t\nearrow T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M})}\genus(M(t)).

and
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
has nice stable ends.

Finally, assume that for some point
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\in\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
there is some tangent flow of the form
t\mapsto\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
, i.e., the tangent flow is multiplicity one. Then:

•

all tangent flows at
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))
occur with multiplicity-one—possibly with a different
\Sigma
,

•

\genus(\Sigma)\geq 1
, and

•

F(\Sigma)\geq\lambda_{1}+\delta_{0}
for some numerical constant
\delta_{0}
.

Proof.

By
[
CHH22
, Theorem 1.9]
the level set flow of
M
does not fatten before
T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})
. Hence, by
[
CM16
, Corollary 1.4]
(and
[
CCMS20
, Corollary F.5]
), we have that
M(t)
is smooth and
\mathcal{M}(t)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M(t)
for almost every
t\in[0,T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})]
. Combined with
[
HW20
, Theorems 3.1, B.2, and C.1]
, this implies that almost every
t\in[0,T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})]
is a regular time for the flow. Now, by
[
Whi95
, Theorem 1]
, we have that
t\mapsto\genus(M(t))
is non-increasing for
t\in\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{reg}}
. This verifies the first set of bullet points.

The second set of bullet points follows from a straightforward adaptation of
[
Ilm95
]
to avoid the singular time-slices (for
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
, see Proposition
3.5
). Finally, for the third set of bullet points, the first one is proven in Proposition
H.4
. The second follows from
[
Bre16
, Theorem 2]
and the third from
[
BW17
, Corollary 1.2]
. This completes the proof.
∎

It is convenient to define the quantity

\genus_{T_{\textnormal{gen}}}(\mathcal{M}):=\lim_{\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{reg}}\ni t\nearrow T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M})}\genus(M(t)).

We will refer to this below. Note that by Proposition
9.5
we have
\genus_{T_{\textnormal{gen}}}(\mathcal{M})\leq\genus M
. Moreover, by Lemma
9.3
and Proposition
9.5
we find that the quantities
T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})
and
\genus_{T_{\textnormal{gen}}}(\mathcal{M})
depend only on the initial surface
M
, not the chosen Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}
(by
[
HW18
, Theorem B.6]
a flow
\mathcal{M}
as above always exists). As such, we will sometimes write
T_{\textrm{gen}}(M)
and
\genus_{T_{\textnormal{gen}}}(M)
.

The following lemma will be important later.

Lemma 9.6
.

Consider
\mathcal{M}
a cyclic integral unit-regular Brakke flow in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\mathcal{M}(0)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M
a closed embedded surface
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Assume that
T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M})<\infty
and at any
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\in\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
, all tangent flows occur with multiplicity one. Then
\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
is a non-zero finite set.

Proof.

It is clear that
\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
is non-zero by definition of the first non-generic time. We claim that
\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
is closed. Indeed, if a sequence
(\mathbf{x}_{j},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\in\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
has
(\mathbf{x}_{j},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\to(\mathbf{x}_{\infty},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))
we note that

\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{x}_{\infty},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\geq\limsup_{j\to\infty}\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{x}_{\infty},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\geq\lambda_{1}+\delta_{0}

by upper-semicontinuity of density and the final bullet point in Proposition
9.5
. This implies that
(\mathbf{x}_{\infty},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\in\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
.

We now claim that
\mathfrak{S}_{\textrm{first}}(\mathcal{M})
is discrete. (This will finish the proof.) Indeed, if
(\mathbf{x}_{j},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\to(\mathbf{x}_{\infty},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))
are distinct elements of
\mathfrak{S}_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})
we can rescale
\mathcal{M}
around
(\mathbf{x}_{\infty},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))
by
|\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{\infty}|^{-1}
to yield
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{j}
. Note that up to a subsequence,
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{j}
converges to a tangent flow
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
to
\mathcal{M}
at
(\mathbf{x}_{\infty},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))
. By Proposition
9.5
and the multiplicity one assumption,
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\Sigma
for some
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
. Rescaling the points
\mathbf{x}_{j}
and passing to the limit we find
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
|\tilde{\mathbf{x}}|=1
and
\Theta_{\tilde{\mathcal{M}}}(\mathbf{x},0)\geq\lambda_{1}+\delta_{0}
(by upper-semicontinuity of density and the final bullet point in Proposition
9.5
). This contradicts the fact that all tangent flows to
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
at
(\mathbf{x},0)\neq(\mathbf{0},0)
are multiplicity one cylinders or planes. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 9.7
.

Consider a cyclic integral unit-regular Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\mathcal{M}(0)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M
for a closed embedded surface
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Write
U
for the unique compact region bounded by
M
and choose the inwards pointing unit normal. Assume that
T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M})<\infty
and at any
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\in\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
, all tangent flows occur with multiplicity one. If
M_{j}
is a sequence of normal graphs of functions
0<u_{j}
over
M
with
u_{j}\to 0
in
C^{\infty}(M)
, then

\limsup_{j\to\infty}\genus_{T_{\textnormal{gen}}}(M_{j})\leq\genus_{T_{\textnormal{gen}}}(M)-1.

Remark
.

This result would also hold (with the same proof) if we assumed that
0>u_{j}
.

Before proving Proposition
9.7
, we observe that it immediately implies Theorem
9.2
after finitely many iterations.

Proof of Theorem
9.2
using Proposition
9.7
.

For
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
a closed embedded surface, fix (using
[
HW18
, Theorem B.6]
) a cyclic integral unit-regular Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathcal{M}(0)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M
. If
T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})=\infty
then
\sing\mathcal{M}=\sing_{\textrm{gen}}\mathcal{M}
so we’re in case (1) of Theorem
9.2
with
M^{\prime}=M
(i.e. no perturbation is necessary). If
T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})<\infty
, then either it holds that all
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\in\mathcal{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
have some tangent flow with multiplicity
k=1
or some
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\in\mathcal{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
has only tangent flows with multiplicity
k\geq 2
. In the latter case, we’re in case (2) of Theorem
9.2
(again with
M^{\prime}=M
).

It thus remains to consider the case where
T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})<\infty
and at all
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\in\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
, some tangent flows occur with multiplicity-one. By Proposition
9.5
(the “…Finally,” bullet points) it holds that at all
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\in\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
, all tangent flows occur with multiplicity-one.

Note also that by Proposition
9.5
we see that
\genus_{T_{\textrm{gen}}}(M)\geq 1
. Using Proposition
9.7
we can thus find
M^{\prime}
a (small) normal graph over
M
with

\genus_{T_{\textrm{gen}}}(M^{\prime})\leq\genus_{T_{\textrm{gen}}}(M)-1.

Now, by the argument in the previous paragraph if
M^{\prime}
fails to satisfy the desired properties in Theorem
9.2
for some (and thus any) cyclic unit-regular integral Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(0)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M^{\prime}
it holds that
T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})<\infty
and at all
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}))\in\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
, all tangent flows occur with multiplicity one. We can thus apply Proposition
9.7
with
M^{\prime}
in place of
M
. Note that at each step the genus at the non-generic time must strictly decrease. As such, the perturbations must eventually satisfy the asserted properties in Theorem
9.2
. (By choosing all perturbations sufficiently small, we can arrange that the final surface is a small graph over
M
.) This completes the proof.
∎

The remainder of this section is thus devoted to the proof of Proposition
9.7
. Below we fix a cyclic integral unit-regular Brakke flow
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\mathcal{M}(0)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M
for a closed embedded surface
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Assume that
T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M})<\infty
and at any
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\in\mathfrak{S}_{\textnormal{first}}(\mathcal{M})
, all tangent flows occur with multiplicity one.

Consider
M_{j}
is a sequence of normal graphs of functions
0<u_{j}
over
M
with
u_{j}\to 0
in
C^{\infty}(M)
and fix cyclic integral unit-regular Brakke flows
\mathcal{M}_{j}
with
\mathcal{M}_{j}(0)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M_{j}
. Using Lemma
9.3
it suffices to assume (after passing to a subsequence and replacing
\mathcal{M}
by the limiting Brakke flow) that
\mathcal{M}_{j}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{M}
.

Define

(9.1)

d_{j}:=\max_{(\mathbf{x},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\in\mathfrak{S}_{\textrm{first}}(\mathcal{M})}d((\mathbf{x},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})),\supp\mathcal{M}_{j})

(using the parabolic distance). Note that
0<d_{j}=o(1)
. (Lemma
9.6
implies that
\mathfrak{S}_{\textrm{first}}(\mathcal{M})
is a finite set.)

Before proving Proposition
9.7
we first show that the first non-generic time does not significantly decrease under the perturbation.

Lemma 9.8
.

For
M,M_{j},\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{j}
as above we have
T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}_{j})\geq T_{\textnormal{gen}}(M)-o(d_{j}^{2})
as
j\to\infty
.

Proof.

Passing to a subsequence, we can assume for contradiction that there is some
\eta>0
so that

T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}_{j})\leq T_{\textnormal{gen}}(M)-\eta d_{j}^{2}

By stability of cylindrical (generic) singularities (this follows from upper-semicontinuity of density and the final bullet point in Proposition
9.5
) we see that in fact

(9.2)

T_{\textnormal{gen}}(M)-o(1)\leq T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}_{j})\leq T_{\textnormal{gen}}(M)-\eta d_{j}^{2}

as
j\to\infty
. By translating in space-time and using (
9.2
) we can assume that
T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})=0
and that there is
(\mathbf{x}_{j},T_{j})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\textrm{first}}(\mathcal{M}_{j})
with
T_{j}=T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}_{j})
so that
(\mathbf{x}_{j},T_{j})\to(\mathbf{0},0)\in\mathfrak{S}_{\textrm{first}}(\mathcal{M})
but

(9.3)

T_{j}\leq-\eta d_{j}^{2}.

Now, rescale
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{j},(\mathbf{x}_{j},T_{j})
around the space-time origin to
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{j},\check{\mathcal{M}}_{j},(\check{\mathbf{x}}_{j},\check{T}_{j})
so that
|(\check{\mathbf{x}}_{j},\check{T}_{j})|=1
. Passing to a further subsequence, we assume that
