\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\rightharpoonup\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
,
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\rightharpoonup\check{\mathcal{M}}
,
(\check{\mathbf{x}}_{j},\check{T}_{j})\to(\check{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\check{T}_{0})
. Note that
\check{T}_{0}\leq 0
and
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}(t)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
for some
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
.

We claim that is
\check{\mathcal{M}}
either
\check{\mathcal{M}}(t)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
for all
t<0
or
\check{\mathcal{M}}
is (up to a parabolic dilation around the origin) the ancient flow constructed in Proposition
5.6
. We will verify this claim below but first will show how it completes the proof. By upper-semicontinuity of density and the final bullet point in Proposition
9.5
we see that
\Theta_{\check{\mathcal{M}}}(\check{\mathbf{x}}_{0},\check{T}_{0})\geq\lambda_{1}+\delta_{0}
. Thus, the first case (
\check{\mathcal{M}}(t)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
) cannot occur, since
\check{T}_{0}\leq 0
, so we find that
\check{\mathcal{M}}
is a parabolic dilation of the ancient flow constructed in Proposition
5.6
. By (4) in Proposition
5.8
we see that
\check{T}_{0}=0
. By the scaling invariance of (
9.3
),
\check{T}_{j}\to 0
and the definition of
d_{j}
we see that

d((\mathbf{0},0),\supp\check{\mathcal{M}}_{j})\to 0

and thus
d((\mathbf{0},0),\supp\check{\mathcal{M}})=0
. This contradicts (1) in Proposition
5.7
.

We now prove the claim about
\check{\mathcal{M}}
. We can choose an open set
\Omega
so that
\partial\Omega=\Sigma
and
\supp\check{\mathcal{M}}(t)\subset\sqrt{-t}\overline{\Omega}
(to see that
\check{\mathcal{M}}
is contained to one side of
\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
we can use the fact that the level set flow of the inside
U_{j}
of
M_{j}
contains the level set flow
U
, the inside of
M
). We claim that

(9.4)

\lambda(\check{\mathcal{M}})\leq F(\Sigma).

Indeed, we can choose
(\mathbf{y}_{j},s_{j})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}
with
(\mathbf{y}_{j},s_{j})\to(\mathbf{0},0)
and
r_{j}\to 0
and

\Theta_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}((\mathbf{y}_{j},s_{j}),r_{j})=\lambda(\check{\mathcal{M}})+o(1)\,.

For
r>0
fixed, we can take
j
large and apply Huisken’s monotonicity to yield

\Theta_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}((\mathbf{y}_{j},s_{j}),r)\geq\Theta_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}((\mathbf{y}_{j},s_{j}),r_{j})=\lambda(\check{\mathcal{M}})+o(1).

Sending
j\to\infty
, we find

\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}((\mathbf{0},0),r)\geq\lambda(\check{\mathcal{M}}).

Then, sending
r\to 0
, we obtain (
9.4
).

Since
\supp\check{\mathcal{M}}(t)\subset\sqrt{-t}\overline{\Omega}
we find that any tangent flow to
\check{\mathcal{M}}
at
-\infty
is associated to an integral unit-regular varifold shrinker (
F
-stationary varifold)
V
with
\supp V\subset\overline{\Omega}
. The Frankel property for shrinkers (Corollary
D.2
) implies that
\supp V\cap\Sigma\not=\emptyset
. Then the strong maximum principle for stationary varifolds
[
SW89
,
Ilm96
]
(either applies since
\Sigma
is smooth) gives
V=m\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\Sigma
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
. The entropy bound (
9.4
) then implies that
m=1
.

We can thus apply the global uniqueness result Theorem
7.22
to see that either
\check{\mathcal{M}}(t)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
for all
t<0
or
\check{\mathcal{M}}
is (up to a parabolic dilation around the origin) the ancient flow constructed in Proposition
5.6
. This proves the claim and thus completes the proof.
∎

We now complete the proof of Theorem
9.2
by establishing Proposition
9.7
.

Proof of Proposition
9.7
.

Recall that
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a closed embedded surface and
M_{j}
are the normal graphs of functions
0<u_{j}
with
u_{j}\to 0
in
C^{\infty}(M)
. Write
U_{j}
for the unique compact region bounded by
M
and let
U_{j}(t)
denote the level set flow of
U_{j}
. Write
\mathcal{U}_{j}
for the space-time track of
U_{j}(t)
.

We have fixed cyclic integral unit-regular Brakke flows
\mathcal{M}_{j}
with
\mathcal{M}_{j}(0)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M_{j}
and so that
\mathcal{M}_{j}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{M}
a cyclic integral unit-regular Brakke flow with
\mathcal{M}(0)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M
and so that all tangent flows to
\mathcal{M}
at time
T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})
occur with multiplicity one.

By Lemma
9.6
, the set
\mathfrak{S}_{\textrm{first}}(\mathcal{M})
is finite. Thus, by passing to a subsequence and recalling the definition of
d_{j}
in (
9.1
) we can translate
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{j}
by the same fixed space-time vector to assume that
T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M})=0
and

d_{j}=d((\mathbf{0},0),\supp\mathcal{M}_{j})

(i.e., that the maximum in
d_{j}
is achieved at the space-time origin). Define
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{j},\check{\mathcal{M}}_{j}
by parabolically dilating around the space-time origin by
d_{j}^{-1}
. Exactly as in the proof of Lemma
9.8
we find that (after passing to a subsequence)
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{j}
limits to
\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
and
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\rightharpoonup\check{\mathcal{M}}
a parabolic dilation of the ancient flow constructed in Proposition
5.6
. (Note that the other possibility considered in the proof of Lemma
9.8
, namely
\check{\mathcal{M}}=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
cannot occur since
d((\mathbf{0},0),\supp\check{\mathcal{M}}_{j})=1
by the choice of rescaling.)

Because the ancient flow
\check{\mathcal{M}}=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
is a parabolic dilation of the flow considered in Section
8
, we can apply Proposition
8.5
to find
t_{0}<t_{1}<0
and
\kappa_{0}\in(0,t_{1}-t_{0}),\kappa_{1}\in(0,-t_{1})
and
R>0
so that

(1)

for any
t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\kappa_{0}]
so that
\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
is smooth,
\genus(\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))>0
,

(2)

for any
t\in[t_{1},t_{1}+\kappa_{1}]
so that
\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
is smooth,
\genus(\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))=0
,

(3)

t\mapsto\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
is a simple flow in
\{R/2<|\mathbf{x}|<3R\}\times[t_{0},t_{1}+\kappa_{1}]
,

(4)

and
for
(\mathbf{x},t)\in\partial\mathcal{K}(t)\cap\{R/2<|\mathbf{x}|<3R\}\times[t_{0},t_{1}+\kappa_{1}]
, we have
|\mathbf{x}^{T}|\geq\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{x}|
, for
\mathbf{x}^{T}
the projection of
\mathbf{x}
to
T_{\mathbf{x}}\partial\mathcal{K}(t)
.

(Note that the various constants will be changed by the parabolic dilation, but once this is taken into account they will be fixed with the above properties and will not depend on
j
. This is all we will need later.) Note that by Lemma
9.8
and choice of translation/rescaling we have
T_{\textrm{gen}}(\check{\mathcal{M}}_{j})\geq-o(1)
. In particular, for
j
sufficiently large,
T_{\textrm{gen}}(\check{\mathcal{M}}_{j})\geq t_{1}+\kappa_{1}
.

By unit-regularity and (3) and (4) above, we conclude that for
j
sufficiently large,

(9.5)

\check{\mathcal{M}}_{j}
is a simple flow in
\{3R/4<|\mathbf{x}|<2R\}\times[t_{0}+\kappa_{0}/2,t_{1}+\kappa_{1}/2]
.

Furthermore, by (1) (and (6) in Proposition
5.8
combined with unit-regularity) there is
t_{0,j}\in[t_{0}+\kappa_{0}/2,t_{0}+3\kappa_{0}/4]\cap\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{reg}}(\check{\mathcal{M}}_{j})
with

(9.6)

\genus(\check{M}_{j}(t_{0,j})\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))>0.

Similarly, by (2) there is
t_{1,j}\in[t_{1}+\kappa_{1}/2,t_{1}+3\kappa_{1}/4]\cap\mathcal{T}_{\textrm{reg}}(\check{\mathcal{M}}_{j})
with

(9.7)

\genus(\check{M}_{j}(t_{1,j})\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))=0.

Discarding finitely many terms we can ensure (thanks to
\mathcal{M}_{j}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{M}
and the monotonicity of genus) that for all
j
, it holds that

g_{0,j}:=\genus({\check{M}_{j}(t_{0,j})})\leq\genus_{T_{\textrm{gen}}}(M).

Defining
g_{1,j}:=\genus(\check{M}_{j}(t_{1,j}))
, we claim that

(9.8)

g_{1,j}\leq g_{0,j}-1

for
j
sufficiently large. Thanks to (1)-(4) above, the proof of (
9.8
) is now completed exactly as in
[
CCMS20
, Proposition 11.4]
by using the localized topological monotonicity results proven in
[
CCMS20
, Appendix G]
.

For the reader’s convenience we recall the proof here. If (
9.8
) fails, we can take
j
sufficiently large so that (
9.6
) and (
9.7
) hold but

g_{0,j}=g_{1,j}:=g

For this
j
fixed, we write
W_{j}[t]:=(\check{U}_{j}(t))^{c}
and
W_{j}[a,b]:=\check{\mathcal{U}}_{j}^{c}\cap\mathfrak{t}^{-1}([a,b])
(where
\check{\mathcal{U}}_{j}
is the space-time track of the level set flow of
U_{j}
translated in space-time and then parabolically rescaled by
d_{j}^{-1}
as above and
\check{U}_{j}(t)
is the time
t
slice.)

Fix
B=B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
and recall (
9.5
). By Lemmas
F.4
and
F.5
and (
9.7
) we can choose loops

\gamma_{1}^{(1)},\dots,\gamma_{g}^{(1)}\subset W_{j}[t_{1,j}]

so that

\{[\gamma_{1}^{(1)}],\dots,[\gamma_{g}^{(1)}]\}\subset H_{1}(W_{j}[t_{1,j}],\mathbb{Z})

is linearly independent and each
\gamma_{j}^{(1)}
satisfies one of the following:

•

\gamma_{j}^{(1)}
is contained in
\overline{B}^{c}
, or

•

there is some component
\mathcal{B}_{j}^{(1)}
of
\partial B\cap W_{j}[t_{1,j}]
that has non-zero mod-2 intersection with
\gamma_{j}^{(1)}
and zero mod-2 intersection with each previous
\gamma_{i}^{(1)}
,
i<j
.

(No
\gamma_{j}^{(1)}
is contained in
B
, thanks to (
9.7
).) By
[
Whi95
, Theorem 6.2]
we have that

\{[\gamma_{1}^{(1)}],\dots,[\gamma_{g}^{(1)}]\}\subset H_{1}(W_{j}[t_{0,j},t_{1,j}],\mathbb{Z})

is linearly independent. We now choose loops
\gamma_{1}^{(0)},\dots,\gamma_{g}^{(0)}\subset W_{j}[t_{0,j}]
so that

•

Each
\gamma_{j}^{(1)}
is homotopic to
\gamma_{j}^{(0)}
in
W_{j}[t_{0,j},t_{1,j}]
; see
[
Whi95
, Theorem 5.4]
.

•

If
\gamma^{(1)}_{j}
is contained in
\overline{B}^{c}
then so is
\gamma^{(0)}_{j}
(in fact, so is the entire homotopy between them); see Theorem
G.2
.

•

If
\gamma^{(1)}_{j}
is not entirely contained in
\overline{B}^{c}
then there is some component
\mathcal{B}^{(0)}_{j}
of
\partial B\cap W_{j}[t_{0,j}]
that has non-zero mod-2 intersection with
\gamma_{j}^{(0)}
and zero mod-2 intersection with each previous
\gamma_{i}^{(0)}
,
i<j
; this follows from simplicity of the flow near
\partial B
and the fact that mod-2 intersection is preserved under homotopy.

By Lemma
F.6
we have that

\genus(\check{M}_{j}(t_{0,j})\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0}))=0.

This contradicts (
9.6
), completing the proof of (
9.8
) and thus the proposition.
∎

Appendices

Appendix A
Graphs over shrinkers

Recall the conventions for the second fundamental form and mean curvature as described in Section
3.1
. Suppose that
\Sigma^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is a smooth shrinker (the estimates here are local so
\Sigma
could just be a small piece of a shrinker) and consider for
I\subset\mathbb{R}
an interval of time
u:\Sigma\times I\to\mathbb{R}
so that

|u||A|<\eta<1

along
\Sigma\times I
, where
A
is the second fundamental form of
\Sigma
. This allows us to define the graph

\Gamma_{\tau}:=\{\mathbf{x}+u(\mathbf{x},\tau)\nu_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x}):\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma\}.

We compute here various geometric quantities associated to
\Gamma_{\tau}
. Define

(A.1)

v(\mathbf{x},\tau)=(1+|(\Id-uS_{\Sigma})^{-1}(\nabla_{\Sigma}u)|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Lemma A.1
.

The upwards pointing normal along
\Gamma_{\tau}
is

(A.2)

\nu_{\Gamma}=v^{-1}(-(\Id-uS_{\Sigma})^{-1}\nabla_{\Sigma}u+\nu_{\Sigma}).

In particular

(A.3)

v=(\nu_{\Sigma}\cdot\nu_{\Gamma})^{-1}.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbf{Z}\in T_{\mathbf{x}}\Sigma
if and only if

(\Id-uS_{\Sigma})\mathbf{Z}+du(\mathbf{Z})\nu_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x})\in T_{\mathbf{x}+u(\mathbf{x},\tau)\nu_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x})}\Gamma_{\tau}.

Since the shape operator is self-adjoint, we thus find that the expression for
\nu_{\Gamma}
is orthogonal to
\Gamma_{\tau}
. The assertion thus follows from the definition of
v
.
∎

For
\ell\in\{0,1\}
set

(A.4)

\sigma_{\ell}(\mathbf{x},\tau):=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\sum_{k=0}^{4-2j}|\partial_{\tau}^{j}\nabla^{k}u(\mathbf{x},\tau)|.

We now study the form of the error terms in the linearization of the rescaled mean curvature flow equation.

Lemma A.2
.

The mean curvature of
\Gamma_{\tau}
at
\mathbf{x}+u(\mathbf{x},\tau)\nu_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x})
satisfies

(A.5)

v(\mathbf{x},\tau)H_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{x}+u(\mathbf{x},\tau)\nu_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x}),\tau)=H_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x})+(\Delta_{\Sigma}u+|A_{\Sigma}|^{2}u)(\mathbf{x})+E^{H}

where the error
E^{H}
can be decomposed into terms of the form

E^{H}=uE^{H}_{1}+E^{H}_{2}(\nabla_{\Sigma}u,\nabla_{\Sigma}u)

where
E^{H}_{1}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
and
E^{H}_{2}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma;T^{*}\Sigma\otimes T^{*}\Sigma)
satisfy the following estimates:

|\partial_{\tau}E_{1}^{H}(\mathbf{x},\tau)|\leq C_{1}^{H}\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{x},\tau),\qquad\sum_{k=0}^{2}|\nabla^{k}_{\Sigma}E_{1}^{H}(\mathbf{x},\tau)|\leq C_{1}^{H}\sigma_{0}(\mathbf{x},\tau)

and
29
29

29

recall that
E_{2}
is a section of
T^{*}\Sigma\otimes T^{*}\Sigma
so e.g.,
\nabla_{\Sigma}E_{2}
is a section of
T^{*}\Sigma\otimes T^{*}\Sigma\otimes T^{*}\Sigma

|\partial_{\tau}E_{2}^{H}(\mathbf{x},t)|\leq C_{2}^{H}(1+\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{x},\tau)),\qquad\sum_{k=0}^{2}|\nabla^{k}E_{2}^{H}(\mathbf{x},t)|\leq C_{2}^{H}(1+\sigma_{0}(\mathbf{x},\tau))

where
C_{1}^{H},C_{2}^{H}
depend only on
\eta
and an upper bound for
\sum_{k=0}^{3}|\nabla^{k}A|(\mathbf{x})
.

Proof.

The area formula
[
Sim83
, §2.5]
implies that for
\tau
fixed,
\Psi(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{x}+u(\mathbf{x},\tau)\nu_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x})
satisfies

\Psi^{*}d\mu_{\Gamma}=\sqrt{\det\mathcal{G}}\,d\mu_{\Sigma}

where
\mathcal{G}:T_{\mathbf{x}}\Sigma\to T_{\mathbf{x}}\Sigma
is the linear map

\mathcal{G}=(\Id-uS)^{2}+\nabla_{\Sigma}u\otimes du.

It is straightforward to check that
\mathcal{G}
is invertible as long as
|u||A|<1
.

Set
u_{s}=u+s\varphi
for
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Sigma)
arbitrary. Then (writing
\mathcal{G}_{s}
for
\mathcal{G}
with
u
replaced by
u+s\varphi
) the first variation formula yields

\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\int_{\Sigma}\sqrt{\det\mathcal{G}_{s}}\,d\mu_{\Sigma}=-\int_{\Sigma}H_{\Gamma}(\nu_{\Sigma}\cdot\nu_{\Gamma})\varphi\sqrt{\det\mathcal{G}}\,d\mu_{\Sigma}.

On the other hand, we compute

\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\mathcal{G}_{s}=-2(\Id-uS)S\varphi+\nabla\varphi\otimes du+\nabla u\otimes d\varphi,

so

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\sqrt{\det\mathcal{G}_{s}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\tr\left(\mathcal{G}^{-1}\tfrac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\mathcal{G}_{s}\right)\sqrt{\det\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\mathcal{G}^{-1}\left(\nabla u\otimes d\varphi-(S-uS^{2})\varphi\right)\right)\sqrt{\det\mathcal{G}}.

Integrating by parts, we thus find

(A.6)

vH_{\Gamma}=\frac{v^{2}}{\sqrt{\det\mathcal{G}}}\Div(\sqrt{\det\mathcal{G}}\,\mathcal{G}^{-1}\nabla u)+v^{2}(\tr\mathcal{G}^{-1}S-\tr\mathcal{G}^{-1}S^{2}u).

where we emphasize that all quantities (on the right hand side) are taken along
\Sigma
.

We now simplify this expression. For notational simplicity, below we will write
f\approx g
if
f=g+ue_{1}+e_{2}(\nabla u,\nabla u)
for
e_{1},e_{2}
satisfying the estimates for
E_{1}^{H},E_{2}^{H}
in the statement of the lemma (i.e., terms we will absorb in to the error terms). Note that
v\approx 1
and if we abuse the notation slightly to allow for tensor valued expressions,
\mathcal{G}\approx\Id-2uS
so
\mathcal{G}^{-1}\approx\Id+2uS
. We compute

\displaystyle\tr\mathcal{G}^{-1}S-\tr\mathcal{G}^{-1}S^{2}u

\displaystyle=\tr\left((1+2uS)S\right)-\tr\left(S^{2}u\right)

\displaystyle+\tr\left((\mathcal{G}^{-1}-\Id-2uS)S-(\mathcal{G}^{-1}-\Id)S^{2}u\right)

\displaystyle=H+|A|^{2}u

\displaystyle+\tr\left((\mathcal{G}^{-1}-\Id-2uS_{\Sigma})S-(\mathcal{G}^{-1}-\Id)S^{2}u\right),

which implies that

\displaystyle v^{2}\left(\tr\mathcal{G}^{-1}S-\tr\mathcal{G}^{-1}S^{2}u\right)

\displaystyle\approx H+|A|^{2}u.

We now consider the divergence term in (
A.6
). Write

\frac{v^{2}}{\sqrt{\det\mathcal{G}}}\Div(\sqrt{\det\mathcal{G}}\,\mathcal{G}^{-1}\nabla u)\approx\Div(\mathcal{G}^{-1}\nabla u)+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\log\det\mathcal{G}\cdot\nabla u

Since
\nabla u
enters
\mathcal{G}
quadratically, we easily see that
\nabla\log\det\mathcal{G}\cdot\nabla u\approx 0
. Finally, we have

\displaystyle\Div(\mathcal{G}^{-1}\nabla u)

\displaystyle\approx\Div\left((\Id+\nabla u\otimes du)^{-1}\nabla u\right)

\displaystyle=\Div\left(\left((\Id-\frac{\nabla u\otimes du}{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}\right)\nabla u\right)

\displaystyle=\Div\left(\frac{\nabla u}{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}\right)\approx\Delta u.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

The support function along
\Gamma_{\tau}
satisfies

(A.7)

v(\mathbf{x},\tau)\left(\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma}\cdot\nu_{\Gamma}\right)=\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma}+u(\mathbf{x},\tau)-\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\nabla u+uE^{\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu}

at
\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{x}+u(\mathbf{x},\tau)\nu_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x})
, where
E^{\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu}
satisfies

|\partial_{\tau}E^{\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu}(\mathbf{x},\tau)|\leq C\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{x},\tau),\qquad\sum_{k=0}^{2}|\nabla^{k}E^{\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu}(\mathbf{x},\tau)|\leq C\sigma_{0}(\mathbf{x},\tau)

for
C
depending only on
\eta
and an upper bound
30
30

30

in particular
C
does
not
depend on
|\mathbf{x}|
for
\sum_{k=0}^{3}|\nabla^{k}A|(\mathbf{x})
.

Proof.

Differentiating the shrinker equation
H+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma}=0
we find

S(\mathbf{x}^{T})=2\nabla H.

Hence, using (
A.2
) we compute

\displaystyle v(\mathbf{x},\tau)\left(\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma}\cdot\nu_{\Gamma}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma}+u-\mathbf{x}\cdot(\Id-uS)^{-1}\nabla u

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma}+u-\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla u-u\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}u^{k-1}\mathbf{x}\cdot S^{k}(\nabla u)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma}+u-\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla u-u\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}u^{k}S(\mathbf{x}^{T})\cdot S^{k}(\nabla u)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma}+u-\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\nabla u-2u\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}u^{k}\nabla H\cdot S^{k}(\nabla u).

This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary A.4
.

We have

\displaystyle v(\mathbf{x},\tau)(\partial_{\tau}\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma}-\mathbf{H}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma})\cdot\nu_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}u-{\big(\underbrace{\Delta u-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\nabla u+(|A|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2})u}_{=Lu}\big)}+E

at
\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{x}+u(\mathbf{x},\tau)\nu_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{x})
for
E=uE_{1}+E_{2}(\nabla u,\nabla u)
for
E_{1},E_{2}
satisfying

|\partial_{\tau}E_{1}(\mathbf{x},\tau)|\leq C_{1}\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{x},\tau),\qquad\sum_{k=0}^{2}|\nabla^{k}E_{1}(\mathbf{x},\tau)|\leq C_{1}\sigma_{0}(\mathbf{x},\tau)

and

|\partial_{\tau}E_{2}(\mathbf{x},\tau)|\leq C_{2}(1+\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{x},\tau)),\qquad\sum_{k=0}^{2}|\nabla^{k}E_{2}(\mathbf{x},\tau)|\leq C_{2}(1+\sigma_{0}(\mathbf{x},\tau))

where
C_{1},C_{2}
depend only on
\eta
and an upper bound for
\sum_{k=0}^{3}|\nabla^{k}_{\Sigma}A|(\mathbf{x})
.

In particular, if
\Gamma_{\tau}
is a solution to rescaled mean curvature flow, i.e.,

(\partial_{\tau}\mathbf{x})^{\perp}=\mathbf{H}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{\perp}

then

(A.8)

\partial_{\tau}u=Lu+E

for
E
as above.

Proof.

Recall that our convention is that
\mathbf{H}=H\nu_{\Gamma}
. Thus, when combined with Lemmas
A.2
and
A.3
, we compute

\displaystyle(\partial_{\tau}\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma}-\mathbf{H}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma})\cdot\nu_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=(\nu_{\Sigma}\cdot\nu_{\Gamma})\partial_{\tau}u-H-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}_{\Gamma}\cdot\nu_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=-\underbrace{(H_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x})+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma})}_{=0}+\partial_{\tau}u-(\Delta_{\Sigma}u+|A_{\Sigma}|^{2}u-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\nabla u+\tfrac{1}{2}u)+E^{\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu}-E^{H}.

Combined with the estimates from Lemmas
A.2
and
A.3
, this completes the proof.
∎

Finally, we recall the improved estimates that hold along an asymptotically conical end
\Sigma_{\textrm{con}}
, proven in
[
CCMS20
, Lemma 3.6]
.

Lemma A.5
.

Along an asymptotically conical end
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}
there is
\eta,C
depending on
\Sigma
so that if

|\mathbf{x}|^{-1}|u(\mathbf{x})|+|\nabla u(\mathbf{x})|+|\mathbf{x}||\nabla^{2}u|\leq\eta

then the error term
E
in (
A.8
) decomposes as

(A.9)

E(u)(\mathbf{x})=u(\mathbf{x})E_{1}(\mathbf{x},u(\mathbf{x}),\nabla u(\mathbf{x}),\nabla^{2}u(\mathbf{x}))\\
+\nabla u(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{E}_{2}(\mathbf{x},u(\mathbf{x}),\nabla u(\mathbf{x}),\nabla^{2}u(\mathbf{x})),

where
E_{1}
,
\mathbf{E}_{2}
are smooth functions on the following domains

\displaystyle E_{1}(\mathbf{x},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{R}\times T_{\mathbf{x}}\Sigma\times\operatorname{Sym}(T_{\mathbf{x}}\Sigma\otimes T_{\mathbf{x}}\Sigma)\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{2}(\mathbf{x},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{R}\times T_{\mathbf{x}}\Sigma\times\operatorname{Sym}(T_{\mathbf{x}}\Sigma\otimes T_{\mathbf{x}}\Sigma)\to T_{\mathbf{x}}\Sigma.

Moreover, we can estimate:

\displaystyle r(\mathbf{x})^{2+j-\ell}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}^{i}\nabla_{z}^{j}\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}^{k}\nabla_{\mathbf{A}}^{\ell}E_{1}(\mathbf{x},z,\mathbf{q},\mathbf{A})|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq C(r(\mathbf{x})^{-1}|z|+|\mathbf{q}|+r(\mathbf{x})|\mathbf{A}|)^{\max\{0,1-j-k-\ell\}},

\displaystyle r(\mathbf{x})^{1+j-\ell}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}^{i}\nabla_{z}^{j}\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}^{k}\nabla_{\mathbf{A}}^{\ell}\mathbf{E}_{2}(\mathbf{x},z,\mathbf{q},\mathbf{A})|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq C(r(\mathbf{x})^{-1}|z|+|\mathbf{q}|+r(\mathbf{x})|\mathbf{A}|)^{\max\{0,1-j-k-\ell\}}.

In the above,
C=C(\Sigma,i,j,k,\ell)
, and
i,j,k,\ell\geq 0
.

Along an asymptotically cylindrical end the estimates can be extended as follows.

Lemma A.6
.

Along the core
\Sigma\cap B_{R}(\mathbf{0})
and every asymptotically cylindrical end
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\textnormal{cyl}}
there is
\eta,C
depending on
\Sigma
so that if
\|u\|_{C^{2}}\leq\eta
as well as
|u||A|\leq\eta
then the error term
E
in (
A.8
) decomposes as

(A.10)

E(u)(\mathbf{x})=u(\mathbf{x})E_{1}(\mathbf{x},u(\mathbf{x}),\nabla u(\mathbf{x}),\nabla^{2}u(\mathbf{x}))\\
+\nabla u(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{E}_{2}(\mathbf{x},u(\mathbf{x}),\nabla u(\mathbf{x}),\nabla^{2}u(\mathbf{x})),

where
E_{1}
,
\mathbf{E}_{2}
are smooth functions as in Lemma
A.5
, where we can estimate:

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}^{i}\nabla_{z}^{j}\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}^{k}\nabla_{\mathbf{A}}^{\ell}E_{1}(\mathbf{x},z,\mathbf{q},\mathbf{A})|\leq C(|z|+|\mathbf{q}|+|\mathbf{A}|)^{\max\{0,1-j-k-\ell\}},

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}^{i}\nabla_{z}^{j}\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}^{k}\nabla_{\mathbf{A}}^{\ell}\mathbf{E}_{2}(\mathbf{x},z,\mathbf{q},\mathbf{A})|\leq C(|z|+|\mathbf{q}|+|\mathbf{A}|)^{\max\{0,1-j-k-\ell\}}.

In the above,
C=C(\Sigma,i,j,k,\ell)
, and
i,j,k,\ell\geq 0
.

Proof.

As in the proof of
[
CCMS20
, Lemma 3.6]
one can split the error into
E^{H}(u)
and
E^{\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu}(u)
. The estimate for the
E^{H}(u)
term is as in
[
CCMS20
, Lemma 3.6]
, whereas for the
E^{\mathbf{x}\cdot\nu}(u)
term we can argue as in the proof of Lemma
A.3
.
∎

We also need to understand the linearization of the shrinker mean curvature of two graphs over
\Sigma
, relative to each other and relative to the base
\Sigma
.

Lemma A.7
.

For
\delta<\tfrac{1}{2}(\sup_{\Omega}|A_{\Sigma}|)^{-1}
and
u_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma),i=0,1
with

(A.11)

|u_{i}(\mathbf{x})|+|\nabla_{\Sigma}u_{i}(\mathbf{x})|+|\nabla^{2}_{\Sigma}u_{i}(\mathbf{x})|+|\nabla^{3}_{\Sigma}u_{i}(\mathbf{x})|\leq\delta

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma
. Letting

\Gamma_{i}:=\{\mathbf{x}+u_{i}(\mathbf{x})\nu_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x}):\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma\}

then denoting
w=u_{1}-u_{0}
and
v_{i}:=(\nu_{\Gamma_{i}}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma})^{-1}
there exists
C=C(\sup_{\Sigma}|A_{\Sigma}|+|\nabla A_{\Sigma}|+|\nabla^{2}A_{\Sigma}|)
such that

v_{1}\Big(H_{\Sigma_{1}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}_{\Sigma_{1}}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma_{1}}\Big)-v_{0}\Big(H_{\Sigma_{0}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}_{\Sigma_{0}}\cdot\nu_{\Sigma_{0}}\Big)=L_{\Sigma}w+E^{w}

where
\mathbf{x}_{\Sigma_{i}}=\mathbf{x}+u_{i}(\mathbf{x})\nu_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{x})
and the error term
E
satisfies

E^{w}(\mathbf{x})=w(\mathbf{x})F(\mathbf{x})+\nabla w(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{x})+\nabla^{2}w(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x})\,,

with the estimate

|F|+|\mathbf{F}|+|\mathcal{F}|+|\nabla\mathcal{F}|\leq C\delta

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\Sigma
. Furthermore, under the assumption that
\Sigma
is a properly embedded shrinker, it holds

(A.12)

\left|\int_{\Sigma}w(\mathbf{x}^{T}\otimes\nabla_{\Sigma}w)\cdot\mathcal{F}\,\rho\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}\right|\leq C\delta\|w\|^{2}_{W,1}\,.

In the case
\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}\subset\Sigma
, all the above statements remain the same if along the conical ends
\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}
one replaces (
A.11
) with the weaker assumption

\|u_{i}\|_{3,\alpha;\Sigma_{\textnormal{con}}}^{(1)}\leq\delta

for
i=1,2
.

Proof.

The proof follows similarly as the proof of
[
CCMS20
, Corollary 5.2]
. Denoting

w:=u_{1}-u_{2},\ E^{w}:=E(u_{1})-E(u_{2})

we have

\big(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\tau}-L\big)w=E^{w}\,.

Using Lemma
A.6
and the fundamental theorem of calculus, we obtain as in
[
CCMS20
, (5.11)]

(A.13)

\begin{split}E^{w}&=wE_{1}(\cdot,u_{2},\nabla_{\Sigma}u_{2},\nabla_{\Sigma}^{2}u_{2})+\nabla_{\Sigma}w\cdot\mathbf{E}_{2}(\cdot,u_{2},\nabla_{\Sigma}u_{2},\nabla_{\Sigma}^{2}u_{2})\\
&\quad+\Big[u_{1}\int_{0}^{1}D_{z}E_{1}(\cdots)\,dt\Big]w+\Big[u_{1}\int_{0}^{1}D_{\mathbf{q}}E_{1}(\cdots)\,dt\Big]\cdot\nabla_{\Sigma}w\\
&\quad+\Big[u_{1}\int_{0}^{1}D_{\mathbf{A}}E_{1}(\cdots)\,dt\Big]\cdot\nabla^{2}_{\Sigma}w+\Big[\nabla_{\Sigma}u_{1}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}D_{z}\mathbf{E}_{2}(\cdots)\,dt\Big]w\\
&\quad+\Big[\nabla_{\Sigma}u_{1}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}D_{\mathbf{q}}\mathbf{E}_{2}(\cdots)\,dt\Big]\cdot\nabla_{\Sigma}w\\
&\quad+\Big[\nabla_{\Sigma}u_{1}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}D_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{E}_{2}(\cdots)\,dt\Big]\cdot\nabla^{2}_{\Sigma}w,\end{split}

where, in all six instances,
\cdots
stands for
(\cdot,u_{2}+tw,\nabla_{\Sigma}u_{2}+t\nabla_{\Sigma}w,\nabla_{\Sigma}^{2}u_{2}+t\nabla^{2}_{\Sigma}w)
. We note that we can thus write

E^{w}=wF+\nabla_{\Sigma}w\cdot\mathbf{F}+\nabla^{2}_{\Sigma}w\cdot\mathcal{F}\,.

The estimates for
F,\mathbf{F},\mathcal{F},\nabla\mathcal{F}
then follow from Lemma
A.6
.

In case
\Sigma
is a properly embedded shrinker, recall that Ecker’s Sobolev inequality
[
Eck00
]
(cf.
[
CS21
, Proposition 3.9]
) implies that for
f\in H^{1}_{W}(\Sigma)

\||\mathbf{x}|f\|^{2}_{W}\leq 4n\|f\|_{W,1}^{2}\,,

and we can thus estimate

\left|\int_{\Sigma}w\,(\mathbf{x}^{T}\otimes\nabla_{\Sigma}w)\cdot\mathcal{F}\,e^{-\tfrac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}d\mathcal{H}^{n}\right|\leq C\delta(\||\mathbf{x}|w\|_{W}\|\nabla_{\Sigma}w\|_{W})\leq C\delta\|w\|_{W,1}^{2}\,.

The claim for the conical ends
\|u_{i}\|_{2,\alpha;\Sigma_{\text{con}}}^{(1)}\leq\delta
for
i=1,2
, follow similarly, (compare

[
CCMS20
, (5.11)]
), using Lemma
A.5
instead of Lemma
A.6
.
∎

Appendix B
Positive graphs over shrinkers

We continue the analysis in Appendix
A
and consider
u:\Sigma\times I\to\mathbb{R}
(where we continue to allow for
\Sigma
to be possibly incomplete) so that the graph
\Gamma_{\tau}
is flowing by rescaled mean curvature flow. We continue to require that

|u||A|\leq\eta<1

on
\Sigma\times I
. In this appendix, we additionally require that the function
u
is positive for all
(\mathbf{x},t)\in\Sigma\times I
. We set
v=\log u
. Note that

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}v

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{\tau}u}{u}

\displaystyle\nabla v

\displaystyle=\frac{\nabla u}{u}

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}v

\displaystyle=\frac{\nabla^{2}u}{u}-dv\otimes dv.

Recall the quantities
\sigma_{0}
,
\sigma_{1}
defined in (
A.4
) and that Corollary
A.4
implies that

(B.1)

\partial_{\tau}u=Lu+E

where
E=uE_{1}+E_{2}(\nabla u,\nabla u)
satisfy the estimates stated above. Below we show that these estimates yield estimates for
u^{-1}E
.

Proposition B.1
.

For
(\mathbf{x},\tau)\in\Sigma\times I
, assume that
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{x},\tau)\leq 1
. There is
C
depending only on
\eta
and an upper bound for
\sum_{k=0}^{3}|\nabla^{k}A|(\mathbf{x})
so that

\displaystyle|(u^{-1}E)|

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma_{0}(1+|\nabla v|)

\displaystyle|\nabla v||\nabla(u^{-1}E)|

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma_{0}(1+|\nabla v|^{3})

\displaystyle|\partial_{\tau}(u^{-1}E)|

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma_{1}(1+|\nabla v|^{3})

at
(\mathbf{x},\tau)
.

Proof.

It suffices to only consider
E_{2}
. Writing
C
for a constant that will change from line to line but only depends on
\eta
and an upper bound for
\sum_{k=0}^{3}|\nabla^{k}A|(\mathbf{x})
, we have:

\displaystyle|u^{-1}E_{2}(\nabla u,\nabla u)|

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\sigma_{0})|\nabla v||\nabla u|\leq C\sigma_{0}|\nabla v|.

For the second estimate, we have

\displaystyle|\nabla v||\nabla(u^{-1}E_{2}(\nabla u,\nabla u))|

\displaystyle\leq C|\nabla v|(u^{-2}|\nabla u|^{3}+u^{-1}|\nabla u|^{2}+u^{-1}|\nabla^{2}u||\nabla u|)

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma_{0}(|\nabla v|^{3}+|\nabla v|^{2}).

Similarly,

\displaystyle|\partial_{\tau}(u^{-1}E_{2}(\nabla u,\nabla u))|

\displaystyle\leq C(u^{-2}|\partial_{\tau}u||\nabla u|^{2}+u^{-1}|\nabla u|^{2}+u^{-1}|\nabla u||\partial_{\tau}\nabla u|)

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma_{1}(|\nabla v|^{2}+|\nabla v|).

Clearly both of these estimates imply the asserted bound.
∎

From (
B.1
) we see that
v
satisfies

(B.2)

\partial_{\tau}v=\Delta v+|\nabla v|^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\nabla v+\tfrac{1}{2}+|A|^{2}+u^{-1}E\,.

We denote the corresponding linearized operator by
\mathcal{N}
, defined via

\mathcal{N}w:=\Delta w+2\nabla v\cdot\nabla w-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\nabla w\,.

We extend
\mathcal{N}
to act on tensors in the usual way, i.e.,

\mathcal{N}T:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{E}_{i},\mathbf{E}_{i}}T+2\nabla_{\nabla v}T-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}^{T}}T.

for
\mathbf{E}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{E}_{n}
an orthonormal basis of
T_{p}M
.

Proposition B.2
.

Denoting
v_{\tau}
by the
\tau
-derivative of
v
, we have

(B.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}v_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}v_{\tau}+\partial_{\tau}(u^{-1}E)\,,

(B.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\nabla_{\Sigma}v

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}\nabla v-\tfrac{1}{2}\nabla v+S^{2}(\nabla v)+\nabla|A|^{2}+\nabla(u^{-1}E)\,,

(B.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}|\nabla v|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}|\nabla v|^{2}-2|\nabla^{2}v|^{2}-|\nabla v|^{2}+2\nabla v\cdot S^{2}(\nabla v)+2\nabla v\cdot\nabla|A|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+2\nabla v\cdot\nabla(u^{-1}E)

Proof.

Equation (
B.3
) follows directly from differentiating (
B.2
) with respect to
\tau
. To prove (
B.4
), we consider the gradient of (
B.2
). We first note that (recalling the conventions (
3.2
) and (
3.3
))

\nabla(\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\nabla v)=\nabla v+\nabla_{\mathbf{x}^{T}}\nabla v-2HS(\nabla v),

so

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\nabla v=\nabla\Delta v+2\nabla_{\nabla v}\nabla v-\tfrac{1}{2}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}^{T}}\nabla v-\tfrac{1}{2}\nabla v+HS(\nabla v)+\nabla|A|^{2}+\nabla(u^{-1}E).

Using the commutator identities (
B.6
) and (
B.7
) proven below, we find

\nabla\Delta v=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{E}_{i},\mathbf{E}_{i}}\nabla v-\Ric(\nabla v,\cdot)^{\sharp}.

On the other hand, the (traced) Gauss equations (
3.7
) yield

\Ric(\nabla v,\cdot)^{\sharp}=HS(\nabla v)-S^{2}(\nabla v),

so putting these expressions together, (
B.4
) follows. Combining (
B.4
) with

\mathcal{N}(\nabla v\otimes\nabla v)=(\mathcal{N}\nabla v)\otimes\nabla v+\nabla v\otimes\mathcal{N}\nabla v+2\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\nabla_{\mathbf{E}_{i}}\nabla v)\otimes(\nabla_{\mathbf{E}_{i}}\nabla v)

and taking the trace yields (
B.5
). ∎

Lemma B.3
.

For a function
f
on a Riemannian manifold
(M^{n},g)

(B.6)

\displaystyle\nabla^{3}_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}f

\displaystyle=\nabla^{3}_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Z}}f+R(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\nabla f)

(B.7)

\displaystyle\nabla^{3}_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}f

\displaystyle=\nabla^{3}_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Y}}f

Proof.

We can assume that
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
are parallel at the point under consideration and compute

\displaystyle\nabla^{3}_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}f

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{X}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}f

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{X}}\nabla_{\mathbf{Y}}\nabla_{\mathbf{Z}}f-\nabla_{\mathbf{X}}\nabla_{\nabla_{\mathbf{Y}}\mathbf{Z}}f

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{Y}}\nabla_{\mathbf{X}}\nabla_{\mathbf{Z}}f-\nabla_{\nabla_{\mathbf{X}}\nabla_{\mathbf{Y}}\mathbf{Z}}f

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{Y}}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Z}}f+\nabla_{\mathbf{Y}}\nabla_{\nabla_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{Z}}f-\nabla_{\nabla_{\mathbf{X}}\nabla_{\mathbf{Y}}\mathbf{Z}}f

\displaystyle=\nabla^{3}_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Z}}f-\nabla_{\nabla_{\mathbf{X}}\nabla_{\mathbf{Y}}\mathbf{Z}-\nabla_{\mathbf{Y}}\nabla_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{Z}}f

\displaystyle=\nabla^{3}_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Z}}f+R(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\nabla f).

This proves (
B.6
). The first line of the calculation also clearly implies (
B.7
).
∎

Combining Proposition
B.1
with Proposition
B.2
yields

Proposition B.4
.

For
(\mathbf{x},\tau)\in\Sigma\times I
, assume that
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{x},\tau)\leq 1
. There is
C
depending only on
\eta
and an upper bound for
\sum_{k=0}^{3}|\nabla^{k}A|(\mathbf{x})
so that
w:=v_{\tau}-\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla v|^{2}
satisfies

w_{\tau}\geq\mathcal{N}w+|\nabla^{2}v|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla v|^{2}-|\nabla v|^{2}|A|^{2}-|\nabla v||\nabla|A|^{2}|-C\sigma_{1}(1+|\nabla v|^{3})\,.

B.1.
Li–Yau estimate

In the following we assume that
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}
(i.e.,
\Sigma
has nice ends). We define

(B.8)

\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma\cap B_{2R}(\mathbf{0})

where
R
is assumed to satisfy the following three conditions

(B.9)

R\geq\max\{10^{10}n,\sup_{\Sigma}|A|,\sup_{\Sigma}|\nabla|A|^{2}|\},

as well as

(B.10)

0\leq\inf_{r\in[9nR^{2},\infty)}(\tfrac{5}{24n}r^{2}-10^{-8}n^{-1}r^{\frac{3}{2}}-2),

and (
B.11
) below.

We introduce a cut-off function
\gamma
such that
\gamma(r)=0
for
r\geq 4
,
\gamma(r)=1
for
r\leq 1,-2\leq\gamma^{\prime}(r)\leq 0
and
|\gamma^{\prime\prime}|\leq 2
for all
r
. We let
\eta(\mathbf{x}):=\gamma(|\mathbf{x}|^{2}R^{-2})
. Since
\Sigma
has nice ends we can assume that
R
is sufficiently large such that

(B.11)

|\Delta_{\Sigma}\eta|\leq R^{-1}\,,

where
C=C(\sup_{\Sigma}|A|^{2})
.

Proposition B.5

(Li–Yau type estimate)
.

Assume
u:\Sigma^{\prime}\times(-\infty,0)\to(0,\infty)
is such that the graph
\Gamma_{\tau}
is flowing by rescaled mean curvature flow and that
\sup_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\sigma_{1}(\cdot,\tau)\to 0
as
\tau\to-\infty
.
Then there is
\tau_{0}\ll-1
such that

|\nabla\log u|^{2}\leq 20nR^{2}+2\partial_{\tau}\log u

holds for
(\mathbf{x},\tau)\in B_{R}(0)\cap\Sigma\times(-\infty,\tau_{0}]
.

Proof.

We fix
\tau_{0}
sufficiently negative below. Given
T\leq\tau_{0}
, we define

W(\mathbf{x},\tau)=w(\mathbf{x},\tau)\eta^{2}(\mathbf{x})+h(\tau)\,,

where
w=v_{\tau}-\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla v|^{2}
and

h(\tau)=10n(\tau-T+R^{-2})^{-1}\,.

From Proposition
B.4
we see that

\begin{split}\partial_{\tau}W&\geq\mathcal{N}W-\tfrac{1}{10n}h^{2}+\eta^{2}|\nabla^{2}v|^{2}-\eta^{2}|\nabla v|^{2}|A|^{2}-\eta^{2}|\nabla v||\nabla|A|^{2}|\\
&\quad-C\sigma_{1}\eta^{2}(1+|\nabla v|^{3})-w\Delta\eta^{2}-4\eta\nabla w\cdot\nabla\eta-4\eta w\nabla v\cdot\nabla\eta\\
&\quad+w\eta\,\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\nabla\eta\,.\end{split}

Towards a contradiction, we assume that there exists a point
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\tau_{1})
such that
\tau_{1}\leq T
,
W(\mathbf{p}_{1},\tau_{1})=0
, and
W(\cdot,\tau)>0
holds for
T-R^{2}<\tau<\tau_{1}

. Since
\nabla W(\mathbf{p}_{1},\tau_{1})=0
, we have
\eta\nabla w=-2w\nabla\eta
at
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\tau_{1})
. In addition, we have
W_{\tau}-\mathcal{N}W\leq 0
at
(p_{1},\tau_{1})
. Moreover,
W=0
yields
w\leq 0
at
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\tau_{1})
and we have
\mathbf{x}^{T}\cdot\nabla\eta\leq 0
at
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\tau_{1})
by the definition of
\eta
. Hence at
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\tau_{1})
,

\begin{split}0&\geq-\tfrac{1}{10n}h^{2}+\eta^{2}|\nabla^{2}v|^{2}-\eta^{2}|A|^{2}|\nabla v|^{2}-\eta^{2}|\nabla v||\nabla|A|^{2}|\\
&\quad-C\sigma_{1}\eta^{2}(1+|\nabla v|^{3})-2w\eta\Delta\eta+6w|\nabla\eta|^{2}-4\eta w\nabla v\cdot\nabla\eta.\end{split}

By using the definition of
R,\eta
and using
|\nabla^{2}v|^{2}\geq n^{-1}|\Delta v|^{2}
this implies

(B.12)

\begin{split}0&\geq-\tfrac{1}{10n}h^{2}+\tfrac{1}{n}\eta^{2}|\Delta v|^{2}-\eta^{2}R^{2}|\nabla v|^{2}-\eta^{2}R|\nabla v|-C\sigma_{1}\eta^{2}(1+|\nabla v|^{3})\\
&\quad-20R^{-1}|w|(1+\eta|\nabla v|)\end{split}

at
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\tau_{1})
.

On the other hand, by using (
B.2
) and
|\mathbf{x}|\leq 2R
on
B_{2R}(\mathbf{0})
together with Proposition
B.1
, we have

w\geq\Delta v+\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla v|^{2}-(R+C\sigma_{0})|\nabla v|+\tfrac{1}{2}+|A|^{2}-C\sigma_{0},

for some
C
depending on
\sup_{B_{2R}(\mathbf{0})}\sum_{k=0}^{3}|\nabla^{k}A|
. By choosing
\tau_{0}
sufficiently negative, we can arrange that
C\sigma_{0}\leq\min\{\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{3}R\}
(note that the constant
C
here depends only on
\Sigma
, since it comes in through the applications of Propositions
B.1
and Proposition
B.4
), we find that

w\geq\Delta v+\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla v|^{2}-\tfrac{4}{3}R|\nabla v|\geq\Delta v+\tfrac{1}{4}|\nabla v|^{2}-2R^{2},

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Since
w\leq 0
at
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\tau_{1})
, we see that at this point

-w\geq-w\eta^{2}=h\geq 10nR^{2}

and thus (note that
n\geq 2
)

(B.13)

\tfrac{1}{4}|\nabla v|^{2}+\tfrac{9}{10}|w|\leq-\Delta v,

at
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\tau_{1})
. This yields

\tfrac{1}{8}\eta^{2}|\Delta v|^{2}\geq\tfrac{1}{8}\left(\tfrac{9}{10}\right)^{2}w^{2}\eta^{2}\geq\tfrac{1}{10}w^{2}\eta^{2}\geq\tfrac{1}{10}h^{2}.

Combining with (
B.12
) implies

\begin{split}0&\geq\tfrac{7}{8n}\eta^{2}|\Delta v|^{2}-4R^{2}\eta^{2}|\Delta v|-2R\eta^{2}|\Delta v|^{\tfrac{1}{2}}\\
&\quad-C\sigma_{0}\eta^{2}(|\Delta v|^{\frac{3}{2}}+1)-30R^{-1}|\Delta v|(1+2\eta|\Delta v|^{\frac{1}{2}}).\end{split}

We can choose
\tau_{0}
sufficiently negative so that we have

\displaystyle 0\geq\tfrac{7}{8n}\eta^{2}|\Delta v|^{2}-10^{-8}n^{-1}\eta|\Delta v|^{\frac{3}{2}}-6R^{2}|\Delta v|-2.

On the other hand, by using (
B.13
) we derive

\eta^{2}|\Delta v|\geq\tfrac{9}{10}|w|\eta^{2}=\tfrac{9}{10}h\geq 9nR^{2}.

Combining these expressions we derive a contradiction to (
B.10
). In conclusion, we have
W\geq 0
for
\tau\leq T
. Therefore, given
T\leq\tau_{0}

0\leq W(x,T)=w(x,T)+h(T)=w(x,T)+10nR^{2},

holds in
B_{R}(0)\cap\Sigma
.
∎

Appendix C
Integral Brakke
\mathbf{X}
-flows

We record here the notion of Brakke flows with a transport term as introduced by Hershkovits–White in
[
HW18
]
. An (
n
-dimensional)
integral Brakke
\mathbf{X}
-flow
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
(or more generally a Riemannian manifold) is a
1
-parameter family of Radon measures
(\mu(t))_{t\in I}
over an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
so that:

(1)

For almost every
t\in I
there is an integral
n
-dimensional integral varifold
V(t)
with
\mu(t)=\mu_{V(t)}
so that
V(t)
has locally bounded first variation and has mean curvature
\mathbf{H}
orthogonal to
\textrm{Tan}(V(t),\cdot)
almost everywhere.

(2)

For a bounded interval
[t_{1},t_{2}]\subset I
and compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, it holds

\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{K}(1+|\mathbf{H}|^{2})d\mu(t)dt<\infty.

(3)

For
[t_{1},t_{2}]\subset I
and
f\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times[t_{1},t_{2}];[0,\infty))
then

\displaystyle\int f(\cdot,t_{2})d\mu(t_{2})-\int f(\cdot,t_{1})d\mu(t_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int\left(\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial t}+\nabla^{\perp}f\cdot(\mathbf{H}+\mathbf{X})-f\mathbf{H}\cdot(\mathbf{H}+\mathbf{X}^{\perp})\right)d\mu(t)dt.

Convergence/compactness of integral Brakke
\mathbf{X}
-flows is essentially the same as in the standard
\mathbf{X}=0
case. See
[
HW18
]
for further discussion.

Appendix D
Ilmanen’s avoidance principle

For
R>0
and
(\mathbf{x}_{0},t_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
, we define

u(\mathbf{x},t):=(R^{2}-|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}_{0}|^{2}-2n(t-t_{0}))_{+}.

Let
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
be open and assume that
u(\cdot,t)
vanishes on
\partial W(t)
for all
t\in\mathfrak{t}(W)
. For
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in W(t)
, define the distance

(D.1)

d_{t}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}):=\inf\Big\{\int_{\gamma}u(\gamma(s),t)^{-1}\,ds:\gamma\text{ is a curve joining }\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\text{ in }W(t)\Big\}.

We use the standard convention that
\inf\emptyset=\infty
. Note that
d_{t}
is just the distance in the (complete) conformally Euclidean metric
g_{t}:=u(\cdot,t)^{-2}g_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}
. More generally, we can consider the distance between two closed sets in
W_{t}
defined in the usual way.

Theorem D.1

(Ilmanen)
.

Consider two closed weak set flows
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and constants satisfying
R>0,\gamma>0
,
a<b<a+\frac{R^{2}-\gamma}{2n}
. Assume that

\mathcal{M}(t)\cap B_{\sqrt{\gamma+R^{2}-2n(t-a)}}(\mathbf{x}_{0})\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(t)\cap B_{\sqrt{\gamma+R^{2}-2n(t-a)}}(\mathbf{x}_{0})

are disjoint for
t\in[a,b)
. Then, using this choice of
R
and
\mathbf{x}_{0}
along with
t_{0}=a
in the definition of
u
and
d_{t}
above, we have that
t\mapsto d_{t}(\mathcal{M}(t),\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(t))
is non-decreasing for
t\in[a,b)
and

\mathcal{M}(b)\cap\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(b)\cap B_{\sqrt{R^{2}-\gamma-2n(b-a)}}(\mathbf{x}_{0})=\emptyset.

See
[
CCMS20
, Appendix C]
for a proof.

In particular we recall that a
F
-stationary varifold is the varifold version of self-shrinker. The avoidance principle immediately implies (cf.
[
CCMS20
, Corollary C.4]
)

Corollary D.2

(Frankel property for self-shrinkers)
.

If
V,V^{\prime}
are
F
-stationary varifolds then
\supp V\cap\supp V^{\prime}\not=\emptyset
.

Appendix E
Brakke flows avoid smooth flows with bounded curvature

Recall that (in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
) a Brakke flow avoids another Brakke flow as long as one of them has compact initial condition (and they are both initially disjoint). See
[
Ilm94
, 10.6]
.

When both flows have non-compact initial data, it is less clear to what extent this holds. We note that the Ecker–Huisken maximum principle can be used when the flows are both smooth and one is graphical over the other one (and in
[
CCMS20
]
we observed that one can weaken the smoothness assumption in a compact set by using Ilmanen’s avoidance principle). In this appendix we record the following observation of a related but slightly different nature.

Proposition E.1
.

Suppose that
\Omega_{t}
is a family of open sets with the property that
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\partial\Omega_{t}:=M_{t}
is a smooth mean curvature flow with uniformly bounded curvature

(E.1)

|A_{M_{t}}|\leq C

and uniformly controlled area ratios on small scales, i.e.,

(E.2)

\limsup_{r\to 0}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1},t\in[0,T]}\frac{|M_{t}\cap B_{r}(\mathbf{x})|}{\omega_{n}r^{n}}=1.

If
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\tilde{\mu}_{t}
is a Brakke flow with
\supp\tilde{\mu}_{0}\cap\overline{\Omega}_{0}=\emptyset
, then it holds that
\supp\tilde{\mu}_{t}\cap\overline{\Omega}_{t}=\emptyset
for all
t\in(0,T]
.

The point is that neither of the flows are assumed to have compact support.

Proof.

For
R>0
(large) fix a compact set
K_{0}^{R}
as follows. Choose
|\delta_{R}|<1/R
so that
\partial\Omega_{0}
is tranverse to
\partial B_{R+\delta_{R}}(\mathbf{0})
. Then, we can fix
K_{0}^{R}\subset\overline{\Omega_{0}\cap B_{R+\delta_{R}}(\mathbf{0})}
so that (i)
K_{0}^{R}
does not fatten under the level-set flow and (ii)
\partial K_{0}^{R}
is smooth (iii)
\partial K_{0}^{R}\cap B_{R/2}(\mathbf{0})
is a graph over a portion of
M_{0}
with
C^{\lfloor R\rfloor}
-norm
\leq 1/R
and (iv) the condition (
E.2
) holds for
\partial K_{0}^{R}
in place of
M_{t}
, uniformly as
R\to\infty
.

Let
K^{R}_{t}
denote the level set flow of
K_{0}^{R}
. We note that
K_{t}^{R}
is a set-theoretic sub-solution (cf.
[
Ilm94
, 10.1]
) with compact initial condition and moreover because
\tilde{\mu}_{t}
is a Brakke flow,
\supp\tilde{\mu}_{t}
is a set-theoretic subsolution by
[
Ilm94
, 10.5]
. Using the fact that two set-theoretic solutions avoid each-other when one has compact initial conditions by
[
Ilm94
, 10.2]
, we find that
\supp\tilde{\mu}_{t}\cap K_{0}^{R}=\emptyset
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Let
R\to\infty
. We claim that
K^{R}_{t}
converges to
K_{t}
for
t\in[0,T]
in the local Hausdorff sense. In fact,
\partial K_{t}^{R}
converges in the locally smooth graphical sense to
M_{t}
. To see this, first note that
t\mapsto\mu_{t}^{R}:=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor\partial^{*}K_{t}^{R}
is an integral unit-regular Brakke flow by
[
Ilm94
, 11.3]
(since
K_{0}^{R}
is non-fattening under the level-set flow by (i) above). Using (iv) and a covering argument we can find a subsequence
R_{i}\to\infty
and pass
\mu_{t}^{R_{i}}
to the limit as a Brakke flow
\mu_{t}
. Note that
\mu_{0}=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor M_{0}
. By Lemma
E.2
below, we find that
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor M_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
. (Thus, we did not need to take a subsequence.) Unit regularity of
\mu_{t}^{R}
then shows that
\mu_{t}^{R}
is actually smooth on compact sets for
R
large and converges locally smoothly. This yields the asserted convergence of
K_{t}^{R}
to
K_{t}
.

Suppose that
\tilde{\mu}_{t}
is as in the statement of the proposition but there is
t\in(0,T]
and
\mathbf{x}\in K_{t}\cap\supp\tilde{\mu}_{t}
. Choose
\gamma,R_{0}>0
so that
R_{0}^{2}-\gamma>2nT
and
\mathbf{x}\in B_{\sqrt{R_{0}^{2}-2nT-\gamma}}(\mathbf{0})
. By Ilmanen’s avoidance principle, Theorem
D.1
, applied to
K_{t}^{R}
and
\supp\tilde{\mu}_{t}
, (with constants
a=0,b=T,R_{0},\gamma
) we have that

d_{t}(K_{t}^{R},\supp\tilde{\mu}_{t})\geq d_{0}(K_{t}^{R},\supp\tilde{\mu}_{0}),

where
d_{t}
is as in (
D.1
).
Note that
d_{0}(K_{t}^{R},\supp\tilde{\mu}_{0})\geq c>0
as
R\to\infty
by (iii) above (and disjointness of
K_{0}
and
\supp\tilde{\mu}_{0}
). On the other hand, we can take
l_{R}
to be a line segment connecting
\mathbf{x}
to
K_{t}^{R}
. Because
K_{t}^{R}
is converging to
K_{t}
, we find that

d_{t}(K_{t}^{R},\supp\tilde{\mu}_{t})\leq\int_{l_{R}}u(l_{R}(s),t)^{-1}ds=o(1)

as
R\to\infty
(with the notation as in Appendix
D
above). This is a contradiction, completing the proof.
∎

Lemma E.2
.

Suppose that
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto M_{t}
is a smooth mean curvature flow with the property that
M_{t}
satisfies (
E.1
) and (
E.2
). Then for
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\mu_{t}
an integral unit-regular Brakke flow with
\mu_{0}=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor M_{0}
, then it holds that
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor M_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

By unit-regularity and (
E.1
), (
E.2
), the set of times
t
with
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor M_{t}
is closed. To prove openness, we can note that again by unit-regularity and (
E.1
), (
E.2
), if
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor M_{t}
then for
s>0
small,
\mu_{t+s}=\mathcal{H}^{n}\lfloor M_{t+s}^{\prime}
for some smooth mean curvature flow
M_{t+s}^{\prime}
with bounded curvature. Then,
M_{t+s}^{\prime}=M_{t+s}
by uniqueness of smooth mean curvature flows with bounded mean curvature
[
CY07
]
. Alternatively, one could argue that
M_{t+s}^{\prime}
is a small graph (with zero initial conditions at
s=0
) over
M_{t+s}
and apply the Ecker–Huisken maximum principle.
∎

Appendix F
The topology of regions in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

We collect some useful results concerning the topology of regions in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Recall the following definitions.

Definition F.1
.

If
M
is an oriented compact connected surface without boundary, decomposing
M=\cup_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}
into connected components, then we set

\genus(M):=\sum_{\alpha}\genus(M_{\alpha})=\tfrac{1}{2}b_{1}(M).

Definition F.2
.

If
M^{2}
is an oriented compact surface with boundary
\partial M
, the genus of
M
is defined to be the genus of the oriented surface
\tilde{M}
formed by capping off each circle in
\partial M
by a disk.

Lemma F.3
.

If
M^{2}
is an oriented compact surface with boundary so that there are two curves in
M^{2}
with non-zero algebraic intersection, then
\genus(M)>0
.

Proof.

We can assume that
M
is connected. The capped off surface
\tilde{M}
has two curves with non-zero algebraic intersection. These curves cannot be nullhomotopic in
\tilde{M}
(since algebraic intersection is unchanged by homotopy), so
\tilde{M}
is not a sphere.
∎

The following result is well-known, but we give the proof for completeness.

Lemma F.4
.

Suppose that a closed set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a
3
-manifold with compact boundary
\partial\Omega
. Set
g=\genus(\partial\Omega)
. Then
H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}^{g}
.

Proof.

By compactifying, it suffices to prove that for
\Omega\subset S^{3}
a compact
3
-submanifold with boundary
\partial\Omega
of genus
g
, it holds that
H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}^{g}
(adding the point at infinity to
\Omega
will not change
H_{1}
). Write
\Omega^{\prime}=S^{3}\setminus\Omega^{\circ}
.

Alexander duality (cf.
[
Hat02
, Theorem 3.44]
) yields

H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{1}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})=H^{1}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}^{2g}.

In particular,
H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Z}),H_{1}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})
are free and finitely generated with

\rank H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Z})+\rank H_{1}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})=2g.

Thus, using the universal coefficients theorem for homology
[
Hat02
, Theorem 3A.3]
, it suffices to prove that

(F.1)

\dim H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Q}),\dim H_{1}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})\geq\genus M,

We prove (
F.1
) for
\Omega
since the argument for
\Omega^{\prime}
will be identical.

We will write
i:\partial\Omega\to\Omega
for the inclusion map. We now follow
[
Hat07
, Lemma 3.5]
closely. Poincaré duality and the long exact sequence for the pair
(\Omega,\partial\Omega)
yields the commutative diagram

\textstyle{H_{2}(\Omega,\partial\Omega;\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{\approx}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\textstyle{\approx}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\approx}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\Omega,\partial\Omega;\mathbb{Q}).}

The map
i^{*}
is the dual map to
i_{*}
(since we are working over a field). Thus,
\dim\ker i_{*}=\dim\coker i^{*}
. Combined with exactness of the rows in the above diagram, we find

\dim\image\partial=\dim\ker i_{*}=\dim\coker i^{*}=\dim\coker\partial

Thus,

\dim\ker i_{*}=\dim\image\partial=\tfrac{1}{2}\dim H_{1}(\partial\Omega,\mathbb{Q})=g.

Thus,

\dim H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Q})\geq\dim\image i_{*}=\dim H_{1}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb{Q})-\dim\image i_{*}=g.

This establishes (
F.1
) and thus completes the proof.
∎

The next lemma is a refinement of
[
CCMS20
, Lemma 11.7]
(using Lemma
F.4
in place of Alexander duality).

Lemma F.5
.

Suppose that a closed set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a
3
-manifold with compact boundary
\partial\Omega
. Set
g=\genus(\partial\Omega)
. Assume that
\partial\Omega
is transverse to a sphere
\partial B\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. There are loops
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{g}
so that
\{[\gamma_{1}],\dots,[\gamma_{g}]\}\subset H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Z})
is linearly independent and for
i=1,\dots,g
, either

•

\gamma_{i}
is contained in
B
or in
\overline{B}^{c}
, or

•

there is a component
\mathcal{U}_{i}
of
\partial B\cap\Omega
that has non-zero mod-2 intersection with
\gamma_{i}
and zero mod-2 intersection with each previous
\gamma_{j}
,
j<i
.

Moreover, we can arrange that exactly
2\genus(S\cap B)
of the
\gamma_{i}
are contained entirely in
B
and if, in
H_{1}(\Omega\cap\overline{B};\mathbb{Z})
,

\sum_{\{i:\gamma_{i}\subset B\}}n_{i}[\gamma_{i}]=[\beta]

for some cycle
\beta\subset\Omega\cap\partial B
, then all of the coefficients
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
vanish.

Proof.

The proof is essentially identical to
[
CCMS20
, Lemma 11.7]
(with Lemma
F.4
in place of Alexander duality). For the reader’s convenience we give the proof below.

We set
S=\partial\Omega
. We induct on the number of components
b\geq 0
of
S\cap\partial B
.

We begin with the base case
b=0
. Here, the claim follows from Lemma
F.4
. (To ensure that the loops constructed are contained either in
B
or
\overline{B}^{c}
, we can apply Lemma
F.4
to
\Omega\cap\overline{B}
and
\Omega\setminus B
separately.)

For the inductive step, if we consider the
b
components of
S\cap\partial B
, there is an inner most component (non-unique)
\alpha
of
S\cap\partial B
with a disk
D\subset\partial B
with
\partial D=\alpha
and
S\cap D^{\circ}=\emptyset
. We form a new surface
S^{\prime}
by removing an annulus
A=U_{\varepsilon/10}(\alpha)\subset S
and then gluing in two disks that are slight deformations of
D
in/out of
B
to cap off the boundary components
S\setminus A
. We can arrange that this occurs in
U_{\varepsilon}(D)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\varepsilon>0
small enough so that
U_{\varepsilon}(D)
is contractible. Now, we can form
\Omega^{\prime}
with
\partial\Omega^{\prime}=S^{\prime}
and so that
\Omega^{\prime}
agrees with
\Omega
outside of
U_{\varepsilon}(D)
.

Note that
S^{\prime}
has
b-1
components in
S^{\prime}\cap\partial B
, so we will be able to use the inductive hypothesis. We also note that
\genus(S^{\prime}\cap B)=\genus(S\cap B)
.

We consider two cases depending on whether or not
\alpha
separates the component of
S
that contains it or not.

Separating case.
Suppose
\alpha
is separating. In this case, it is easy to see that
\genus(S^{\prime})=\genus(S)=g
. The inductive hypothesis yields a set of loops
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{g}
satisfying the claim with
\Omega^{\prime}
replacing
\Omega
. As long as
\varepsilon>0
was chosen small enough above, we can assume that the
\gamma_{j}
are contained in
\Omega
. We claim that these curves satisfy the assertion of the lemma (for
\Omega
). The two bullet points follow immediately from the construction.

Write
I_{B}
for the set of indices with
\gamma_{i}\subset B
and similarly for
I_{B^{c}}
. Suppose that there are integers
n_{i}
so that

\sum_{i\in I_{B}}n_{i}[\gamma_{i}]=[\beta]

in
H_{1}(\Omega\cap\overline{B};\mathbb{Z})
for some
1
-cycle
\beta\subset\Omega\cap\partial B
. Write
\beta=\beta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime}
where
\beta^{\prime\prime}
are the components of
\beta
intersecting
D
. Note that in fact
\beta^{\prime\prime}\subset D
and thus
\beta^{\prime\prime}
bounds in
D
. As such, we can reduce to the case where there is a
2
-chain
\sigma\subset(\Omega\setminus U_{\varepsilon}(D))\cap\overline{B}
with

\partial\sigma=\beta^{\prime}-\sum_{i\in I_{B}}n_{i}\gamma_{i}.

(Indeed, by assumption there is such a
\sigma
in
\Omega\cap\overline{B}
. Since
\partial\sigma\cap U_{\varepsilon}(D)=\emptyset
, we can then push
\sigma
out of
U_{\varepsilon}(D)
.) Because
(\Omega\setminus U_{\varepsilon}(D))\cap\overline{B}\subset\Omega^{\prime}\cap\overline{B}
, all
n_{i}
vanish by the inductive hypothesis. This proves the final assertion in the claim.

Finally, it remains to check that
\{[\gamma_{1}],\dots,[\gamma_{g}]\}\subset H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Z})
is linearly independent. Suppose that

n_{1}[\gamma_{1}]+\dots+n_{g}[\gamma_{g}]=0\textrm{ in $H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Z})$}.

By construction (specifically the second bullet point) we can consider the intersection with the regions
\mathcal{U}_{i}
(proceeding from large to small indices) to see that
n_{i}=0
for any
\gamma_{i}
that intersects
\partial B
. Using Mayer–Vietoris (or transversality) we can find a
1
-cycle
\beta\subset\Omega\cap\partial B
with

\sum_{i\in I_{B}}n_{i}[\gamma_{i}]=[\beta]\textrm{ in $H_{1}(\Omega\cap\overline{B};\mathbb{Z})$ and }[\beta]=-\sum_{i\in I_{B^{c}}}n_{i}[\gamma_{i}]\textrm{ in $H_{1}(\Omega\cap B^{c};\mathbb{Z})$}.

As proven above, this yields
n_{i}=0
for
i\in I_{B}
. As above, if we write
\beta=\beta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime}
for
\beta^{\prime\prime}
the components intersecting
D
(so
\beta^{\prime\prime}\subset D
) then we find that
[\beta^{\prime}]=0
in
H_{1}(\Omega^{\prime}\cap\overline{B};\mathbb{Z})
and

\sum_{i\in I_{B^{c}}}n_{i}[\gamma_{i}]=-[\beta^{\prime}]

in
H_{1}(\Omega^{\prime}\setminus B;\mathbb{Z})
. Thus,we find that
\sum_{i\in I_{B^{c}}}n_{i}[\gamma_{i}]=0
in
H_{1}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})
. Thus
n_{i}=0
for
i\in I_{B^{c}}
by the inductive hypothesis. This completes the proof of linear independence, and thus finishes the consideration of the separating case.

Nonseparating case
. If
\alpha
does not separate the component of
S
that contains it, we find that
\genus(S^{\prime})=g-1
. We can use the inductive hypothesis to find
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{g-1}\subset\Omega\cap\Omega^{\prime}
satisfying the assertion for
\Omega^{\prime}
. We now define
\gamma_{g}
depending on whether or not
D\subset\Omega
. If
D\subset\Omega
we can choose a loop in
S
intersecting
\alpha
precisely once and then push this loop into
\Omega
to find
\gamma_{g}
a loop in
\Omega
intersecting
D
exactly once. If
D\cap\Omega=\emptyset
then we define
\gamma_{g}
to be
\alpha
pushed into
\Omega
and out of
B
. The various assertions are checked exactly as above. This completes the proof.
∎

The next lemma is an analogue of
[
CCMS20
, Lemma 11.7]
in this setting.

Lemma F.6
.

Suppose that a closed set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a
3
-manifold with compact boundary
\partial\Omega
. Set
g=\genus(\partial\Omega)
. Assume that
\partial\Omega
is transverse to a sphere
\partial B\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Assume that we have
\{[\gamma_{1}],\dots,[\gamma_{g}]\}\subset H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Z})\approx\mathbb{Z}^{g}
linearly independent and where each
\gamma_{i}
satisfies one of the following conditions:

•

\gamma_{i}
is contained in
B
or
\overline{B}^{c}
, or

•

there is a component
\mathcal{U}_{i}
of
\partial B\setminus S
that has non-zero mod-2 intersection with
\gamma_{i}
and zero mod-2 intersection which each previous
\gamma_{j}
,
j<i
.

Then, if
\genus(\partial\Omega\cap B)>0
, at least one of the
\gamma_{i}
is contained in
B
.

Proof.

Assume this does not hold. Lemma
F.5
and the assumption that
\genus(\partial\Omega\cap B)>0
yields
\eta\subset\Omega\cap B
so that
m\eta
is not homologous in
\Omega
to a cycle in
\partial B\cap\Omega
for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
. We claim that

\{[\gamma_{1}],\dots,[\gamma_{g}],[\eta]\}\subset H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Z})\approx\mathbb{Z}^{g}

is linearly independent, a contradiction.

If it were linearly dependent, we could write

m[\eta]=\sum_{i=1}^{g}n_{i}[\gamma_{i}]\textrm{ in $H_{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{Z})$}.

The second bullet point implies that
n_{i}=0
if
\gamma_{i}
is not contained entirely in
B^{c}
. Mayer–Vietoris then implies that
m\eta
is homologous to a cycle in
\partial B\cap\Omega
, so
m=0
. This yields a linear dependency between the
[\gamma_{i}]
’s, a contradiction. This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix G
Localized topological monotonicity

We recall here some results from
[
CCMS20
, Appendix G]
. We begin with the following result.

Definition G.1

(Simple flow
[
CCMS20
, Appendix G]
)
.

For
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
an open set with smooth boundary, a
simple flow
in
U\times I
is a closed subset of space-time
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}
so that there is a compact
2
-manifold with boundary and a continuous map
f:M\times I\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
so that

(1)

\mathcal{M}(t)\cap\overline{U}=f(M\times\{t\})
(where
\mathcal{M}(t)=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:(\mathbf{x},t)\in\mathcal{M}\}
)

(2)

f
is smooth on
M^{\circ}\times I
where
M^{\circ}=M\setminus\partial M

(3)

f(\cdot,t)
,
t\in I
is an embedding of
M^{\circ}
into
U

(4)

t\mapsto f(M^{\circ}\times\{t\})
is a smooth mean curvature flow

(5)

f|_{\partial M\times I}
is a smooth family of embeddings of
\partial M
into
\partial U
.

We now recall some definitions from
[
Whi95
]
and
[
CCMS20
, Appendix G]
(since we will only use these results in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
we do not state them in general dimensions). For
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,T]
we set

W[t]:=\mathcal{M}^{c}\cap\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(\{t\}),\qquad W[0,T]:=\mathcal{M}^{c}\cap\mathfrak{t}^{-1}([0,T]).

Similarly, for
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
an open set with smooth boundary, we set

W_{\Omega}[t]:=\mathcal{M}^{c}\cap\Omega\cap\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(\{t\}),\qquad W[0,T]:=\mathcal{M}^{c}\cap(\Omega\times[0,T])\cap\mathfrak{t}^{-1}([0,T]).

The following result proven
[
CCMS20
, Appendix G]
localizes the corresponding result in
[
Whi95
]
.

Theorem G.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a level set flow and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be an open set with smooth boundary. Assume that
\mathcal{M}
is a simple flow in
U\times[0,T]
for some tubular neighborhood
U
of
\partial\Omega
. Then any loop in
W_{\Omega}[0,T]
is homotopic to one in
W_{\Omega}[0]
. In particular

H_{1}(W_{\Omega}[0];\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(W_{\Omega}[0,T];\mathbb{Z})

is surjective.

Appendix H
Some remarks concerning tangent flows in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

Huisken’s monotonicity and compactness of Brakke flows implies that we can extract tangent flows to a given Brakke flow around every point in space-time. It is a well-known problem to show that such tangent flows are unique, i.e., independent of the chosen sequence of dilations. (Several uniqueness results are known
[
Sch14
,
CIM15
,
CM15
,
CS21
,
Zhu23
,
Zhu21
]
when the tangent flow is associated to a multiplicity one smooth shrinker.)

In this appendix we observe that certain soft arguments (along with existing results on the structure and compactness of shrinkers) can be used to prove that if a tangent flow with certain properties exists, then any other tangent flow must have these properties (even if uniqueness is not clear). The arguments used here are inspired by work of Bernstein–Wang
[
BW15
]
who proved that if one tangent flow is a shrinking sphere/cylinder with multiplicity, then all tangent flows are (but the cylinder could
a priori
rotate between distinct tangent flows).

H.1.
Smooth multiplicity-one tangent flows

The main result here is Proposition
H.4
which roughly says that in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
a Brakke flow has one tangent flow supported on a smooth shrinker with unit multiplicity then all tangent flows are of this form. Using the idea of
[
BW15
]
we will see that the key to proving this is the following lemma.

Lemma H.1
.

Suppose that
V_{i}
are
F
-stationary cyclic integral varifolds in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
V_{i}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\Sigma
for
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
. Then for
i
sufficiently large,
V_{i}=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\Sigma_{i}
for some
\Sigma_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}

Proof.

Assume for contradiction that there is
p_{i}\in\sing V_{i}
. Allard’s theorem guarantees that
|\mathbf{p}_{i}|\to\infty
. Because
V_{i}
is cyclic and integral,
\Theta_{V_{i}}(\mathbf{p}_{i})\geq 2
. Thus, there is
0<r_{i}=o(1)
so that

\int\rho_{(p_{i},-1+r_{i}^{2})}(\mathbf{x},-1)d\mu_{V_{i}}\geq 2-o(1)

as
i\to\infty
(where
\rho
is the Gaussian density defined in (
2.1
)).

Let
\mathcal{M}_{i}
be the Brakke flow (for
t<0
) associated to
V_{i}
(and similarly
\mathcal{M}
the flow associated to
\Sigma
). Note that
\mathcal{M}_{i}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{M}
. Define
\tilde{t}_{i}:=-|\mathbf{p}_{i}|^{-2}=o(1)
,
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}_{i}:=|\mathbf{p}_{i}|^{-1}\mathbf{p}_{i}
and
\tilde{r}_{i}:=|\mathbf{p}_{i}|^{-1}r_{i}
. Set
\tilde{X}_{i}:=(\tilde{\mathbf{p}}_{i},\tilde{t}_{i}+\tilde{r}_{i}^{2})
. Observe that

\Theta_{\mathcal{M}_{i}}(\tilde{X}_{i},\tilde{r}_{i})=\int\rho_{(\mathbf{p}_{i},-1+r_{i}^{2})}(\mathbf{x},-1)d\mu_{V_{i}}\geq 2-o(1).

Thus, Huisken’s monotonicity formula yields
\Theta_{\mathcal{M}_{i}}(\tilde{X}_{i},\tilde{r}_{i}+r)\geq 2-o(1)
as
i\to\infty
, for any
r>0
fixed. We can pass to a subsequence and assume that
\tilde{X}_{i}\to\tilde{X}=(\tilde{\mathbf{p}},0)
, for
|\tilde{\mathbf{p}}|=1
. By the convergence
\mathcal{M}_{i}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{M}
we thus have

\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(\tilde{X},r)\geq 2
for all
r>0
, so in particular

\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(\tilde{X})\geq 2.

On the other hand, by
[
Wan16a
]
(cf. Proposition
3.5
),
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
, implying that
\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(\tilde{X})\in\{1,\lambda_{1}\}
. This is a contradiction, completing the proof.
∎

We recall the distance on Radon measures
d_{V}
defined in e.g.
[
CIM15
, (2.11)]
,

d_{V}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\sum_{k}2^{-k}\left|\int f_{k}e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}d\mu_{1}-\int f_{k}e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}d\mu_{2}\right|

where
\{f_{n}\}
is a fixed countable dense subset of the unit ball in
C^{0}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Recall that
d_{V}
metrizes the weak* topology on the set of Radon measures
\mu
with
\int e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}d\mu<\infty
.

Write
\mathfrak{F}
for the set of cyclic integral
F
-stationary varifolds in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. By an abuse of notation we will consider
\mathcal{S}_{2}\subset\mathfrak{F}
by identifying
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
with
\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\Sigma
. We can rewrite the previous lemma as follows:

Corollary H.2
.

There is
\varepsilon_{0}>0
so that if
V\in\mathfrak{F}
has
d_{V}(V,\mathcal{S}_{2})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
then
V\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
.

On the other hand, we recall the following standard result.

Lemma H.3
.

Consider
\mathcal{N}
a rescaled Brakke flow in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with entropy
\lambda(\mathcal{N})<\infty
. Then
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}F(\mathcal{N}(\tau)):=\theta_{0}
exists and
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}d_{V}(\mathcal{N}(\tau),\mathfrak{F})=0
. Furthermore, for
s_{0}>0
fixed,

\limsup_{\tau\to\infty}\sup_{|\tau^{\prime}-\tau|\leq s_{0}}d_{V}(\mathcal{N}(\tau),\mathcal{N}(\tau^{\prime}))=0.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Huisken’s monotonicity formula for Brakke flows (in the rescaled setting) cf.
[
Ilm95
]
.
∎

Proposition H.4
.

Consider
\mathcal{M}
a cyclic integral unit-regular Brakke flow in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Suppose that some tangent flow to
\mathcal{M}
at
X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}
satisfies
t\mapsto\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma
for
t<0
, where
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
is a smooth self-shrinker. Then, any tangent flow to
\mathcal{M}
at
X
is of the form
t\mapsto\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma^{\prime}
for
t<0
, for some
\Sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}
denote the rescaled Brakke flow obtained from
\mathcal{M}
with respect to the point
X
. By assumption there is
\tau_{i}\to\infty
so that
\mathcal{N}(\tau_{i})\rightharpoonup\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\Sigma
as
i\to\infty
. For
\varepsilon_{0}>0
defined in Corollary
H.2
we consider

\tau_{i}^{\prime}:=\sup\{\tau^{\prime}\geq\tau_{i}:d_{V}(\mathcal{N}(\tau^{\prime}),\mathcal{S}_{2})\leq\varepsilon_{0}/2\textrm{ for all }\tau\in[\tau_{i},\tau^{\prime}]\}.

We consider two cases. First, we suppose that
\tau_{i}^{\prime}=\infty
for some
i
. Consider
s_{i}\to\infty
and pass to a subsequence (see Lemma
H.3
) so that
\mathcal{N}(s_{i})\rightharpoonup V\in\mathcal{F}
. By assumption, we have
d_{V}(V,\mathcal{S}_{2})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
so
V\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
by Corollary
H.2
. Thus, the proof is completed in this case.

It thus suffices to rule out the possibility that
\tau_{i}^{\prime}<\infty
for all
i\to\infty
. First, note that
\tau_{i}^{\prime}-\tau_{i}\to\infty
. Indeed, Lemma
H.3
implies that for
s_{0}>0
fixed

\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{i},\tau_{i}+s_{0}]}d_{V}(\mathcal{N}(\tau^{\prime}),\mathcal{S}_{2})\leq d_{V}(\mathcal{N}({\tau_{i}}),\mathcal{S}_{2})+o(1)=o(1)

as
i\to\infty
. Now, we have

\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{i}^{\prime}-1,{\tau_{i}^{\prime}}+1]}d_{V}(\mathcal{N}(\tau^{\prime}),\mathcal{S}_{2})\leq o(1)+d_{V}(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{i}^{\prime}-1),\mathcal{S}_{2})

Note that Lemma
H.3
implies that after passing to a subsequence,
\mathcal{N}(\tau_{i}^{\prime}-1)
converges to an element
V
of
\mathcal{F}
. This element satisfies
d_{V}(V,\mathcal{S}_{2})\leq\varepsilon_{0}/2
. Thus by Corollary
H.2
, we have
V\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
. In particular, this yields
d_{V}(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{i}^{\prime}-1),\mathcal{S}_{2})=o(1)
. Thus

\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{i}^{\prime}-1,{\tau_{i}^{\prime}}+1]}d_{V}(\mathcal{N}(\tau^{\prime}),\mathcal{S}_{2})=o(1).

This contradicts the definition of
\tau_{i}^{\prime}
, completing the proof. ∎

H.2.
Genus and multiplicity at the first non-generic time

The results of this subsection are not explicitly used in this paper, but we include it because it may be of some interest elsewhere. Consider
\mathcal{M}
an integral unit-regular Brakke flow with
\mathcal{M}(0)=\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor M
for
M
a closed embedded surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We recall here (part of) Definition
9.1
.

Definition H.5

(Generic singularities and first non-generic time)
.

•

We define
\sing_{\textnormal{gen}}\mathcal{M}\subset\sing\mathcal{M}
to be the set of singular points for
\mathcal{M}
so that one (and thus all
[
CM15
]
) tangent flow is a multiplicity-one shrinking sphere or cylinder.

•

Then, we define
T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}):=\inf\mathfrak{t}(\sing\mathcal{M}\setminus\sing_{\textrm{gen}}\mathcal{M})
, i.e., the first time that there is a “non-generic” singularity.

Recall that any tangent flow to
\mathcal{M}
at
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))\in\sing\mathcal{M}
is of the form

t\mapsto k\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\sqrt{-t}\Sigma

for some smooth properly embedded self-shrinker
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
with
\genus(\Sigma)\leq\genus(M):=g_{0}
. Write
\mathcal{S}_{2}(g)\subset\mathcal{S}_{2}
for the set of genus
g
shrinkers. Continuing with the notation from the previous section, we will write
k\mathcal{S}_{2}(g):=\{k\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\Sigma:\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}(g)\}\subset\mathfrak{F}
for the multiplicity
k
shrinkers of genus
g
.

Lemma H.6
.

The set
k\mathcal{S}_{2}(g)
is closed with respect to
d_{V}
.

Proof.

Consider
\mu\in\mathfrak{F}
and
\mu_{j}=k\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\Sigma_{j}\in k\mathcal{S}_{2}(g)
with
d_{V}(\mu_{j},\mu)\to 0
. By
[
CM12b
,
SW20b
]
(cf.
[
BW15
, Proposition 3.4]
) we can pass to a subsequence so that
\Sigma_{j}
converges in
C^{\infty}_{\textrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
to
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}(g)
with multiplicity one. Thus, the weak* limit of
\mu_{j}=k\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\Sigma_{j}
is
k\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\Sigma=\mu
. This completes the proof.
∎

Let
\mathcal{N}
denote the rescaled Brakke flow obtained by rescaling
\mathcal{M}
around
X=(\mathbf{x},T_{\textrm{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))
. We record the following addendum to Lemma
H.3
. Set
\mathfrak{F}^{k_{0},g_{0}}:=\cup_{k=1}^{k_{0}}\cup_{g=0}^{g_{0}}k\mathcal{S}_{2}
.

Lemma H.7
.

There is
k_{0}\geq 0
so that
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}d_{V}(\mathcal{N}(\tau),\mathfrak{F}^{k_{0},g_{0}})=0
.

Proof.

Since
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
has
F(\Sigma)\geq 1
(by Brakke’s theorem, cf.
[
Whi05
]
) we can choose
k_{0}>\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(X)
and then apply
[
Whi95
,
Ilm95
]
(cf. Proposition
9.5
).
∎

Proposition H.8
.

Suppose that one tangent flow to
\mathcal{M}
at
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))
is associated to a genus
g
shrinker with multiplicity
k
. Then, any other tangent flow to
\mathcal{M}
at
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))
is associated to a (possibly different) genus
g
shrinker with multiplicity
k
.

Proof.

We follow a similar strategy to the proof of Proposition
H.4
. Write
\mathcal{N}
for the rescaled Brakke flow associated to
\mathcal{M}
at
(\mathbf{x},T_{\textnormal{gen}}(\mathcal{M}))
. By assumption there is
\tau_{i}\to\infty
with
\mathcal{N}(\tau_{i})\rightharpoonup k\mathcal{H}^{2}\lfloor\Sigma\in k\mathcal{S}_{2}^{g}
. Using Lemma
H.6
we can fix
\varepsilon_{0}>0
so that if
V\in\mathfrak{F}^{k_{0},g_{0}}
has
d_{V}(V,k^{\prime}\mathcal{S}_{2}(g^{\prime}))\leq\varepsilon_{0}
for some
k^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,k_{0}\},g^{\prime}\in\{0,\dots,g_{0}\}
then
V\in k^{\prime}\mathcal{S}_{2}(g^{\prime})
. Then, we can define

\tau_{i}^{\prime}:=\sup\{\tau^{\prime}\geq\tau_{i}:d_{V}(\mathcal{N}(\tau^{\prime}),k\mathcal{S}_{2}^{g})\leq\varepsilon_{0}/2\textrm{ for all }\tau\in[\tau_{i},\tau^{\prime}]\}.

If
\tau_{i}^{\prime}=\infty
for some
i
, then for
s_{i}\to\infty
so that
\mathcal{N}(s_{i})\to V\in\mathfrak{F}^{k_{0},g_{0}}
we have
V\in k\mathcal{S}_{2}^{g}
by the choice of
\varepsilon_{0}
. Thus, it remains to rule out
\tau_{i}^{\prime}<\infty
for all
i
.

As in Proposition
H.4
, Lemmas
H.3
and
H.7
yield
\tau_{i}^{\prime}-\tau_{i}\to\infty
. Moreover, up to a subsequence
\mathcal{N}(\tau_{i}^{\prime}-1)
converges to
V\in\mathfrak{F}^{k_{0},g_{0}}
and by choice of
\tau_{i}^{\prime}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
, we thus see that
V\in k\mathcal{S}_{2}^{g}
. Lemma
H.3
then yields a contradiction, completing the proof.
∎
